Quantum Optimal Transport: Quantum Couplings and Many-Body Problems
François Golse

Abstract
. This text is a set of lecture notes for a 4.5-hour course given at the Erdős Center (Rényi Institute, Budapest) during the Summer School “Optimal Transport on Quantum Structures” (September 19th-23rd, 2023). Lecture I introduces the quantum analogue of the Wasserstein distance of exponent 2 2 defined in [F. Golse, C. Mouhot, T. Paul: Comm. Math. Phys. 343 (2016), 165–205], and in [F. Golse, T. Paul: Arch. Ration. Mech. Anal. 223 (2017) 57–94]. Lecture II discusses various applications of this quantum analogue of the Wasserstein distance of exponent 2 2 , while Lecture III discusses several of its most important properties, such as the triangle inequality, and the Kantorovich duality in the quantum setting, together with some of their implications.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
49Q22, 49N15, 81S30, 35Q41, 81V70, 82C10, 35Q55, 81Q20, 35Q83

Introduction

Optimal transport has become a thriving field of mathematical research in the last decade of the 20th century. Nowadays, it is used in a variety of subjects
which the founder of this theory (G. Monge, 1781) could obviously not have foreseen: statistics, machine learning, probability theory, fluid mechanics, besides
more classical applications such as the calculus of variations, partial differential equations, geometry. In his book
[
63
]
, C. Villani speaks of a
“revival” of optimal transport following Y. Brenier’s remarkable paper
[
15
]
.

Among the many applications of optimal transport is a quite fascinating observation by Dobrushin
[
30
]
, who realized that one special kind of approximation
used in classical nonequilibrium statistical mechanics, namely the time-dependent mean-field limit, can be proved rigorously by using a notion of distance on
the set of probability measures which is defined in terms of optimal transport.

The same mean-field limit is routinely used in quantum dynamics, but, until relatively recently, the methods of proof used in the rigorous justification of this
limit were radically different in the quantum and in the classical settings. This state of affairs was slightly disturbing, since one would expect that it should be
possible to take the classical limit of quantum mechanics and the mean-field limit of both classical and quantum dynamics in any order — in other words,
one expects that both limits should be represented by a commutative diagram.

There have been some attempts in that direction at the beginning of the 2000s
[
43
,
51
]
, and, slightly later, this suggested the natural
idea of “lifting” Dobrushin’s approach
[
30
]
to the quantum setting. Since Dobrushin used an optimal transport metric to compare
N
-particle densities
and their mean-field limits, it became a very natural motivation for defining an analogous optimal transport “metric” for the purpose of comparing quantum states.
Ideally, this “metric” should converge to the metric used by Dobrushin in the classical limit.

I started working on this problem with C. Mouhot and T. Paul, after a visit to C. Mouhot in Cambridge in September 2014. At this point, we realized that mean-field
quantum dynamics satisfies an analogous continuous dependence on the initial data defined in terms of a quantum optimal transport problem as in Dobrushin’s
analysis. Our initial contribution
[
37
]
was followed by a series of works involving other collaborations
[
38
,
19
,
20
,
36
,
39
,
40
,
41
]
, and exploring both our quantum analogue of optimal transport and its applications to
various problems in quantum dynamics. It is precisely this approach to quantum optimal transport which is presented in these lectures.

But there are other, possibly (most likely?) unrelated approaches to quantum optimal transport.

One such approach, due to E. Carlen and J. Maas is based on the Benamou-Brenier formula (Theorem 8.1 in
[
63
]
): see
[
22
]
. Another
approach, based on the notion of quantum channel and closer in spirit to the one presented in this course, yet different, is due to G. De Palma and D. Trevisan

[
26
]
— see also Dario Trevisan’s course
[
62
]
in this volume. Some of these different approaches to a theory of quantum optimal transport
(see in particular Eric Carlen’s lectures
[
21
]
) will be presented in this school. Still another approach to the problem of quantum optimal transport is the very
early reference
[
65
]
(see also the beautiful book
[
11
]
by I. Bengtsson and K. Życzkowski, and especially section 7.7 there).

Since this text is a set of lecture notes, several quizzes/exercises are proposed to the reader. Some of these exercises review classical material which the reader
is expected to master before going further; some others discuss natural extensions of the material presented in this course. In any case, solving these exercises
is strongly recommended in order to gain familiarity with the notions presented in these notes.

I am indebted to several colleagues for the mathematics discussed in these lectures, in the first place to C. Mouhot, T. Paul, E. Caglioti, and S. Jin, with whom
I had the pleasure to work on various problems related to quantum optimal transport. The observation inequality for the Schrödinger equation discussed in
Lecture II comes from questions posed by C. Bardos. I owe my first acquaintance with Dobrushin’s remarkable paper
[
30
]
to M. Pulvirenti, who gave
a most lucid account of it in a lecture at École normale supérieure in 1997. I also benefited from numerous discussions on optimal transport with Y. Brenier.
Most of these lectures are based on the numerous analogies between classical and quantum optimal transport. However, some very fundamental properties
of classical optimal transport may fail to have quantum analogues. I am very grateful to D. Serre, who kindly showed me an example where the quantum and
the classical theory significantly differ (see Quiz 31 in Lecture III).

Finally, I wish to express my gratitude to the organizers of this 2022 Summer School on Optimal Transport on Quantum Structures at the Erdős Center,
J. Maas, S. Rademacher, T. Titkos and D. Virosztek for their kind invitation, and more generally to the Rényi Institute for its most enjoyable hospitality.

Table of Contents

Lecture I:
Extending the Wasserstein distance of exponent
2
to density operators.

Lecture II:
Applying the quantum Wasserstein pseudometric to particle dynamics.

Lecture III:
Triangle inequalities and optimal transport in the quantum setting.

1.
Lecture I: Extending the Wasserstein Distance of Exponent
2

to Density Operators

Our general purpose is to extend optimal transport (Wasserstein) distances, defined on Borel probability measures on phase space, i.e.
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, to their quantum
analogue, i.e. to density operators on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

In this first lecture, we

\bullet
recall some fundamental results on classical optimal transport (section
1.1
),

\bullet
recall some material on trace-class and Hilbert-Schmidt operators (section
1.2
),

\bullet
introduce one first noncommutative extension of optimal transport (section
1.3
),

\bullet
present our quantum extension of the Wasserstein metric
\mathcal{W}_{2}
(section
1.4
), and

\bullet
discuss some basic estimates and examples of computations (section
1.5
).

1.1.
A Crash-Course on Classical Optimal Transport

Before embarking on a description of a quantum analogue of the Wasserstein distance of exponent
2
for density operators, we need to recall some fundamental notions
and results pertaining to the the classical theory of optimal transport. There are many excellent reference textbooks on optimal transport, such as
[
63
,
4
,
64
,
58
,
33
]
, where the interested reader will find the proofs of all the statements in this section — together with many fascinating applications
of optimal transport in various areas of mathematics. Of course, Alessio Figalli’s course
[
32
]
in this school is strongly recommended as a general introduction to optimal
transport.

1.1.1.
The Monge and the Kantorovich Problems

Optimal transport grew from Monge’s celebrated ‘‘Mémoire
1
1

1

“Déblai” and “remblai” are technical terms for earthwork in French. “Déblai” means excavation,
whereas “remblai” is the French word for embankment or backfill. A first version of Monge’s memoir was read on February 7th 1776 at the Académie des sciences, followed by
a second version, read on March 27th 1781, and finally published in 1784.
sur la théorie des déblais et des remblais”.

In modern mathematical terminology, Monge’s problem can be stated as follows.

Monge’s problem.
For all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, find
T:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}^{n}
measurable such that
T\#\mu=\nu
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|T(x)-x|\mu(dx)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|F(x)-x|\mu(dx)\text{ with }F:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}^{n}\text{ measurable s.t. }F\#\mu=\nu\right\}

\displaystyle,

where
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
designates the set of all Borel probability measures on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
, while

\mathcal{P}_{k}(\mathbf{R}^{n}):=\left\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{n})\text{ s.t. }\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|x|^{k}\mu(dx)<\infty\right\}\,,

and
T\#\mu
is the push-forward of the measure
\mu
by the transformation
T
, defined by the formula

T\#\mu(B):=\mu(T^{-1}B)\,,\quad\text{ for all Borel }B\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}\,.

With such a general formulation, Monge’s problem does not always have at least one solution. (For example, set
n=1
, choose
\mu:={\delta}_{0}
and
\nu:=\tfrac{1}{2}({\delta}_{+1}+{\delta}_{-1})
.
Then there does not exist any map
T:\,\mathbf{R}\to\mathbf{R}
such that
T\#\mu=\nu
.)

However, if
\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbf{R}^{n}
, Monge’s problem always has at least one solution. This was proved more than 200 years
after Monge’s first version of his memoir, in 1979, by Sudakov
[
61
]
(see also section 6 of
[
3
]
, where a gap in Sudakov’s original argument is fixed).

Before Sudakov’s proof, Kantorovich proposed in
[
47
]
a relaxed version of Monge’s problem, for which the existence of a solution is elementary.

The Kantorovich relaxation of Monge’s problem.
For all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, find

\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu):=\min_{\rho\in\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)}\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{n}\times\mathbf{R}^{n}}|x-y|\rho(dxdy)\,,

where
\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)
is the set of “couplings”, or “transport plans” between
\mu
and
\nu
, defined as follows:

\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu):=\left\{\rho\!\in\!\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{2n})\left|\begin{aligned} \rho(A\!\times\!\mathbf{R}^{n})\!=\!\mu(A),\\
\rho(\mathbf{R}^{n}\!\times\!A)\!=\!\nu(A),\end{aligned}\right.\quad\text{ for all Borel }A\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}\right\}\,.

Observe that
\mu\otimes\nu\in\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)
, so that
\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)\not=\varnothing
.

That Kantorovich’s problem is indeed a relaxation of Monge’s problem is explained by the following elementary observation.

Remark.
If
T:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}^{n}
is a measurable map such that
T\#\mu=\nu
, the probability measure
\rho(dxdy)=\mu(dx){\delta}_{T(x)}(dy)
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)
(this
is trivial). Moreover, for all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, one has

\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|F(x)-x|\mu(dx)\text{ with }F:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}^{n}\text{ measurable s.t. }F\#\mu=\nu\right\}

provided that
\mu
has no atom (this is not obvious: see Theorem 2.1 in
[
3
]
).

Quiz 1.
Prove the existence of a solution to the Kantorovich problem. (Hint: if
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, then
\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)\subset\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n}\times\mathbf{R}^{n})
, and

\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)
is weakly relatively compact in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{n}\times\mathbf{R}^{n})
by Prokhorov’s Theorem.)

The main result in
[
47
]
is the following equivalent variational formula for
\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)
, which can be deduced from the definition above by convex duality,
applying the Fenchel-Moreau-Rockafellar duality theorem (see Theorem 1.12 in
[
17
]
).

Kantorovich(-Rubinstein) duality.
For all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
,

\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)=\sup_{\chi\in\operatorname{Lip}(\mathbf{R}^{n},\mathbf{R})\atop\operatorname{Lip}(\chi)\leq 1}\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\chi(z)\mu(dz)-\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\chi(z)\nu(dz)\right|\,.

For a proof of Kantorovich duality, see chapter 1 of
[
63
]
.

The Kantorovich duality formula for
\mathcal{W}_{1}
has several important applications to the topology of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, listed below.

Consequences of the Kantorovich(-Rubinstein) duality.

(1) The functional
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is a metric on
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
.

(2) Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
and
\mu_{j}
be a sequence of elements of
\mathcal{P}_{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
. Then the three conditions below are equivalent

(a)
\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu_{j},\mu)\to 0
as
j\to\infty
,

(b)
\mu_{j}\to\mu
weakly in the sense of probability measures as
j\to\infty
and

\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{j\geq 1}\int_{|x|>R}|x|\mu_{j}(dx)=0\,,

(c)
\mu_{j}\to\mu
weakly in the sense of probability measures as
j\to\infty
and

\lim_{j\to\infty}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|x|\mu_{j}(dx)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|x|\mu(dx)\,.

Statement (2) is Theorem 7.12 in
[
63
]
. As for (1), the Kantorovich(-Rubinstein) duality formula obviously implies that
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is nonnegative, symmetric in both
its arguments, and satisfies the triangle inequality. Finally, if
\mathcal{W}_{1}(\mu,\nu)=0
, then

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\psi(z)\mu(dz)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\psi(z)\nu(dz)

for each
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, since
\phi:=\psi/(1+\|\operatorname{grad}\psi\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{n})})
satisfies
\operatorname{Lip}(\phi)\leq 1
. Hence
\mu=\nu
(viewing
\mu
and
\nu
as distributions
of order
0
on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
).

The functional
\mathcal{W}_{1}
is usually referred to as the Wasserstein distance of exponent
1
, and sometimes as the Monge-Kantorovich or the Kantorovich-Rubinstein distance.

1.1.2.
The Wasserstein Distance of Exponent
2

In Monge’s own words “Le prix du transport d’une molécule [est], toute choses égales d’ailleurs, proportionnel à son poids & à l’espace qu’on lui fait parcourir” (all else
being equal, the cost of transport for one molecule is proportional to its weight and to the distance over which it is transported).

But one could assume instead that the cost of transport is proportional to some power of the distance. In this section, we consider the special case where the cost of transport
is proportional to the
square
distance. In that case, the Monge and the Kantorovich problems are as follows.

Monge’s problem.
For all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, find
T:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}^{n}
measurable such that
T\#\mu=\nu
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|T(x)-x|^{2}\mu(dx)

\displaystyle=\inf\left\{\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|F(x)-x|^{2}\mu(dx)\text{ with }F:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}^{n}\text{ measurable s.t. }F\#\mu=\nu\right\}

\displaystyle.

The Kantorovich problem.
For all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, find

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu):=\left(\min_{\rho\in\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)}\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{n}\times\mathbf{R}^{n}}|x-y|^{2}\rho(dxdy)\right)^{1/2}\,.

The existence of a solution to the Kantorovich problem is proved by exactly the same weak compactness argument as in Quiz 1 above.

Remark.
For each
p\in(1,+\infty)
, there exists an analogue of the functional
\mathcal{W}_{2}
, denoted
\mathcal{W}_{p}
, in the case where the cost of transport is proportional to the

p
th power of the distance. We have chosen to restrict our attention to the cases
p=1
and
p=2
, for which “quantum” analogues have been defined.

The Kantorovich duality in that case is, at first sight, different from the case of exponent
1
.

Kantorovich duality for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
.
For all
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
,

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)^{2}=\sup_{a(x)+b(y)\leq|x-y|^{2}\atop a,b\in C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}a(z)\mu(dz)+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}b(z)\nu(dz)\,.

Moreover, there exist two l.s.c. proper convex functions
{\alpha},{\beta}
such that
{\alpha}\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n},\mu)
and
{\beta}\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n},\nu)
, satisfying
2
2

2

A convex function

{\alpha}:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
is said to be proper if there exists at least one point
x\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
such that
{\alpha}(x)<+\infty
. Its Legendre dual is

{\alpha}^{*}(p):=\sup_{x\in\mathbf{R}^{n}}(p\cdot x-{\alpha}(x))\,,\qquad p\in\mathbf{R}^{n}\,.

If
{\alpha}
is a l.s.c. proper convex function on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
, then the function
{\alpha}^{*}
is also l.s.c. proper
and convex on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
, and one has
{\alpha}^{**}={\alpha}
. See section 1.4 and Theorem 1.11 in
[
17
]
.

{\alpha}^{*}={\beta}
and
{\beta}^{*}={\alpha}
, and such that

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)^{2}=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}(|x|^{2}-2{\alpha}(x))\mu(dx)+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}(|y|^{2}-2{\beta}(y))\nu(dy)\,.

The first equality is Theorem 1.3 in
[
63
]
, while the existence of an optimal pair
{\alpha},{\beta}
is Theorem 2.9 in
[
63
]
.

Perhaps the most important consequence of the Kantorovich duality for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is the structure of optimal couplings for the Kantorovich problem.

Optimal couplings.

(a) (Knott-Smith Theorem.) Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
. A probability measure
\rho\in\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)
is an optimal coupling for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
if and only if there exists

\Phi:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
, l.s.c. proper and convex, such that

\operatorname{supp}(\rho)\subset\text{graph}({\partial}\Phi)\,,

where
{\partial}\Phi
is the subdifferential
3
3

3

If
\Phi:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
is a proper convex function, its subdifferential at
x\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
is the set

{\partial}\Phi(x):=\{\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{n}\text{ s.t. }\Phi(y)\geq\Phi(x)+\xi\cdot(y-x)\,,\quad y\in\mathbf{R}^{n}\}\,.

See Example 2.1.4 in
[
16
]
. The subdifferential of
a proper convex function is an example of monotone operator: if
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
and if
\xi_{1}\in{\partial}\Phi(x_{1})
and
\xi_{2}\in{\partial}\Phi(x_{2})
, then

(\xi_{2}-\xi_{1})\cdot(x_{2}-x_{1})\geq 0\,.

One can check that (1)
\Phi
is differentiable at
x\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
if and only if
{\partial}\Phi(x)
contains a single element,
which is
\operatorname{grad}\Phi(x)
, and (2) if a l.s.c. proper convex function
\Phi
on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
is strictly convex in a neighborhood of

x\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
, its Legendre dual
\Phi^{*}
is differentiable on
{\partial}\Phi(x)
, and
\operatorname{grad}\Phi^{*}(\xi)=x
for all
\xi\in{\partial}\Phi(x)
.
of
\Phi
.

(b) (Brenier’s Theorem.) Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
. If
\mu(S)=0
for each Borel set
S\subset\mathbf{R}^{n}
of Hausdorff dimension
\mathcal{H}-\text{dim}(S)\leq n-1
, there
exists a unique optimal coupling for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
, of the form

\rho(dxdy)=\mu(dx){\delta}_{\operatorname{grad}\Phi(x)}(dy)

with
\Phi:\,\mathbf{R}^{n}\to\mathbf{R}\cup\{+\infty\}
convex and such that
\Phi\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n},\mu)
.

See Theorem 2.12 in
[
63
]
, and more generally chapter 2 of
[
63
]
for a proof of the Knott-Smith and the Brenier theorems.

Since
\Phi\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n},\mu)
, one has
\mu(\Phi^{-1}(\{+\infty\}))=0
, on the other hand
\Phi
is locally Lipschitz continuous on the interior of its domain

\operatorname{Dom}(\Phi):=\mathbf{R}^{n}\setminus\Phi^{-1}(\{+\infty\})
. Then (1)
\operatorname{Dom}(\Phi)
is a convex subset of
\mathbf{R}^{n}
, and hence
{\partial}\operatorname{Dom}(\Phi)
has Hausdorff dimension

\leq n-1
, and (2) the set of points
x
in the interior of
\operatorname{Dom}(\Phi)
such that
\Phi
is not differentiable at
x
has Hausdorff dimension
\leq n-1
(see

[
2
]
). Since

(\operatorname{grad}\Phi)^{-1}(B):=\{x\in\mathbf{R}^{n}\text{ s.t. }\Phi\text{ is differentiable at }x\text{ and }\operatorname{grad}\Phi(x)\in B\}\,,

and since
\operatorname{grad}\Phi(x)
exists for
\mu
-a.e.
x\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
, the push-forward measure
(\operatorname{grad}\Phi)_{\#}\mu
is well-defined.

We shall conclude this section with a brief list of the most important topological properties of
\mathcal{W}_{2}
.

Properties of
\mathcal{W}_{2}
.

(1) The functional
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is a metric on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
.

In particular

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)=0\iff\mu=\nu\,;

the optimal coupling in that case is
\rho(dxdy)\!=\!\mu(dx){\delta}_{x}(dy)
, and the Brenier transport map is the identity, which is the gradient of the convex function

x\mapsto\tfrac{1}{2}|x|^{2}
.

(2) Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
and
\mu_{j}
be a sequence of elements of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
. Then the two conditions below are equivalent:

(a)
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu_{j},\mu)\to 0
as
j\to\infty
,

(b)
\mu_{j}\to\mu
weakly in the sense of probability measures, and

\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{j\geq 1}\int_{|x|>R}|x|^{2}\mu_{j}(dx)=0\,.

Statement (1) is Theorem 7.3 in
[
63
]
, while statement (2) is Theorem 7.12 in
[
63
]
. The proof of the triangle inequality is definitely nontrivial,
at variance with the case of
\mathcal{W}_{1}
, for which the triangle inequality follows from the expression of
\mathcal{W}_{1}
using the Kantorovich duality.

Here is a nontrivial example where
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)
can be computed explicitly. See
[
34
]
for a proof of the formula below.

Example.
Let
G_{1},G_{2}
be Gaussian laws on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
with means
m_{1},m_{2}
and covariance matrices
A_{1},A_{2}
. Then

\mathcal{W}_{2}(G_{1},G_{2})^{2}=|m_{1}-m_{2}|^{2}+\operatorname{trace}\left(A_{1}+A_{2}-2\left(\sqrt{A_{1}}A_{2}\sqrt{A_{1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

Quiz 2.
For all
m_{1},m_{2}\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
, compute
\mathcal{W}_{p}(\delta_{m_{1}},\delta_{m_{2}})
for
p=1
and
p=2
. Does there exist optimal transport map(s) in both cases?

In most reference textbooks on optimal transport, the proof of the triangle inequality for
\mathcal{W}_{p}
with
1<p<\infty
is based on a nontrivial construction referred to as “glueing”
couplings having a common marginal (Lemma 7.6 in
[
63
]
, or Lemma 5.3.2 in
[
4
]
). This procedure is itself based on the notion of “disintegration” of a
probability measure on a Cartesian product with respect to one of its marginals (obviously related to the notion of conditional probability): see Theorem 5.3.1 in
[
4
]
.
There is an alternative to the approach based on the disintegration theorem, which uses instead the Hahn-Banach theorem: see Exercise 7.9 in
[
63
]
.

In the case where there exist optimal transport maps (e.g. when the sets of Hausdorff codimension
\geq 1
are negligible for the probability measures considered in the triangle
inequality, according to the Brenier theorem), the proof of the triangle inequality is very simple: see Lemma 5.3 in
[
58
]
. In the setting considered here (where the
underlying metric space is
\mathbf{R}^{n}
with its canonical Euclidean distance), one can always reduce the triangle inequality for general probability measures to this simple case by
an approximation argument (Lemma 5.2 in
[
58
]
).

However, we shall see later that the very notion of a transport map in the quantum setting remains to be clarified. Likewise, the possibility of “glueing” couplings with a common
marginal in the quantum setting seems to be an open problem at the time of this writing.

For these reasons, a third approach to the triangle inequality for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
, entirely based on the Kantorovich duality formula, is proposed in the exercise below
4
4

4

I came up
with this proof during the week of the summer school at the Rényi Institute. The same method applies to the Wasserstein distance of exponent
p
for all
p\in(1,\infty)
, but the
computations (which I shall publish elsewhere) are more involved than in the case
p=2
, which is the only one of interest here. I have not seen this proof in any of the reference
textbooks on optimal transport that I have been using, and I do not know whether it is original.
. Perhaps this could be useful later, for instance in the quantum setting.

Quiz 3.
Let
\mu,\nu,\rho\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, and let
A,{\Gamma}
be optimal functions in the Kantorovich duality formula for
\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\rho)
such that
A\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n},\mu)

and
{\Gamma}\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n},\rho)
, i.e.

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\rho)^{2}=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}A(x)\mu(dx)+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}{\Gamma}(z)\rho(dz)\,.

while
{\alpha}
and
{\gamma}
defined by
{\alpha}(x):=\tfrac{1}{2}(|x|^{2}-A(x))
and
{\gamma}(z):=\tfrac{1}{2}(|z|^{2}-{\Gamma}(z))
are l.s.c. convex functions such that
{\alpha}^{*}={\gamma}
and
{\gamma}^{*}={\alpha}
. For each
\eta>0
,
set

B_{\eta}(y):=(1+\tfrac{1}{\eta})\inf_{z\in\mathbf{R}^{n}}\left(|y-z|^{2}-\tfrac{{\Gamma}(z)}{1+\frac{1}{\eta}}\right)\,,\qquad y\in\mathbf{R}^{n}\,.

(1) Prove that the function

y\mapsto\tfrac{1}{2}\left(|y|^{2}-\tfrac{B_{\eta}(y)}{1+\tfrac{1}{\eta}}\right)

is the Legendre transform of a l.s.c. proper convex function to be computed in terms of
{\Gamma}
.

(2) Prove that
A(x)-B_{\eta}(y)\leq(1+\eta)|x-y|^{2}
for all
x,y\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

(3) Prove that
B_{\eta}\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n},\nu)
for each
\eta>0
.

(4) Prove that, for each
\eta>0

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}A(x)\mu(dx)-\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}B_{\eta}(y)\nu(dy)\leq(1+\eta)\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)^{2}\,.

(5) Prove that, for each
\eta>0

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}B_{\eta}(y)\nu(dy)+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}{\Gamma}(z)\rho(dz)\leq(1+\tfrac{1}{\eta})\mathcal{W}_{2}(\nu,\rho)^{2}\,.

(6) Prove that, for each
\eta>0

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\rho)^{2}\leq(1+\eta)\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)^{2}+(1+\tfrac{1}{\eta})\mathcal{W}_{2}(\nu,\rho)^{2}\,.

(7) Prove that
\mathcal{W}_{2}
satisfies the triangle inequality.

1.2.
Density Operators in Quantum Mechanics

In classical mechanics, the state of a point particle is completely defined by its position
q\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
and its momentum
p\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
. The point particle phase space is therefore

\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, and it is natural to study the probability of finding a point particle in subsets of the phase space. This is precisely the statistical formalism used by Maxwell
and Boltzmann in the kinetic theory of gases.

In quantum mechanics, the analogous formalism involves density operators, a special class of operators on the Hilbert space
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Before studying density operators,
we need to recall some fundamental notions in the theory of operators on Hilbert spaces.

1.2.1.
Trace of an Operator

Let
\mathfrak{H}
be a complex, separable Hilbert space with inner product denoted by
(\cdot\,|\,\cdot)_{\mathfrak{H}}
, and let
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
designate the algebra of bounded operators on the Hilbert
space
\mathfrak{H}
.

Positive operators.
An operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
is said to be positive if

T=T^{*}\,,\quad\text{ and }\quad(x|Tx)_{\mathfrak{H}}\geq 0\,,\qquad x\in\mathfrak{H}\,.

Equivalently, if
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
, then

T=T^{*}\geq 0\iff\text{ there exists }S\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ s.t. }T=S^{*}S\,.

Trace of a positive operator.
For
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
such that
T=T^{*}\geq 0
, we define

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(T):=\sum_{j\geq 1}(e_{j}|Te_{j})_{\mathfrak{H}}\in[0,+\infty]\text{ for all Hilbert basis }(e_{j})_{j\geq 0}\text{ of }\mathfrak{H}\,.

One easily checks that, if this sum is finite for
one
Hilbert basis
(e_{j})_{j\geq 0}
of
\mathfrak{H}
, it is finite for
all
Hilbert basis of
\mathfrak{H}
, since the (infinite dimensional)
“transition matrix” from any Hilbert basis of
\mathfrak{H}
to
(e_{j})_{j\geq 0}
is a unitary operator on
\mathfrak{H}
.

The definition of the trace follows the definition of the Lebesgue integral on the real line: first we define trace of any self-adjoint positive operator, as we define the integral of
any measurable positive function. Then we define the analogue of the Lebesgue space
L^{1}
, and extend the trace to this space by linearity, exactly in the same way as the
integral is extended from the set of measurable positive functions to the Lebesgue space
L^{1}
.

Trace-class operators.
The set of trace-class operators is

\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}):=\{T\!\in\!\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ s.t. }\|T\|_{1}\!:=\!\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(|T|)<\infty\}\,,

where
|T|:=\sqrt{T^{*}T}
for each
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
.

The following properties of
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
are well known.

Properties of trace-class operators.

(a) Trace-class operators are compact:

\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})\subset\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})\,,

where
\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})
is the set of compact operators on
\mathfrak{H}
, i.e. the operator-norm closure of the set of finite rank operators on
\mathfrak{H}
. (One easily checks that
\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})

is a two-sided ideal of
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
, stable by the involution
T\mapsto T^{*}
.)

(b) The set of trace-class operators
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
is a two-sided ideal of
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
, which is stable by the involution
T\mapsto T^{*}
:

\displaystyle A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ and }T\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})

\displaystyle\implies AT\text{ and }TA\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})\,,

\displaystyle T\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})

\displaystyle\implies T^{*}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

(c) The trace, which is defined on the set of positive trace-class operators on
\mathfrak{H}
, extends as a linear functional on
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
satisfying the properties

\operatorname{trace}(T^{*})=\overline{\operatorname{trace}(T)}\,,\quad\operatorname{trace}(AT)=\operatorname{trace}(TA)\,,\quad\text{ and }|\operatorname{trace}(AT)|\leq\|A\|\|T\|_{1}\,,

for all
T\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
and all
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
.

(d) The
\mathbf{C}
-linear space
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
is a Banach space for the trace norm
T\mapsto\|T\|_{1}
; besides
5
5

5

The topological dual of a real or complex normed linear
space
E
, i.e. the set of continuous linear functionals on
E
(with real or complex values), is denoted by
E^{\prime}
.

\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})\,,\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})^{\prime}=\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

In both equalities, the duality is defined by the trace, i.e.

\langle T,K\rangle_{\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}),\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})}:=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(TK)\,,\qquad\langle A,T\rangle_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H}),\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})}:=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(AT)\,.

Partial Trace.
The construction of the trace explained before is strikingly similar to the construction of the Lebesgue integral. In the present section, we are going
to study an analogue of the Fubini theorem.

For each
T\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{2})
, one defines
T_{1}=\operatorname{trace}_{2}(T)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{1})
by the formula

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{1}}(T_{1}A)=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{2}}(T(A\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}_{2}}))\,,\qquad\text{ for all }A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

There is a similar definition of
\operatorname{trace}_{1}(T)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{2})
.

Observe indeed that the map

\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H}_{1})\ni A\mapsto\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{2}}(T(A\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}_{2}}))\in\mathbf{C}

is a norm-continuous linear functional on
\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H}_{1})
, and is therefore represented by a unique trace-class operator
T_{1}
on
\mathfrak{H}_{1}
. That the defining identity for

\operatorname{trace}_{2}(T)
holds for all
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H}_{1})
follows from the density of finite rank operators in
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
for the trace-norm.

Remark.
The analogue of this construction in the context of integration is the following form of the Fubini theorem: if
f\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{m}\times\mathbf{R}^{n})
, the function

y\mapsto f(x,y)
belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
for a.e.
x\in\mathbf{R}^{m}
, the function

F:\,x\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}f(x,y)dy

belongs to
L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{m})
, and

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{m}\times\mathbf{R}^{n}}f(x,y)dxdy=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{m}}F(x)dx\,.

The function
F
in this statement is easily seen to be the analogue of
\operatorname{trace}_{2}(T)
for
T\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H}_{1}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{2})
.

1.2.2.
Hilbert-Schmidt Operators

An operator
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
is said to be a Hilbert-Schmidt operator if

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(T^{*}T)<\infty\,.

The Hilbert-Schmidt class is the set of Hilbert-Schmidt operators:

\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H}):=\{T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ s.t. }\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(T^{*}T)<\infty\}\,.

The Hilbert-Schmidt class
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})
is a Hilbert space for the inner product

(T_{1}|T_{2})_{2}:=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(T_{1}^{*}T_{2})

defining the Hilbert-Schmidt norm

\|T\|_{2}:=\sqrt{\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(T^{*}T)}\,.

Moreover

\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})\subset\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})\subset\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})\subset\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})

with continuous inclusions, and

\|T\|\leq\|T\|_{2}\leq\|T\|_{1}\,,\qquad T\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

Besides
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})
is a two-sided ideal of
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
.

If
T=T^{*}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, then
T
is a compact operator, so that there exists
(e_{j})_{j\geq 1}
, a Hilbert basis of
\mathfrak{H}
and
(\tau_{j})_{j\geq 1}\in\ell^{2}(\mathbf{N}^{*};\mathbf{R})
such that

T=\sum_{j\geq 1}\tau_{j}P_{j}\,,\quad\|T\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\tau_{j}^{2}\quad\text{ with }P_{j}\phi:=(e_{j}|\phi)_{\mathfrak{H}}e_{j}\,.

Besides, in the case where
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, one has therefore

(1)

T\phi(x)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}t(x,y)\phi(y)dy\,,\qquad\text{ for all }\phi\in\mathfrak{H}\,,

where

t(x,y):=\sum_{j\geq 1}\tau_{j}e_{j}(x)\overline{e_{j}(y)}\,.

In particular

(2)

\|T\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{j\geq 1}\tau_{j}^{2}=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}|t(x,y)|^{2}dxdy\,.

Conversely, an integral operator
T
of the form (
1
) belongs to
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})
if and only if
t\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
, and the first left-hand side in (
2
)
is equal to the last right-hand side of in (
2
).

The interested reader will discover additional important properties of trace-class and Hilbert-Schmidt operators in the next two exercises.

Quiz 4.
In this exercise,
\mathfrak{H}:=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

(1) Prove that any
T\in L^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
can be put in the form
T=T_{1}T_{2}
with
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, and that
\|T\|_{1}\leq\|T_{1}\|_{2}\|T_{2}\|_{2}
.

(2) For all
T\in L^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
, can one find
T_{1},T_{2}\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})
such that

T=T_{1}T_{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\|T\|_{1}=\|T_{1}\|_{2}\|T_{2}\|_{2}\,?

(3) Prove that for each
T\in L^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
, there exists
t\equiv t(x,y)
such that
z\mapsto t(x+z,x)
belongs to
C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{z};L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{x}))
,
which is an integral kernel for
T
, in the sense that

T\phi(x)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}t(x,y)\phi(y)dy\,,\qquad\text{ for all }\phi\in\mathfrak{H}\,,

and that

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(T)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}t(x,x)dx\,.

Question (3) suggests that
t(x,y)
is the analogue for
T
of the entries of a matrix if the infinite dimensional Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
is replaced with
\mathbf{C}^{n}
. One might
therefore believe that an integral operator is trace-class if the restriction of its integral kernel to the diagonal is summable. This is not the case, as shown by the next
example.

Quiz 5.
Consider the Volterra operator
V
defined on
L^{2}([0,1])
by the formula

V\phi(x):=\int_{0}^{x}\phi(y)dy\,,\qquad\phi\in L^{2}([0,1])\,.

(1) Prove that
V
is the operator defined by the integral kernel
v(x,y)={\bf 1}_{0\leq y\leq x}
.

(2) Is
V
a Hilbert-Schmidt operator on
L^{2}([0,1])
?

(3) Does the function
x\mapsto v(x,x)
belong to
L^{1}([0,1])
?

(4) Is
V
a trace-class operator on
L^{2}([0,1])
?

(5) What are the eigenvalues of
V
?

(6) What is the spectral radius of
V
?

This last exercise shows the importance of the continuity condition in question (3) of Quiz 3. Any trace-class operator
T
on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, being a Hilbert-Schmidt operator,
is an integral operator defined by a unique integral kernel in
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Since the diagonal is a Lebesgue-negligible set in
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, the restriction
to the diagonal of this integral kernel is a priori not a well defined function. Yet, any trace-class operator
T
on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
has an integral kernel satisfying the continuity
condition of (3) in Quiz 3, which is a representative of the unique
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
integral kernel of
T
viewed as a Hilbert-Schmidt operator on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, and
the trace of
T
is indeed the integral of the restriction of this particular integral kernel to the diagonal, which is a well-defined element of
L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

1.2.3.
Density Operators

As mentioned above, density operators on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
are the quantum analogue of Borel probability measures on the single-particle phase space
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.
The positivity of a probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
becomes the positivity of an operator
R
on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, while the analogue of the normalization
condition
\mu(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})=1
in the quantum setting is the condition
\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(R)=1
.

Throughout this section, we set
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

(Quantum) Density operators.
A density operator on
\mathfrak{H}
is an element of

\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H}):=\{T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ s.t. }T=T^{*}\geq 0\text{ and }\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(T)=1\}\subset\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

As explained above,
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
is the quantum analogue of
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

When dealing with computations involving quantum states, it will be especially convenient to use the notation involving bras and kets, which is recalled below.

Dirac bra-ket notation.
For
\phi,\psi\in\mathfrak{H}
, we denote by
|\psi\rangle
the vector
\psi
, while

\langle\phi|\text{ denotes the linear functional }\psi\mapsto\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\overline{\phi(x)}\psi(x)dx=\langle\phi|\psi\rangle\,.

With this notation, one easily checks that

\psi\in\mathfrak{H}\text{ and }\|\psi\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1\implies|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|=\text{ orthogonal projection on }\mathbf{C}\psi\,.

Example: Schrödinger’s coherent state.
For
q,p\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, set

|q,p\rangle(x):=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d/4}\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{2{\hbar}}|x-q|^{2}\right)\exp\left(\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}p\cdot(x-\tfrac{q}{2})\right)\,.

One easily checks that
\|\,|q,p\rangle\,\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
so that
|q,p\rangle\langle q,p|\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
.

The density operator
|q,p\rangle\langle q,p|
defined above is a quantum analogue (and by no means the only one) of the probability measure
{\delta}_{(q,p)}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

Since density operators are the quantum analogue of phase space (Borel) probability measures, a natural problem is that of comparing two density operators by some
procedure which corresponds to a comparison between phase space (Borel) probability measures in the classical limit of quantum mechanics. In the sequel, we shall
consider an important example, involving Schrödinger’s coherent states, for which explicit computations are very easy.

Key example.
For
(q_{1},p_{1})\not=(q_{2},p_{2})\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, set

R_{1}:=|q_{1},p_{1}\rangle\langle q_{1},p_{1}|\quad\text{ and }\quad R_{2}=|q_{2},p_{2}\rangle\langle q_{2},p_{2}|\,.

Then
R_{1}-R_{2}
is a self-adjoint, rank-
2
operator, such that
\operatorname{trace}(R_{1}-R_{2})=0
, and hence there exists
{\lambda}>0
and an orthonormal basis
(e,f)
of
\operatorname{Ran}(R_{1}-R_{2})

for which

R_{1}-R_{2}={\lambda}|e\rangle\langle e|-{\lambda}|f\rangle\langle f|\,.

Therefore

\|R_{1}-R_{2}\|_{1}=2{\lambda}\quad\text{ and }\quad\|R_{1}-R_{2}\|_{2}=\sqrt{2}{\lambda}\,,

so that

\|R_{1}-R_{2}\|_{1}=\sqrt{2}\|R_{1}-R_{2}\|_{2}\,.

On the other hand,
R_{1}-R_{2}
is the Hilbert-Schmidt integral operator with integral kernel

\displaystyle r(x,y):=

\displaystyle(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d/2}\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{2{\hbar}}(|x-q_{1}|^{2}+|y-q_{1}|^{2})\right)\exp\left(\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}p_{1}\cdot(x-y)\right)

\displaystyle-(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d/2}\exp\left(-\tfrac{1}{2{\hbar}}(|x-q_{2}|^{2}+|y-q_{2}|^{2})\right)\exp\left(\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}p_{2}\cdot(x-y)\right)\,,

so that

\|R_{1}-R_{2}\|_{2}^{2}=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}|r(x,y)|^{2}dxdy=2(1-e^{-(|q_{1}-q_{2}|^{2}+|p_{1}-p_{2}|^{2})/2{\hbar}})\,.

Thus

\|R_{1}-R_{2}\|_{1}=2\sqrt{1-e^{-(|q_{1}-q_{2}|^{2}+|p_{1}-p_{2}|^{2})/2{\hbar}}}\to 2\quad\text{ as }{\hbar}\to 0^{+}

since
(q_{1},p_{1})\not=(q_{2}-p_{2})
. In other words, passing to the limit as
{\hbar}\to 0
,

\|\,|q_{1},p_{1}\rangle\langle q_{1},p_{1}|-|q_{2},p_{2}\rangle\langle q_{2},p_{2}|\,\|_{1}\to\|{\delta}_{(q_{1},p_{1})}-{\delta}_{(q_{2},p_{2})}\|_{TV}=\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&2&&\text{ if }(q_{1},p_{1})\not=(q_{2},p_{2})\,,\\
&0&&\text{ if }(q_{1},p_{1})=(q_{2},p_{2})\,,\end{aligned}\right.

In particular,
\|\cdot\|_{1}
fails to discriminate between density operators concentrating on phase space points at a distance
\gg O({\hbar}^{1/2})
of each other in the classical limit.

1.3.
The Connes Distance in Noncommutative Geometry

It is well known that one of the main differences between quantum and classical mechanics is that the product of phase space coordinates of a point particle in classical mechanics
is a commutative operation, whereas the quantum analogues of these quantities are operators on a Hilbert space, and their product is in general noncommutative. For example,
think of a point particle in space dimension
1
, and let
q,p\in\mathbf{R}
designate respectively its position and momentum in classical mechanics. Obviously

pq-qp=0\,.

In quantum mechanics, the real-valued functions
(q,p)\mapsto q
and
(q,p)\mapsto p
are replaced with (unbounded) operators
\hat{q}
and
\hat{p}
on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R})
satisfying
the canonical commutation relation (CCR)

[\hat{p},\hat{q}]=\hat{p}\hat{q}-\hat{q}\hat{p}=-i\hbar\text{Id}\,.

To the best of our knowledge, the first attempt at extending the notions of optimal transport to the noncommutative setting is due to Connes
[
24
]
. We shall briefly
describe his work on this topic in this section.

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a unital
C^{*}
-algebra. (We recall that a
C^{*}
-algebra is a complex Banach algebra endowed with a linear involution
x\mapsto x^{*}
such that
({\alpha}x)^{*}=\bar{\alpha}x^{*}

for all
{\alpha}\in\mathbf{C}
and
\|x^{*}x\|=\|x\|^{2}
for all
x
in the algebra. The latter condition is known as “the
C^{*}
identity”. This is a very strong condition, which connects the norm
with the algebraic structure. For instance, it implies that
\|x\|^{2}
is the spectral radius of
x^{*}x
for each
x
in the algebra.) Here are a few examples of
C^{*}
-algebras:

(a)
\mathcal{A}=C(X,\mathbf{C})
with
X
compact; in this example

f^{*}(x):=\overline{f(x)}\text{ for all }x\in X\,,\qquad\|f\|:=\sup_{x\in X}|f(x)|\,.

This is the prototype of a unital commutative
C^{*}
-algebra, the unit being the constant function
x\mapsto 1
.

(b)
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
where
\mathfrak{H}
is a separable complex Hilbert space; in this example, the involution is
T\mapsto T^{*}
where
T^{*}
is the adjoint of the operator
T
on
\mathfrak{H}
,
while
\|T\|
is the operator-norm of
T
, i.e.
\|T\|:=\sup\{\|Tx\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\,:\,\|x\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1\}
. This is a unital
C^{*}
-algebra, with unit
\text{Id}_{\mathfrak{H}}
, and it is not separable for the norm
topology.

(c)
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})
, the set of compact operators on an infinite dimensional, separable complex Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}
; this is a non unital
C^{*}
-subalgebra of
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
; in fact

\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})
is the only norm-closed two-sided ideal of
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
.

(d)
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})/\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})
where
\mathfrak{H}
is an infinite dimensional, separable complex Hilbert space; this a unital
C^{*}
-algebra, which is simple — meaning that its only closed
two-sided ideals are
\{0\}
and
\mathcal{A}
— and known as the Calkin algebra; obviously
\mathcal{A}
is not of the form
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H}_{1})
for any separable Hilbert space.

A
state
on a
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
is a positive linear functional on
\mathcal{A}
— positive meaning that
{\omega}(a^{*}a)\geq 0
— of norm
1
— meaning that

\|{\omega}\|:=\sup\{|{\omega}(x)|\,:\,x\in\mathcal{A}\text{ and }\|x\|\leq 1\}=1\,.

It is a classical exercise
6
6

6

Here is a hint for the interested reader to prove the direct implication. If
{\omega}
is a positive linear functional on
\mathcal{A}
, check that
(x,y)\mapsto{\omega}(x^{*}y)

is a positive sesquilinear form on
\mathcal{A}
, and use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality to check that
{\omega}(x^{*})=\overline{{\omega}(x)}
and that
{\omega}
is continuous on
\mathcal{A}
with norm
\|{\omega}\|={\omega}(1)
.

to check that, if
{\omega}
is continuous linear functional on a unital
C^{*}
-algebra
\mathcal{A}
, then

{\omega}\text{ is positive }\iff\|{\omega}\|={\omega}(1)\,.

Examples of states

\bullet
\mathcal{A}=C(X;\mathbf{C})
with
X
compact, and
{\omega}(f)=f(x_{0})=\langle{\delta}_{x_{0}},f\rangle
, for some
x_{0}\in X
;

\bullet
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
and
{\omega}(A):=\langle\psi|A|\psi\rangle
for some
\psi\in\mathfrak{H}
with
\|\psi\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
;

\bullet
\mathcal{A}=\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
and
{\omega}(A):=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(RA)
for some
R\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
.

Let
(\mathfrak{H},D)
be a Fredholm module on
\mathcal{A}
, meaning that

(a) there is a
∗
-linear representation
7
7

7

I.e.
\pi
is a morphism of algebras from
\mathcal{A}
to
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
satisfying the condition
\pi(a^{*})=\pi(a)^{*}
)

\pi
of
\mathcal{A}

in
\mathfrak{H}
,

(b)
D=D^{*}
is a self-adjoint unbounded operator on
\mathfrak{H}
such that

(I+D^{2})^{-1}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})\,,

(c)
\{a\in\mathcal{A}\text{ s.t. }[D,\pi(a)]\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\}
is norm-dense in
\mathcal{A}
.

Theorem (Connes
[
24
]
).
Assume that

\{a\in\mathcal{A}\text{ s.t. }\|[D,\pi(a)]\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leq 1\}/\mathbf{C}1\text{ is bounded.}

Then, the following formula metrizes the set of states on
\mathcal{A}
:

\operatorname{dist}_{C}({\omega}_{1},{\omega}_{2}):=\sup\{|{\omega}_{1}(a)-{\omega}_{2}(a)|\text{ s.t. }\|[D,\pi(a)]\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leq 1\}\,.

There is an obvious similarity between Connes’ definition and the Kantorovich(-Rubinstein) duality for
\mathcal{W}_{1}
: it clearly suggests to think of Connes’ distance as the
analogue of the metric
\mathcal{W}_{1}
in noncommutative geometry.

It is interesting to see how Connes’ definition can be applied to a commutative setting, corresponding to example (a) above of a
C^{*}
-algebra.

Example 1: the Dirac operator as a Fredholm module.
Set
\mathcal{A}=C(M)
where
M
is a compact spin Riemannian manifold (see for instance
[
13
]
),
with Riemannian metric
g
. Let
S
be the spinor bundle on
M
, and set
\mathfrak{H}:=L^{2}(M;S)
, the Hilbert space of
L^{2}
sections of
S
. Let
\mathcal{A}
act on
\mathfrak{H}
by scalar
multiplication — to avoid unnecessary complications in the notation, we write
a\xi
instead of
\pi(a)\xi
everywhere in this example. Finally, let
D
be the Dirac operator
on
M
.

Then, the geodesic distance
\operatorname{dist}_{g}
on
M
satisfies the following property:

\operatorname{dist}_{g}(x,y)=\sup\{|a(x)-a(y)|\,:\,a\in C(M)\text{ s.t. }\|[D,a]\|\leq 1\}=\operatorname{dist}_{C}({\delta}_{x},{\delta}_{y})\,.

Proof.

Denote by
{\gamma}(v)\zeta
the Clifford multiplication of
\zeta\in S_{x}
by
v\in T_{x}M
. Since
D
is a differential operator of order
1
, one easily checks that

([D,a]\xi)_{x}\!=\!{\gamma}((\text{grad }a)_{x})\xi_{x}\,,\quad\xi\in\mathfrak{H}\,,

for all
a\in C^{1}(M)
, so that

\|[D,a]\|\!=\!\|\text{grad }a\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}\,.

Hence

\operatorname{dist}_{C}({\delta}_{x},{\delta}_{y})=\sup_{\operatorname{Lip}(a)\leq 1}|a(x)-a(y)|\,,

and

\operatorname{dist}_{g}(x,y)\leq\operatorname{dist}_{C}({\delta}_{x},{\delta}_{y})\leq\operatorname{dist}_{g}(x,y)\,.

The upper bound is obvious by definition of the Lipschitz constant; as for the lower bound, it suffices to pick the function
a(z):=\operatorname{dist}_{g}(z,y)
.
∎

Example 2: the word length on a discrete group as a Fredholm module.
Let
\Gamma
be a discrete group, with reduced
C^{*}
-algebra
C^{*}_{red}(\Gamma)

defined as the
C^{*}
-algebra generated by the left regular representation
{\lambda}
on
\mathfrak{H}:=\ell^{2}(\Gamma)
. (We recall that
({\lambda}(g)\xi)_{h}:=\xi_{g^{-1}h}
for each

\xi=(\xi_{h})_{h\in\Gamma}\in\ell^{2}(\Gamma)
.) Let
L:\,\Gamma\to\mathbf{R}_{+}
be a length function (for instance the
word length with respect to a system of generators of
\Gamma
). In other words, we assume that

L(1)=0\,,\quad L(g^{-1})L(g)\,,\quad L(gh)\leq L(g)+L(h)\,,\qquad g,h\in\Gamma\,.

Assume that
L(g)\to+\infty
as
g\to\infty
, and set

D\xi:=(L(g)\xi_{g})_{g\in\Gamma}\quad\text{ for all }\xi=(\xi_{g})_{g\in\Gamma}\in\ell^{2}(\Gamma)\,.

Then
(\mathfrak{H},D)
is an unbounded Fredholm module on
C^{*}_{red}(\Gamma)
. As in the preceding example, we seek to compute
\|[D,a]\|
for
a\in C^{*}_{red}(\Gamma)
.
One finds that

\|[D,{\lambda}(g)]\|=L(g)\,.

(Indeed, one has

({\lambda}(g)D{\lambda}(g^{-1})\xi)_{h}=(D{\lambda}(g^{-1})\xi)_{g^{-1}h}=L(g^{-1}h)({\lambda}(g^{-1})\xi)_{g^{-1}h}=L(g^{-1}h)\xi_{h}\,,

so that

(({\lambda}(g)D{\lambda}(g^{-1})-D)\xi)_{h}=(L(g^{-1}h)-L(h))\xi_{h}\,,

and

\|[{\lambda}(g),D]\|=\|{\lambda}(g)D{\lambda}(g^{-1})-D\|=\sup_{h\in\Gamma}|L(g^{-1}h-L(h)|=L(g)\,.

Connes’ distance in noncommutative geometry is defined via an analogue of the Kantorovich(-Rubinstein) duality formula. Whether there exists a formula involving a notion
of noncommutative coupling or transport plan seems to be an open question at the time of this writing — see however an interesting contribution to this problem by D’Andrea
and Martinetti
[
25
]
.

In spite of its great interest, the Connes distance is not exactly the quantum analogue of the Wasserstein distance which we are looking for. The first example presented above
suggests that the Connes distance is a noncommutative analogue of the classical distance on the space of positions, and not of a phase space distance. Moreover, the Connes
distance is clearly an analogue of the Monge, or Wasserstein distance of exponent
1
, instead of the Wasserstein distance of exponent
2
, for which the optimal transport
problem seems to have more structure (in particular by the Knott-Smith or the Brenier theorems).

1.4.
A Quantum Analogue of
\mathcal{W}_{2}
.

This section gathers together analogues of the Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{2}
in the quantum setting introduced in
[
37
,
38
]
. The presentation given
here is closer in spirit to
[
40
]
.

Other approaches to the problem of generalizing
\mathcal{W}_{2}
to the quantum setting have been proposed by other authors: see for instance
[
26
]
and the earlier
reference
[
65
]
.

1.4.1.
Transport Cost

The first step in extending the Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{2}
to the quantum setting is obviously to find some appropriate definition of the transport cost.

In classical mechanics, the phase space coordinates of a point particle are its position
q\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
and its momentum
p\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

In quantum mechanics, these coordinates, or functions thereof, must be replaced with appropriate operators. This procedure — associating operators on a Hilbert space
to functions of the classical phase space coordinates — is called “quantization”.

The simplest quantization procedure

(i) associates to each function of the position variable only a multiplication operator

a(q)\to\text{ multiplication by }a(y)\text{ in }L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y})\,;

(ii) associates to the classical momentum variable
p
the momentum operator

p\mapsto-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}\text{ viewed as an unbounded self-adjoint operator on }L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y})\,.

The classical transport cost is a function on the Cartesian product of the phase space with itself which is the square Euclidean distance from the phase space point
(x,\xi)

to the phase space point
(q,p)
in
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, i.e.

|x-q|^{2}+|\xi-p|^{2}\,.

The most natural thing to do is to quantize this expression in the variables
(q,p)
to measure the cost of transporting a particle from the classical phase space point
(x,\xi)

to a quantum state with position
y
and momentum
-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}
(whatever it means). This leads to the

Classical-to-quantum
transport cost, which is an operator on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y})
:

c_{\hbar}(x,\xi):=|x-y|^{2}+|\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}|^{2}\,.

This is ‘‘the’’ quantization
8
8

8

In truth, there is more than one quantization procedure; we shall see later in this lecture a notion of “Toeplitz quantization”, which in this
case would give a slightly different result, with a difference of order
O({\hbar})
.
in
(y,\eta)
of
(q,p)\mapsto|x-q|^{2}+|\xi-p|^{2}
.

One can also quantize this expression in both variables
(x,\xi)
and
(q,p)
to measure the cost of transporting a quantum particle from a quantum state with position
x

and momentum
-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x}
to a quantum state with position
y
and momentum
-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}
. This leads to the

Quantum-to-quantum
transport cost, which is an operator on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y})
:

C_{\hbar}:=|x-y|^{2}-{\hbar}^{2}({\nabla}_{x}-{\nabla}_{y})\cdot({\nabla}_{x}-{\nabla}_{y})\,.

This is “the” quantization of the function
(x,\xi,q,p)\mapsto|x-q|^{2}+|\xi-p|^{2}
.

Notice that
c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)
is the Hamiltonian of the harmonic oscillator, shifted in phase space by
(x,\xi)
, while
C_{\hbar}
is the Hamiltonian of a harmonic oscillator in the
variable
x-y
.

Observe that

c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)\geq d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}}\,,\quad\text{ for all }x,\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\,,

while

C_{\hbar}\geq 2d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}\,,

where
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. These lower bounds are implied by Heisenberg’s uncertainty inequalities.

The exercise below gathers together several important facts related to the Hamiltonian of the harmonic oscillator
y^{2}-{\hbar}^{2}{\partial}_{y}^{2}
on the real line.

Quiz 6.
In this exercise
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R})
. Set

{\omega}(x):=\pi^{-1/4}e^{-x^{2}/2}\,,

and

a:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(x+{\partial}_{x})\,,\quad a^{*}:=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(x-{\partial}_{x})\,.

(1) Find
\operatorname{Ker}(a)
, and prove that
\operatorname{Ker}(a)\subset\mathfrak{H}
. Compute the commutator

[a,a^{*}]=aa^{*}-a^{*}a

together with the operators
aa^{*}
and
a^{*}a
.

Set
\mathcal{V}:=\{\psi\in H^{1}(\mathbf{R})\text{ s.t. }y\mapsto y\psi(y)\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R})\}
.

(2) Find

\inf_{\psi\in\mathcal{V},\,\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}=1}\int_{\mathbf{R}}\overline{\psi(y)}(y^{2}-\hbar^{2}\partial_{y}^{2})\psi(y)dy\,.

(3) Find

\inf_{\psi\in\mathcal{V},\,\|\psi\|_{L^{2}}=1}\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}}|y|^{2}|\psi(y)|^{2}dy\right)^{1/2}\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}}|\hbar{\partial}_{y}\psi(y)|^{2}dy\right)^{1/2}\,.

(Hint: change
\hbar
in
{\epsilon}\hbar
in question (2) where
{\epsilon}>0
is arbitrary, and conclude by a minimization argument in
{\epsilon}>0
.)

Question (3) leads to an inequality which is a mathematical formulation of Heisenberg’s uncertainty principle (for its physical interpretation, see for instance §16 in
[
48
]

or chapter I.C.3 in
[
23
]
.

(4) Find the spectrum of
a^{*}a
. (Hint: find
\operatorname{Ker}(a^{*}a)
by using question (1). Then, argue as in the solution of the following classical exercise in algebra: if
\mathcal{A}
is a unital
algebra with unit denoted by
1
, and if
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
, then
1-ab
is invertible in
\mathcal{A}
iff
1-ba
is invertible in
\mathcal{A}
. To solve this exercise, the idea is to guess a formula
relating
(1-ab)^{-1}
and
(1-ba)^{-1}
, which can be done easily by writing
(1-x)^{-1}
as a formal series in powers of
x
.)

(5) Compute
a^{*}af_{n}
for each integer
n\geq 0
, where
f_{n}:=(a^{*})^{n}{\omega}
.

(6) Set
\phi_{n}=f_{n}/\sqrt{n!}
. Prove that
(\phi_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is an orthonormal system of
\mathfrak{H}
.

(7) Prove that
\phi_{n}=(-1)^{n}{\omega}H_{n}/\sqrt{2^{n}n!}
for each integer
n\geq 0
, where

H_{n}=(-1)^{n}{\omega}^{-2}{\partial}_{x}^{n}{\omega}^{2}

is the
n
-th Hermite polynomial in the so-called “physical form”. What is the leading coefficient in
H_{n}
?

(8) Prove that the orthonormal system
(\phi_{n})_{n\geq 0}
is complete in
\mathfrak{H}
.

(9) Consider the Fourier transform scaled as follows:

\mathbf{F}\psi(\xi):=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{\mathbf{R}}\psi(x)e^{-i\xi x}dx\,.

Compute
\mathbf{F}(\phi_{n})
for each integer
n\geq 0
.

1.4.2.
Finite Energy Density Operators

Before going further in the definition of a quantum analogue of the Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{2}
, we need to define the analogue of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

Using the basic quantization procedure given in the previous section, shows that the phase space Euclidean norm is transformed into the Hamiltonian of a quantum harmonic
oscillator:

\underbrace{|q|^{2}+|p|^{2}}_{\text{phase space}\atop\text{Euclidean norm}}\to\underbrace{|x|^{2}-{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}_{x}}_{\text{harmonic oscillator}}\,.

This suggests the following definition of a quantum analogue to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
(the set of Borel probability measures on phase space with finite second order moments):

\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\!:=\{R\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ s.t. }\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(R^{\frac{1}{2}}(|x|^{2}-{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}_{x})R^{\frac{1}{2}})<\infty\}\,.

In other words,
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
is the set of density operators with finite energy for the quantum harmonic oscillator.

If
\{\psi_{n}\!\in\!L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d},|x|^{2}dx)\cap H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\}
is an orthonormal system in
\mathfrak{H}\!=\!L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, then

R\!=\!\sum_{n\geq 1}\!\rho_{n}|\psi_{n}\rangle\langle\psi_{n}|\!\in\!\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\!\iff\!\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&\rho_{n}\geq 0\text{ and }\sum_{n\geq 1}\rho_{n}=1\,,\\
&\sum_{n\geq 1}\rho_{n}(\|x\psi_{n}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}^{2}\!\!+\!{\hbar}^{2}\|{\nabla}\psi_{n}\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}})\!<\!\infty\,.\end{aligned}\right.

The very simple verification of this statement is left to the reader.

1.4.3.
Couplings

We already know the notion of coupling between two probability densities
\mu
and
\nu
belonging to
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
: the set of such couplings is denoted by

\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)
.

Similarly, we define the set of
couplings of
2
quantum density
operators
R,S\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(R,S):=\{T\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H})\text{ s.t. }\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(T(A\otimes I+I\otimes B))=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(RA+SB)\,,

\displaystyle\quad\text{ for all }A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\}

\displaystyle.

The condition involving the test operators
A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
can be equivalently replaced by conditions on partial traces:

\operatorname{trace}_{2}(T)=R\quad\text{ and }\quad\operatorname{trace}_{1}(T)=S\,.

Finally, we define a notion of
coupling of a classical probability density
f
and a quantum density operator
R
. Let
f(x,\xi)
be a probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{2d}

and let
R\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
. A coupling of
f
and
R
is a measurable
9
9

9

Since
f\in L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(x,\xi))=\|Q(x,\xi)\|_{1}=f(x,\xi)<\infty
for a.e.

(x,\xi)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, one has
Q(x,\xi)\ni\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
for a.e.
(x,\xi)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
. Since
\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
is separable, the map
Q
is weakly measurable if
and only if it is strongly measurable by the Pettis Theorem (see Theorem 2 in chapter II of
[
28
]
). In other words, there are no measurability issues with
Q
.

operator-valued map
Q
defined on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, with values in
\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
, satisfying the following properties:

\displaystyle\mathbf{R}^{2d}\ni(x,\xi)\mapsto Q(x,\xi)=Q(x,\xi)^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ s.t. }Q(x,\xi)\geq 0\text{ a.e.}

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(x,\xi))=f(x,\xi)\text{ a.e.,}\qquad\text{ and }\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}Q(x,\xi)dxd\xi=R\,.

In this case again, the set of couplings of
f
with
R
will be denoted by
\mathcal{C}(f,R)
.

Here are trivial examples of couplings (usually not the most clever couplings in optimal transport).

Examples of couplings.

(a) For all
R,S\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
, the tensor product
R\otimes S
belongs to
\mathcal{C}(R,S)
.

(b) For each
f
probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{2d}
, and each density operator
R
on
\mathfrak{H}
, the operator-valued map

fR=f\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}R:\,(x,\xi)\mapsto f(x,\xi)R

belongs to
\mathcal{C}(f,R)
.

In particular

\mathcal{C}(R,S)\not=\varnothing\quad\text{ and }\quad\mathcal{C}(f,R)\not=\varnothing\,.

(We have already observed that the set of couplings of two probability measures is never empty, since it always contains the tensor product of these two measures.)

1.4.4.
Extending the Wasserstein Distance to
\mathfrak{D}:=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})

We are now ready to define the most important object in these lectures, namely the extension of the Wasserstein
\mathcal{W}_{2}
distance to the (disjoint) union of the sets
of classical and quantum densities.

First we define the (disjoint) union of the set of (classical) Borel probability measures on phase space with finite second order moments, and of the set of finite energy
(quantum) density operators:

\mathfrak{D}:=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

Definition of
\mathfrak{d}
on
\mathfrak{D}\times\mathfrak{D}
.

(1) For each
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{2d})
, set

\mathfrak{d}(\mu,\nu):=\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\,.

(2) For each phase space probability density
f
such that
f(x,\xi)dxd\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{2d})
and each
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, set

\mathfrak{d}(f,R):=\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{C}(f,R)}\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(x,\xi)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)Q(x,\xi)^{\frac{1}{2}})dxd\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

(3) For each
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, set

\mathfrak{d}(R,S):=\inf_{T\in\mathcal{C}(R,S)}\left(\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}T^{\frac{1}{2}})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

Remark.
For all
f(x,\xi)dxd\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{2d})
and all
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, one has

\mathfrak{d}(f,R)\geq\sqrt{d{\hbar}}\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\mathfrak{d}(R,S)\geq\sqrt{2d{\hbar}}\,.

In particular
\mathfrak{d}(R,R)>0
, so that
\mathfrak{d}
is not a bona fide metric on
\mathfrak{D}
.

Quiz 7.
Let
\mathfrak{H}
be a (complex) separable Hilbert space.

(1) Let
A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
. Prove that

A^{*}B+B^{*}A\leq|A|^{2}+|B|^{2}\,.

(We recall that
|A|^{2}:=A^{*}A
.)

(2) Prove that, for all
{\epsilon}>0
and all
A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
, one has

A^{*}B+B^{*}A\leq{\epsilon}|A|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{{\epsilon}}|B|^{2}\,.

(3) Prove that, for all
{\epsilon}>0
and all
A,B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
, one has

|A+B|^{2}\leq(1+{\epsilon})|A|^{2}+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\epsilon}})|B|^{2}\,.

(4) Let
A,B
be Hilbert-Schmidt operators on
\mathfrak{H}
. Deduce from (2) that

|\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(B^{*}A)|\leq\sqrt{\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(|A|^{2})}\sqrt{\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(|B|^{2})}\,.

(5) Let
R=R^{*}\geq 0
be a trace-class operator on
\mathfrak{H}
. Prove that

|\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(B^{*}AR)|\leq\sqrt{\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(|A|^{2}R)}\sqrt{\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(|B|^{2}R)}\,.

(6) Let
f
be a convex function on
\mathbf{R}
(in particular
f\in C(\mathbf{R})
), let
R\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
, and let
A=A^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
. Prove that

f(\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(AR))\leq\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(f(A)R)\,.

(Hint: prove that, for each
z,m\in\mathbf{R}
and for all
{\lambda}\in[f^{\prime}_{g}(m),f^{\prime}_{d}(m)]
, one has the inequality
f(z)\geq f(m)+{\lambda}(z-m)
. Using the spectral measure
(\xi|E(dz)\xi)
of
A
, where

\xi\in\mathfrak{H}
and
E
is the spectral decomposition
10
10

10

See for instance chapter 12 in
[
57
]
.
of
A
, prove that
f(A)\geq f(m)I+{\lambda}(A-mI)
. Conclude by choosing
m

appropriately.)

(7) How should one modify (1) if
A
and
B
are unbounded operators on
\mathfrak{H}
?

(8) Prove that

\mathfrak{d}(f,R)+\mathfrak{d}(R,S)<\infty

for each probability density
f
with finite second order moments on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, and all
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
.

In practice, the “pseudometric”
\mathfrak{d}
is not easy to compute, except in a few cases. But before discussing these cases, we need to return to the question of “quantization”
— i.e. associating an operator on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
to a function on phase space (i.e.
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
).

1.4.5.
Toeplitz Operators and Husimi Transform.

We begin with the definition of a quantum analogue of the Dirac mass at the phase space point
(q,p)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

Gaussian wave packet (Schrödinger coherent state).
For all
q,p\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, set

|q,p\rangle(x):=(\pi{\hbar})^{-d/4}\exp(-\tfrac{1}{2{\hbar}}|x-q|^{2})\exp(\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}p\cdot(x-\tfrac{q}{2}))\,.

This is a plane wave oscillating at frequency
|p|/{\hbar}
in the direction
p/|p|
, modulated by a Gaussian envelope of width
O(\sqrt{{\hbar}})
, centered at the position
q
.
Therefore, the oscillating profile of the wave function
|q,p\rangle
encodes the momentum vector
p
, while the envelope of the oscillations encodes the position
q\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

Oscillating structure of a Gaussian wave-packet

Figure 2.
With
{\hbar}=8\cdot 10^{-5}
, plot of
Z=
real part of the coherent state centered at
q=(0,0)
with momentum
p=(1,0)
with space variable
(X,Y)\in\mathbf{R}^{2}

Toeplitz map.
To
m
, a (complex) Radon measure on the phase space
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, one associates the operator

\mathcal{T}[m]:=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}|q,p\rangle\langle q,p|m(dqdp)\,.

The form-domain of
\mathcal{T}[m]
is the set of
\phi\in\mathfrak{H}
such that the function
(q,p)\mapsto\langle q,p|\psi\rangle
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{2d};m)
.

Observe that
|q,p\rangle\langle q,p|
is a self-adjoint positive operator (specifically, a rank-
1
projection in
\mathfrak{H}
). Therefore, if the measure
m
is real-valued, the operator

\mathcal{T}[m]
(with some appropriate domain) is expected to be self-adjoint, and if
m
is a positive measure on phase space, the associated Toeplitz operator
\mathcal{T}[m]

is expected to be a positive operator on
\mathfrak{H}
(possibly unbounded, with some appropriate domain).

Basic properties of the Toeplitz map.

(1) The set of Schrödinger coherent states is a resolution of the identity:

\mathcal{T}[1]=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}|q,p\rangle\langle q,p|dqdp=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}I_{\mathfrak{H}}\,.

(2) The image by the Toeplitz map
\mathcal{T}
of the set of Borel phase space probability measure on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
is included in
\mathcal{D}(L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}))
:

m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\implies\mathcal{T}[m]\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})\,,\quad\text{ with }\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\,.

(3) One has

\mathcal{T}[q]=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}x\text{ (position operator),}

while

\mathcal{T}[p]=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}(-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x})\text{ (momentum operator).}

(4) If
f
is a quadratic form on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, then

\left\{\begin{aligned} \mathcal{T}[f(q)]&=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\left(f(x)+\tfrac{1}{4}{\hbar}({\Delta}f)I_{\mathfrak{H}}\right)\,,\text{ and}\\
\mathcal{T}[f(p)]&=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\left(f(-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x})+\tfrac{1}{4}{\hbar}({\Delta}f)I_{\mathfrak{H}}\right)\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Quiz 8.
Prove the statements (1)-(4) above. (Hint: recall the formulas for moments of order
\leq 2
of Gaussian distributions, together with the oscillating
integrals

\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}e^{ip\cdot(x-y)}dp={\delta}_{0}(x-y)\,,\qquad\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}p_{j}e^{ip\cdot(x-y)}dp=-i{\partial}_{x_{j}}{\delta}_{0}(x-y)\,,

and

\tfrac{1}{2\pi}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}p_{j}p_{k}e^{ip\cdot(x-y)}dp=-{\partial}_{x_{j}}{\partial}_{x_{k}}{\delta}_{0}(x-y)\,,\qquad j,k=1,\ldots,d

— which are to be understood in the sense of tempered distributions. For instance, the first formula above is equivalent to the Fourier inversion formula on
the set of tempered distributions.)

The Toeplitz map associates an operator on
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
to a function on the phase space
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
. Conversely, given an operator on
\mathfrak{H}
,
we seek to associate a function on phase space. There are various ways of doing this, one of which is the Husimi transform.

Husimi Transform.
To
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
, one associates its Husimi transform

\mathcal{H}[T](q,p):=\tfrac{1}{(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}}\langle q,p|T|q,p\rangle\,.

This definition can be extended to all unbounded operators on
T
such that the Gaussian wave packet
|q,p\rangle
belongs to the form domain of
T
for each

q,p\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

Basic properties of the Husimi transform.

(1) For each
T\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
,

T=T^{*}\implies\mathcal{H}[T](q,p)\in\mathbf{R}\,,\text{ and }T\geq 0\implies\mathcal{H}[T]\geq 0\,.

(2) The Husimi transform is an “almost inverse” of the Toeplitz map: for each
m\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})

\mathcal{H}[\mathcal{T}[m]]=e^{\frac{{\hbar}}{2}{\Delta}_{q,p}}m\,,

since

\langle q,p|q^{\prime},p^{\prime}\rangle=e^{-\frac{1}{4{\hbar}}(|q-q^{\prime}|^{2}+|p-p^{\prime}|^{2})}e^{-\frac{i}{{\hbar}}(p\cdot q^{\prime}-q\cdot p^{\prime})}\,.

(3) One has

\mathcal{H}[I]=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}\,,

while

\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&\mathcal{H}[f(x)](q,p)=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}(I+\tfrac{1}{4}{\hbar}{\Delta})f(q)\,,\text{ and }\\
&\mathcal{H}[f(-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x})](q,p)=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}(I+\tfrac{1}{4}{\hbar}{\Delta})f(p)\,.\end{aligned}\right.

(4) One has

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(R^{*}\mathcal{T}[f])=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}\overline{\mathcal{H}[R](q,p)}f(q,p)dqdp\,.

Quiz 9.
Prove the statements (1)-(4) above.

An important property of the Husimi transform is that it is a one-to-one transformation. In other words, the Husimi transform of an operator specifies it completely.

Quiz 10.
Set
\mathfrak{H}:=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

(1) Let
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
. Prove that
\mathcal{H}[R]
is a probability density, and compute

\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}(|q|^{2}+|p|^{2})\mathcal{H}[R](q,p)dqdp\,.

(2) Let
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, and assume that
\mathcal{H}[R]=\mathcal{H}[S]
. Prove that
R=S
. (Hint: let
r\equiv r(y,y^{\prime})
be an integral kernel of
R
. Set

J(x,\xi)=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}r(y,y^{\prime})e^{-(|y|^{2}+|y^{\prime}|^{2})/2{\hbar}}e^{x\cdot(y+y^{\prime})-i\xi\cdot(y-y^{\prime})/{\hbar}}dydy^{\prime}\,.

Prove that
J
extends as a holomorphic function on
\mathbf{C}^{d}\times\mathbf{C}^{d}
, and therefore is uniquely determined by its restriction to
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
. Conclude
by (a) computing the formula relating
\mathcal{H}[R]
to
J
, and (b) by computing the integral kernel
r
of
R
in terms of
J
.)

Here is another way of associating a function on the phase space
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
to an operator on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

Quiz 11.
Set
\mathfrak{H}:=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. To each
A\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
with integral kernel
a\equiv a(x,y)
such that
z\mapsto a(x+z,x)
belongs to
C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{d};L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d}))

(see Quiz 4), we associate its Wigner transform

W[A](x,\xi):=\tfrac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}a(x+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y,x-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y)e^{-i\xi\cdot y}dy

(where the integral above is to be understood as the partial Fourier transform of the continuous bounded function
y\mapsto a(x+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y,x-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y)
with
values in
L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{x})
, which is therefore a tempered distribution).

(1) Prove that

\overline{W[A]}=W[A^{*}]\,,

and that, for each
A,B\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(A^{*}B)=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}\overline{W[A](x,\xi)}W[B](x,\xi)dxd\xi\,,

and

\|A\|_{2}=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d/2}\|W[A]\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})}\,.

Prove that the Wigner transform has a unique extension to
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})
.

(2) Prove that, for each
A\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
,

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(A)=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}W[A](x,\xi)dxd\xi\,.

(3) Let
t\mapsto R(t)
be a time-dependent density operator, solution of the von Neumann equation

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}R(t)=[-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}+V,R(t)]\,.

Prove that
W[R(t)]
is a solution of the Wigner equation

({\partial}_{t}+\xi\cdot{\nabla}_{x})W[R(t)](x,\xi)+\Theta[V]W[R(t)](x,\xi)=0\,,

where
\Theta[V]
is the linear operator with distribution kernel

\tfrac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\tfrac{1}{i{\hbar}}(V(x+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y)-V(x-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y))e^{iy\cdot(\eta-\xi)}dy\,.

Prove that

\Theta[V]=-{\nabla}V(x)\cdot{\nabla}_{\xi}

in the case where
V
is a polynomial of degree
2
.

In that case (for a quadratic potential
V
), the Wigner equation coincides with the classical Liouville equation on the phase space
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}

{\partial}_{t}W[R(t)](x,\xi)+\{\tfrac{1}{2}|\xi|^{2}+V(x),W[R(t)](x,\xi)\}=0\,.

(4) However, one cannot think of
W[A]
as a distribution function as in the kinetic theory of gases. Indeed

0\leq A=A^{*}\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ does not imply }W[A]\geq 0\,.

To see this, compute
W[|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|](0,0)
where
\psi(x)=\sqrt{2}\pi^{-1/4}xe^{-x^{2}/2}
for
x\in\mathbf{R}
.

(5) The relation between the Wigner and the Husimi transform is given by the following formula: for each
A\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})
, one has

\mathcal{H}[A](x,\xi)=\exp(\tfrac{{\hbar}}{4}{\Delta}_{x,\xi})W[A](x,\xi)\,.

(6) Let
\psi\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R})
satisfy
\|\psi\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R})}=1
and
W[|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|]\geq 0
. Prove that there exist
q_{0},p_{0}\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
and
u\in\mathbf{C}
with

|u|=1
such that
\psi=u|q_{0},p_{0}\rangle
. (Hint: prove that

F(z):=\int_{\mathbf{R}}\psi(x)e^{-\frac{1}{2}x^{2}-zx}dx

defines an entire function on
\mathbf{C}
, that

0<|F(z)|^{2}\leq Ce^{\Re(z)^{2}}\,,\qquad z\in\mathbf{C}\,,

for some constant
C>0
. Conclude by Hadamard’s theorem
11
11

1
]
.
that
F(z)=e^{g(z)}
where

is a polynomial of degree
2
.)

1.4.6.
Explicit Computations/Estimates

We have gathered together in this section several useful explicit computations, or bounds, on the “pseudometric”
\mathfrak{d}
.

Theorem 1.

(1) For all
f,g
probability densities on
\mathbf{R}^{2d}
with finite 2nd order moments,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{T}[f],\mathcal{T}[g])^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\!\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,g)^{2}\!+\!2d{\hbar},\qquad

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{T}[f],\mathcal{T}[f])=\sqrt{2d{\hbar}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(f,\mathcal{T}[g])^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\!\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,g)^{2}\!+\!d{\hbar},\qquad

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(f,\mathcal{T}[f])=\sqrt{d{\hbar}}\,.

(2) For all
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
and all probability density
f
on
\mathbf{R}^{2d}
with finite 2nd order moments,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[R],\mathcal{H}[S])^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{d}(R,S)^{2}+2d{\hbar}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,\mathcal{H}[R])^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathfrak{d}(f,R)^{2}+d{\hbar}\,.

(3) Moreover, if
\text{rank}(R)=1
, then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(R,S)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}((R\otimes S)^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(R\otimes S)^{\frac{1}{2}})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,,\quad\text{ and }

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(f,R)

\displaystyle=\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}f(x,\xi)\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(R^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)R^{\frac{1}{2}})dxd\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

Remark.
The second inequality in (1) can be recast as

\mathfrak{d}(f,\mathcal{T}[g])^{2}\leq\mathfrak{d}(f,g)^{2}+\mathfrak{d}(g,\mathcal{T}[g])^{2}

since

\mathfrak{d}(g,\mathcal{T}[g])=\sqrt{d{\hbar}}=\min\mathfrak{d}\,.

This suggests that

(1) “the segment
[g,\mathcal{T}[g]]
is orthogonal to the set of classical densities”, and

(2) the “angle”
{\theta}
between the “segment”
[g,f]
and the “segment”
[g,\mathcal{T}(g)]
in
\mathfrak{D}_{2}
is acute.

Therefore, one could think of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
as a limit set (for the classical limit
{\hbar}\to 0
) — or boundary — of
\mathfrak{D}
, and that the set
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
of quantum densities
lies on the “concave” side of the set of classical densities.

Figure 3.
A geometric interpretation of Theorem 1 (1).

Proof of Theorem 1.

Proof of (1).
Let
{\nabla}\Phi
(with
\Phi
convex) be the Brenier map pushing
f
to
g
. The optimal coupling of
f
and
g
for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is

\Lambda:=f(x,\xi){\delta}_{{\nabla}\Phi(x,\xi)}(dyd\eta)dxd\xi\,.

Hence

\mathcal{T}[\Lambda]\!\in\!\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{T}[f],\!\mathcal{T}[g])\quad\text{ and }\quad(x,\xi)\!\mapsto\!f(x,\xi)\mathcal{T}[{\delta}_{{\nabla}\Phi(x,\xi)}]\!\in\!\mathcal{C}(f,\!\mathcal{T}[g])\,.

On the other hand (see Appendix B, and especially formulas (52)-(53), in
[
37
]
)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}[C_{\hbar}](q,p,q^{\prime},p^{\prime})

\displaystyle=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-2d}(|q-q^{\prime}|^{2}+|p-p^{\prime}|^{2}+2d{\hbar})\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}[c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)](q,p)

\displaystyle=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}(|x-q|^{2}+|\xi-p|^{2}+d{\hbar})\,.

Therefore

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{T}[f],\mathcal{T}[g])^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(\mathcal{T}[\Lambda]^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}\mathcal{T}[\Lambda]^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{4d}}\underbrace{(|q-q^{\prime}|^{2}+|p-p^{\prime}|^{2}+2d{\hbar})}_{=(2\pi{\hbar})^{2d}\overline{\mathcal{H}[C_{\hbar}](q,p)}}\Lambda(dqdpdq^{\prime}dp^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,g)^{2}+2d{\hbar}\,,

and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(f,\mathcal{T}[g])^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathcal{T}[{\delta}_{{\nabla}\Phi(x,\xi)}]^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)\mathcal{T}[{\delta}_{{\nabla}\Phi(x,\xi)}]^{\frac{1}{2}})f(x,\xi)dxd\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\underbrace{(|(x,\xi)-{\nabla}\Phi(x,\xi)|^{2}+d{\hbar})}_{=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\overline{\mathcal{H}[c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)]({\nabla}\Phi(x,\xi))}}f(x,\xi)dxd\xi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,g)^{2}+d{\hbar}\,.

Proof of (2).
Pick sequences
