a_{n},b_{n}\in C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{2d};\mathbf{R})
such that

\displaystyle a_{n}(q,p)+b_{n}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\leq|q-q^{\prime}|^{2}+|p-p^{\prime}|^{2}\,,\quad\text{ and}

\displaystyle\underbrace{\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}a_{n}(q,p)\mathcal{H}[R](q,p)dqdp}_{=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathcal{T}[a_{n}]R)}+\underbrace{\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}b_{n}(q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\mathcal{H}[S](q^{\prime},p^{\prime})dq^{\prime}dp^{\prime}}_{=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathcal{T}[b_{n}]S)}

\displaystyle\to\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[R],\mathcal{H}[S])^{2}

as
n\to\infty
. That such sequences exist is a consequence of the Kantorovich duality formula for the Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{2}
.

On the other hand, for each
T\in\mathcal{C}(R,S)
, one has

\displaystyle(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathcal{T}[a_{n}]R)+(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathcal{T}[b_{n}]S)

\displaystyle=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{T}[a_{n}]\otimes I+I\otimes\mathcal{T}[b_{n}])T^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=(2\pi{\hbar})^{-2d}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{T}[a_{n}\otimes 1+1\otimes b_{n}]T^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle\leq(2\pi{\hbar})^{-2d}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathcal{T}[|q\!-\!q^{\prime}|^{2}\!+\!|p\!-\!p^{\prime}|^{2}]T^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle.

Now, one has (see the basic properties of the Toeplitz map and Quiz 8 above)

\mathcal{T}[|q\!-\!q^{\prime}|^{2}\!+\!|p\!-\!p^{\prime}|^{2}]=(2\pi{\hbar})^{2d}(C_{\hbar}+2d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}})\,.

Thus, for all
T\in\mathcal{C}(R,S)
, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[R],\mathcal{H}[S])^{2}=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(2\pi{\hbar})^{-d}\left(\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathcal{T}[a_{n}]R)+\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\mathcal{T}[b_{n}]S)\right)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}(C_{\hbar}+2d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}})T^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}T^{\frac{1}{2}})+2d{\hbar}\,.

Minimizing the r.h.s. in
T\in\mathcal{C}(R,S)
leads to

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[R],\mathcal{H}[S])^{2}\leq\mathfrak{d}(R,S)^{2}+2d{\hbar}\,.

Proof of (3).
We begin with a question of a rather fundamental nature in quantum mechanics.

Question.
What is the structure of couplings for rank-
1
density operators?

This question is answered by the following lemma.

Lemma 2.
Let
R\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
. Then

\text{rank}(R)=1\implies\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&\mathcal{C}(f,R)=\{fR\}\,,\quad f\!\in\!\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\!\times\!\mathbf{R}^{d})\,,\\
&\mathcal{C}(R,S)=\{R\otimes S\}\,,\qquad S\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Obviously Lemma 2 implies statement (3) in Theorem 1.

\Box

Proof of Lemma 2.
Since
\text{rank}(R)=1
, it is of the form
R=|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|
, with
\|\phi\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
. We shall prove the second statement in the lemma. Let
Q\in\mathcal{C}(R,S)
. Then

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}(((I-R)\otimes I)Q((I-R)\otimes I))=\operatorname{trace}(Q((I-R)^{2}\otimes I))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{trace}(Q((I-R)\otimes I))=\operatorname{trace}(R(I-R))=0

\displaystyle,

Since

((I-R)\otimes I)Q((I-R)\otimes I)\geq 0\,,

one has

((I-R)\otimes I)Q((I-R)\otimes I)=0\,.

Next, we deduce from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality that

\displaystyle|\langle\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}|(R\otimes I)Q((I-R)\otimes I)\psi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\psi^{\prime}_{2}\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\langle\psi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\psi^{\prime}_{2}|((I-R)\otimes I)Q((I-R)\otimes I)\psi^{\prime}_{1}\otimes\psi^{\prime}_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\times\langle\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}|(R\otimes I)Q(R\otimes I)\psi_{1}\otimes\psi_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle.

Hence

\displaystyle(R\otimes I)Q((I-R)\otimes I)=0

\displaystyle=((R\otimes I)Q((I-R)\otimes I))^{*}

\displaystyle=((I-R)\otimes I)Q(R\otimes I)\,,

Since we already know that
((I-R)\otimes I)Q((I-R)\otimes I)=0
, this implies that

Q=(R\otimes I)Q(R\otimes I)\,.

Therefore
Q=R\otimes T
, where

\langle\psi|T|\psi^{\prime}\rangle:=\langle\phi\otimes\psi|Q|\phi\otimes\psi^{\prime}\rangle\,.

Finally,
T=S
, since, for all
A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
, one has

\operatorname{trace}(SA)=\operatorname{trace}(Q(I\otimes A))=\operatorname{trace}((R\otimes T)(I\otimes A))=\operatorname{trace}(TA)\,.

This proves the second statement in the lemma.

\Box

Quiz 12.
Complete the proof of Lemma 2: prove that
\mathcal{C}(f,R)=\{f\otimes_{\mathbf{C}}R\}
, in the case where
f
is a probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
and

R
is a rank-
1
density operator on
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

Remark.
It is well known that, for all
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
and all
(q,p)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, the set of couplings of
\mu
with
{\delta}_{(q,p)}
contains
only one element:

\mathcal{C}(\mu,{\delta}_{(q,p)})=\{\mu\otimes{\delta}_{(q,p)}\}\,.

Lemma 2 suggests that
all
rank-
1
density operators are quantum analogues of the Dirac mass in phase space. Thus the Schrödinger equation governing
the evolution of the wave function
\psi(t,x)
— or the von Neumann equation specialized to
|\psi(t,\cdot)\rangle\langle\psi(t,\cdot)|
— is the quantum analogue of Newton’s
second law of motion in classical mechanics, which can be viewed as the equation governing
{\delta}_{(q(t),p(t))}
, where
q(t)
and
p(t)
are respectively the position
and the momentum of a moving classical particle. This analogy also explains why the wave function is a purely quantum object, which has no classical analogue.
Indeed, if a classical analogue of the wave function existed, it could be thought of as a “square root” of the phase space Dirac measure
{\delta}_{(q(t),p(t))}
.

Quiz 13: another proof of Theorem 1 (2).

(1) Prove that

\mathcal{T}[|q-q^{\prime}|^{2}+|p-p^{\prime}|^{2}]=(2\pi{\hbar})^{2d}(C_{\hbar}+2d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}})\,.

(2) For each
T\in\mathcal{C}(R,S)
, prove that

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}\left(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}T^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)+2d{\hbar}\geq

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}\left(T\mathcal{T}\left[\tfrac{|q-q^{\prime}|^{2}+|p-p^{\prime}|^{2}}{1+{\epsilon}|q-q^{\prime}|^{2}+{\epsilon}|p-p^{\prime}|^{2}}\right]\right)\

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{4d}}\mathcal{H}[T](q,p,q^{\prime},p^{\prime})\tfrac{|q-q^{\prime}|^{2}+|p-p^{\prime}|^{2}}{1+{\epsilon}|q-q^{\prime}|^{2}+{\epsilon}|p-p^{\prime}|^{2}}dqdpdq^{\prime}dp^{\prime}\,.

(3) Conclude by monotone convergence in the right-hand side of the inequality above as
{\epsilon}\to 0^{+}
, after observing that
\mathcal{H}[T]
is a coupling of
\mathcal{H}[R]
and
\mathcal{H}[S]
.

1.5.
Quantum Optimal Transport is Cheaper!

To conclude this first lecture, we shall study an example where
\mathfrak{d}
can be computed essentially explicitly, with interesting implications on the structure of optimal
couplings. The material in this section — together with the somewhat provocative title — is taken from
[
19
]
.

We begin with a simple lemma, which can be viewed as an amplification of statement (1) in Theorem 1.

Lemma 3.
For
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
with optimal coupling
\Pi
for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
, one has

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{T}[\rho_{1}],\mathcal{T}[\rho_{2}])^{2}=\mathcal{W}_{2}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})^{2}+2d{\hbar}

\displaystyle\iff\mathcal{T}[\Pi]\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{T}[\rho_{1}],\mathcal{T}[\rho_{2}])\text{ is a quantum optimal coupling for }\mathfrak{d}

\displaystyle.

The proof of this lemma is left to the reader as an easy exercise (see the proof of Theorem 1).

Here is an example of this kind of situation. With
d=1
and
0<a<b
, set

\mu:=\tfrac{1}{2}({\delta}_{(+a,0)}+{\delta}_{(-a,0)})\,\text{ and }\,\nu:=\tfrac{1}{2}({\delta}_{(+b,0)}+{\delta}_{(-b,0)})\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}\times\mathbf{R})\,.

In other words, we have equal masses (1/2), but different positions — since
0<a<b
.

Proposition 4.
One has

\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{T}[\mu],\mathcal{T}[\nu])^{2}=\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)^{2}+2{\hbar}\,.

The classical optimal transport in this case is obvious: send mass
1/2
from
-a
to
-b
, and mass
1/2
from
+a
to
+b
.

x_{1}

,

\frac{1}{2}
x_{2}

,

\frac{1}{2}
y_{1}

,

\frac{1}{2}
y_{2}

,

\frac{1}{2}
x_{1}

,

\frac{1-{\epsilon}}{2}
x_{2}

,

\frac{1+{\epsilon}}{2}
y_{1}

,

\frac{1}{2}
y_{2}

,

\frac{1}{2}

Figure 4.
Left: equal masses; Right: unequal mass case

Next we consider the following example, with identical locations, but unequal masses: for
0<{\epsilon}<1
, set

\mu=\tfrac{1}{2}({\delta}_{(+a,0)}+{\delta}_{(-a,0)})\,\text{ and }\,\rho_{\epsilon}=\tfrac{1+{\epsilon}}{2}{\delta}_{(+a,0)}+\tfrac{1-{\epsilon}}{2}{\delta}_{(-a,0)}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}\times\mathbf{R})\,.

Proposition 5.
For each
{\epsilon}\in(0,1)
, one has

\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{T}[\mu],\mathcal{T}[\rho_{\epsilon}])^{2}<\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\rho_{\epsilon})^{2}+2{\hbar}\,.

We shall not prove Propositions 4 and 5, which rely on rather intricate computations, and refer instead the interested reader to the article
[
19
]
. However, we shall discuss both results.

Because of Lemma 3, an optimal coupling for
\mathcal{T}[\mu]
and
\mathcal{T}[\nu]
is
\mathcal{T}[{\lambda}]
, where
{\lambda}
is the optimal coupling of
\mu
and
\nu
, i.e.

{\lambda}:=\tfrac{1}{2}({\delta}_{(+a,0)}\otimes{\delta}_{(+b,0)}+{\delta}_{(-a,0)}\otimes{\delta}_{(-b,0)})\,,

so that

{\lambda}:=\tfrac{1}{2}(|+a,0,+b,0\rangle\langle+a,0,+b,0|+|+a,0,+b,0\rangle\langle+a,0,+b,0|)\,,

with the notation

|q,p,q^{\prime},p^{\prime}\rangle(x,y)=|q,p\rangle\otimes|q^{\prime},p^{\prime}\rangle(x,y)=|q,p\rangle(x)|q^{\prime},p^{\prime}\rangle(y)\,.

In the unequal mass case, it is proved in
[
19
]
that there exists a quantum coupling of the form

\displaystyle T=

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l\in\{\pm\}}\tau_{klkl}|ka,0,la,0\rangle\langle ka,0,la,0|

\displaystyle+\sum_{(k,l)\not=(m,n)\in\{\pm\}}\tau_{klmn}|ka,0,la,0\rangle\langle ma,0,na,0|\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{T}[\mu],\mathcal{T}[\rho_{\epsilon}])\,,

with

\sum_{(k,l)\not=(m,n)\in\{\pm\}}|\tau_{klmn}|^{2}>0\,,

and

\operatorname{trace}(T^{1/2}C_{\hbar}T^{1/2})<\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\rho_{\epsilon})^{2}+2{\hbar}\,.

Clearly, any coupling of
\mu
and
\rho_{\epsilon}
must be of the form

\sum_{k,l\in\{\pm\}}q_{kl}{\delta}_{(ka,0)}\otimes{\delta}_{(la,0)}\,,

and therefore belongs to the
4
-dimensional linear space

\text{span}\{{\delta}_{(\pm a,0)}\otimes{\delta}_{(\pm a,0)}\}\,.

On the contrary, couplings of two rank-
2
operators with

R=\tfrac{1}{2}(|+a,0\rangle\langle+a,0\rangle+|-a,0\rangle\langle-a,0|)

and

S_{\epsilon}=\tfrac{1+{\epsilon}}{2}|+a,0\rangle\langle+a,0|+\tfrac{1-{\epsilon}}{2}|-a,0\rangle\langle-a,0|

with
0<{\epsilon}<1
belong to the
16
-dimensional linear space

\text{span}\{|ka,0,la,0\rangle\langle m,a,0,na,0|\,:\,k,l,m,n\in\{\pm\}\}\,.

Therefore, one could summarize the results in
[
19
]
as follows: since there are more degrees of freedom in the set of quantum couplings than in the set of
classical couplings, it is natural to surmise that quantum optimal transport is cheaper than classical optimal transport, since more couplings are allowed in the quantum
case than in the classical case. However, this simple argument is not sufficient to prove a
strict
inequality as in
[
19
]
. Putting together Proposition 5
and Lemma 3 shows that any optimal coupling for
\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{T}[\mu],\mathcal{T}[\rho_{\epsilon}])
must be of the form

\displaystyle T=

\displaystyle\sum_{k,l\in\{\pm\}}\tau_{klkl}|ka,0,la,0\rangle\langle ka,0,la,0|

\displaystyle+\sum_{(k,l)\not=(m,n)\in\{\pm\}}\tau_{klmn}|ka,0,la,0\rangle\langle ma,0,na,0|\in\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{T}[\mu],\mathcal{T}[\rho_{\epsilon}])\,,

with

\sum_{(k,l)\not=(m,n)\in\{\pm\}}|\tau_{klmn}|^{2}>0\,.

While the term

\sum_{k,l\in\{\pm\}}\tau_{klkl}|ka,0,la,0\rangle\langle ka,0,la,0|

has a classical interpretation, since it is the image of a bounded, positive Radon measure on phase space by the Toeplitz map, the term

\sum_{(k,l)\not=(m,n)\in\{\pm\}}\tau_{klmn}|ka,0,la,0\rangle\langle ma,0,na,0|\not=0

does not have any classical interpretation.

At the time of this writing, the structure of optimal couplings for
\mathfrak{d}(R_{1},R_{2})
with
R_{1},R_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
is not very well understood in general, at variance with the
classical case (see however the discussion of this point in
[
20
]
), which is completely clarified by the Knott-Smith and the Brenier theorems. The very
simple examples discussed in
[
19
]
show that optimal couplings between quantum densities are much more involved than in the classical case.

2.
Lecture II: Applying the Quantum Wasserstein Pseudometric

to Particle Dynamics

In this lecture, we shall discuss several applications of the quantum Wasserstein pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
introduced in Lecture I. These applications include

\bullet
various limits of many-body problems in quantum mechanics (section
2.3
),

\bullet
proofs of the uniform in
{\hbar}
convergence of some numerical schemes for quantum dynamics (section
2.4
), and

\bullet
observation inequalities for the Schrödinger and for the von Neumann equations (section
2.4
).

2.1.
Basics of Quantum Dynamics.

As already explained, one does not need to be an expert in quantum mechanics to read these notes.

However, some familiarity with the most elementary notions of quantum mechanics is required in order to understand the simple ideas behind the computations and the mathematical
apparatus studied in this course. An excellent reference to learn quantum mechanics is
[
10
]
; see also
[
23
]
for a more detailed, yet equally lucid presentation.
The reference
[
44
]
is interesting for mathematicians, but contains few physical explanations, at variance with
[
10
,
23
]
.

2.1.1.
Classical Mechanics

We recall the fundamentals of classical dynamics for a point particle of mass
m
, in Hamiltonian form.

The position of this point particle at time
t
is denoted by
q(t)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
, while its momentum at time
t
is denoted by
p(t)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
.

The
Hamiltonian
for the point particle is the total energy of that particle, expressed in terms of its position
q
and momentum
p
— a word of caveat: it is essential at this point
to use these variables, and not other variables, say for instance the velocity instead of the momentum. For a point particle with mass
m

H(q,p)=\underbrace{|p|^{2}/2m}_{\text{kinetic}}+\underbrace{V(q)}_{\text{potential}}=\text{total energy.}

With this, we can write
Newton’s 2nd law of motion in Hamiltonian form
:

\dot{q}(t)={\partial}H/{\partial}p=p(t)/m\,,\qquad\dot{p}(t)=-{\partial}H/{\partial}q=-{\nabla}V(q(t))\,.

(In fact, the first equation is of a kinematic nature, since it can be viewed as a definition of the momentum
p(t)
in terms of the particle mass
m
and of the velocity
\dot{q}(t)
.
Only the second equation corresponds to Newton’s second law.)

Newton’s second law of motion can be viewed as the governing equation for the special phase space probability measure
{\delta}_{(q(t),p(t))}
. But of course, it is equally interesting
to consider the dynamics of phase space Borel probability measures more general than
\delta_{q(t),p(t)}
.

This is precisely the purpose of the
Liouville equation
: if
f\equiv f(t,x,\xi)
is the probability density of finding the point particle at the position
x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
with momentum

\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
at time
t
, it satisfies the equation

{\partial}_{t}f(t,x,\xi)+\tfrac{1}{m}\xi\cdot{\nabla}_{x}f(t,x,\xi)-{\nabla}V(x)\cdot{\nabla}_{\xi}f(t,x,\xi)=0\,.

This equation can be recast in terms of the
Poisson bracket
:

\{H(x,\xi),f(x,\xi)\}:={\nabla}_{\xi}H(x,\xi)\cdot{\nabla}_{x}f(x,\xi)-{\nabla}_{x}H(x,\xi)\cdot{\nabla}_{\xi}f(x,\xi)\,,

as follows:

{\partial}_{t}f(t,x,\xi)+\{H(x,\xi),f(t,x,\xi)\}=0\,.

Newton’s second law of motion in Hamiltonian form is a system of ODEs to which one can apply the Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem. Specifically, the local existence of a unique solution
of the Cauchy problem for the Hamiltonian formulation of Newton’s second law of motion is implied by the assumption

V\in C^{1,1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})

— meaning that
V\in C^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
{\nabla}V
is Lipschitz continuous on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
. Since

H(q(t),p(t))=H(q(0),p(0))

(the verification of this is left to the reader as an exercise), which corresponds to the conservation of total energy by the dynamics deduced from Newton’s second law of motion,
it is easily seen that all solutions of the Cauchy problem for Newton’s second law of motion are defined for all
t\in\mathbf{R}
under the condition

\lim_{|x|\to+\infty}V(x)=+\infty\,.

(Indeed, in that case, the map
(q,p)\mapsto H(q,p)
is proper on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, i.e. the inverse image of any compact subset of
\mathbf{R}
is compact in
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
;
this implies that the Hamiltonian flow generated by Newton’s second law of motion is global by the most elementary continuation argument for ODEs; see for instance (10.5.5)
in section 5 of chapter X in
[
29
]
.)

2.1.2.
Quantum Mechanics

The state of a quantum particle at time
t
is given by its
wave function
:

\psi\equiv\psi(t,x)\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{C})=:\mathfrak{H}

such that

\|\psi(t,\cdot)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1\,.

In classical mechanics, the Hamiltonian is a function on the phase space
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
; in quantum mechanics, the
quantum Hamiltonian
is an (unbounded)
self-adjoint operator on
\mathfrak{H}
:

\mathbf{H}=-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}_{x}+V(x)=\mathbf{H}^{*}\,.

(Here, the real-valued potential operator
V
is to be understood as a multiplication operator, i.e.
\psi(x)\mapsto V(x)\psi(x)
.)

The reason for considering this specific operator by analogy with the case of a point particle in classical mechanics is

The correspondence principle.

V(q)\to\text{multiplication by }V(x)\text{ and }p_{j}\to-i{\hbar}{\partial}_{q_{j}}={\hbar}D_{q_{j}}\,.

With these mathematical objects, the quantum analogue of Newton’s second law of motion is

The Schrödinger equation.

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}\psi(t,x)=\mathbf{H}\psi(t,x)\,.

Assuming that
\mathbf{H}
is an unbounded self-adjoint operator on
\mathfrak{H}
, it generates a unitary group on
\mathfrak{H}
by Stone’s theorem, denoted by
e^{-it\mathbf{H}/{\hbar}}
, so that

\psi(t,\cdot)=e^{-it\mathbf{H}/{\hbar}}\psi(0,\cdot)\,,\qquad t\in\mathbf{R}\,.

As explained in Lecture I, the quantum analogue of Borel probability measures on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
are density operators on
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. The quantum analogue
of the Liouville equation, defining the dynamics of phase space distribution functions is

The von Neumann equation
for
R(t)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
,

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}R(t)=\underbrace{\mathbf{H}R(t)-R(t)\mathbf{H}}_{=:[\mathbf{H},R(t)]}\,.

One easily checks that, under the assumption that
\mathbf{H}
is an unbounded self-adjoint operator on
\mathfrak{H}
,

R(t)=e^{-it\mathbf{H}/{\hbar}}R(0)e^{it\mathbf{H}/{\hbar}}\,.

For instance, one easily checks that, if
\psi(t,\cdot)
is a solution of the Schrödinger equation, then the rank-
1
density operator
R(t):=|\psi(t,\cdot)\rangle\langle\psi(t,\cdot)|

is a solution of the von Neumann equation.

This brings forward a further analogy in the correspondence principle, between the Poisson bracket
\{f,g\}
of two
C^{1}
functions defined on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, and
the commutator
[A,B]
of two operators on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
:

\{\cdot,\cdot\}\to\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}[\cdot,\cdot]\,.

We conclude this section with a quick discussion of sufficient conditions on the potential
V
under which the quantum Hamiltonian
\mathbf{H}=-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V
is
self-adjoint on
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. As explained above, if
\mathbf{H}
is self-adjoint, by Stone’s theorem, it generates a quantum dynamics, namely the unitary group

e^{-it\mathbf{H}/{\hbar}}
on
\mathfrak{H}
.

Self-adjointness of
\mathbf{H}
.
A first procedure for generating self-adjoint Hamiltonians of the form
-{\Delta}+V
is based on the associated (sesquilinear) quadratic form
b

defined as follows:

(\phi,\psi)\mapsto b(\phi,\psi)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}{\nabla}\overline{\phi(x)}\cdot{\nabla}\psi(x)dx+\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}V(x)\overline{\phi}(x)\psi(x)dx

for all
\phi,\psi\in\mathcal{Q}(b)
, where

\mathcal{Q}(b):=\{\phi\in H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\text{ s.t. }|V|^{1/2}\phi\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\}\,.

Assume that
V
is real-valued, and satisfies the following condition:

V\in L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\,,\quad\text{ and there exists }M\geq 0\text{ s.t. }V(x)\geq-M\text{ for a.e. }x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\,.

Then
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\subset\mathcal{Q}(b)
which is therefore dense in
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Then, the quadratic form
b
is semi-bounded, since

\phi\in\mathcal{Q}(b)\implies b(\phi,\phi)\geq-M\|\phi\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}}\,.

Besides, the quadratic form is
closed
, meaning that, for each sequence
\phi_{n}\in\mathcal{Q}(b)
such that

\phi_{n}\to\phi\text{ in }\mathfrak{H}\text{ as }n\to\infty\,,\quad\text{ and }b(\phi_{n}-\phi_{m},\phi_{n}-\phi_{m})\to 0\text{ as }m,n\to\infty\,,

one has

\phi\in Q(b)\,,\quad\text{ and }b(\phi_{n}-\phi,\phi_{n}-\phi)\to 0\text{ as }n\to\infty\,.

(Indeed, one easily checks that
{\nabla}\phi_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{R}^{d})
, and therefore converges towards an
L^{2}
vector field
\xi
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
; since

{\nabla}\phi_{n}\to{\nabla}\phi
in the sense of distributions on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, one has
\phi\in H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\xi={\nabla}\phi
. Similarly,
(1+M+V)^{1/2}\phi_{n}
is a Cauchy sequence
in
\mathfrak{H}
, and therefore converges to some limit
\ell\in\mathfrak{H}
; then, one easily checks that
\ell/\sqrt{1+M+V}\in\mathfrak{H}
and that
\phi_{n}\to\ell/\sqrt{1+M+V}
as
n\to\infty
, so that

\phi=\ell/\sqrt{1+M+V}
by uniqueness of the limit in
\mathfrak{H}
. Hence
\sqrt{1+M+V}\phi\in\mathfrak{H}
, so that
\phi\in\mathcal{Q}(b)
. The remaining part of the proof is routine, and left to the
reader.) By Theorem VIII.15 of
[
53
]
, there exists an unbounded self-adjoint operator
A
with domain
D(A)\subset\mathcal{Q}(b)
such that

\text{ for all }\phi,\psi\in D(A)\,,\quad b(\phi,\psi)=\langle\psi|A\phi\rangle\,.

Obviously
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\subset D(A)
and
A
coincides with
-{\Delta}+V
on
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

A second procedure is the
Kato-Rellich Theorem
(see Theorem X.12 of
[
54
]
). Assume that
A
is a self-adjoint operator on
\mathfrak{H}
with domain
D(A)
, and
B

is a symmetric
12
12

12

I.e.
\langle\phi|B\psi\rangle=\langle B\phi|\psi\rangle
for all
\phi,\psi\in D(B)
. In other words,
D(B)\subset D(B^{*})
and
B^{*}{\big|}_{D(B)}=B
.
operator on
\mathfrak{H}
with
domain
D(B)
. Assume that
D(A)\subset D(B)
, and that there exists
a\in[0,1)
and
b\geq 0
such that

\|B\phi\|\leq a\|A\phi\|+b\|\phi\|\,,\quad\phi\in D(A)\,.

Then
A+B
is self-adjoint on
D(A)
.

With this result, one can prove that
-{\Delta}+V
is self-adjoint on
D(-{\Delta})=H^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
provided that

V\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})+L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\,.

This result is particularly important in atomic physics, since the Coulomb potential

V(x)=\pm\frac{1}{|x-x_{0}|}=\pm\left(\frac{{\bf 1}_{|x-x_{0}|\leq 1}}{|x-x_{0}|}+\frac{{\bf 1}_{|x-x_{0}|>1}}{|x-x_{0}|}\right)\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})+L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\,.

Here,
V
is the potential energy of an electron at the position
x
interacting with a nucleus located at the position
x_{0}
.

One can also combine these two result to treat the case of
-{\Delta}+V+V_{\infty}
where
V\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})+L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})
, while
V_{0}\geq 0
belongs to
L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbf{R}^{3})

and
V_{0}(x)\to+\infty
as
|x|\to+\infty
(in other words,
V_{0}
is a confining potential).

In all these cases, one can see that much less regularity is required on the potential in order to define the quantum dynamics, than in the case of the classical dynamics.
Perhaps the reason for this difference is that classical mechanics deals with the dynamics of much more singular objects (i.e.
{\delta}_{(q(t),p(t))}
) than the wave function

\psi(t,\cdot)\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
in quantum mechanics.

Quiz 14.
Explain how one can use the Kato-Rellich theorem to prove that

V\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})+L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\implies-{\Delta}+V\text{ is self-adjoint with domain }H^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{3})\,.

(Hint: use the Sobolev embedding.)

In all the situations described above, one starts from the operator
-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V
, which is well defined on
C^{2}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\subset L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Both methods described
above (either the method involving a quadratic form, or the Kato-Rellich Theorem) produce an unbounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, which is therefore densely defined
in
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. The domain of this self-adjoint operator contains
C^{2}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, and this self-adjoint operator coincides with the differential operator
-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V

on
C^{2}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
. This self-adjoint operator is therefore an extension of the differential operator
-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V
, defined on
C^{2}_{c}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\subset L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, and we
shall keep the notation
-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V
to designate this extension.

2.1.3.
The Classical Limit of Quantum Mechanics

Consider a point particle of mass
m
, moving at a speed
v
; its de Broglie wavelength is the ratio
2\pi{\hbar}/mv
. For instance a dust particle of diameter
1\mu
with mass

m=10^{-6}\mu
g moving at speed
1
mm/s, has a de Broglie wavelength
6.6\cdot 10^{-6}
Å
\ll 1\mu
, the size of the dust particle. (This example is taken from chapter I,
complement A in
[
23
]
.) If the de Broglie wavelength of a particle is negligible when compared to its size, or to the typical length scale of the experiment, one expects that
the laws of classical mechanics should be sufficient to describe its behavior.

There are various ways of describing the classical limit of quantum mechanics.

As explained in §6 of
[
48
]
, in the quasi-classical regime, the phase of the wave function of a particle is proportional to the mechanical action of that particle, and the constant
of proportionality is Planck’s constant
{\hbar}
. One could therefore study solutions of the Schrödinger equation

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}\psi=-(\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V)\psi

in the form of a
WKB ansatz
i.e. a formal series

\psi(t,x)=\sum_{n\geq 0}{\hbar}^{n}a_{n}(t,x)e^{iS(t,x)/{\hbar}}\,,\quad S(t,x)\text{ and }a_{n}(t,x)\in\mathbf{R}\,,

where the phase
S
and the amplitude coefficients
a_{0},a_{1},\ldots
are smooth.

Assuming that
a_{0}(t,x)\not=0
for all
(t,x)
, one finds that

(a) the phase
S
is a solution of the
eikonal equation
, that is a Hamilton-Jacobi equation:

{\partial}_{t}S+H({\nabla}_{x}S,x)=0\,;

(b) at leading order, the amplitude
a_{0}
is a solution of the
transport equation
:

{\partial}_{t}a_{0}^{2}+\operatorname{div}_{x}(a_{0}^{2}{\nabla}_{x}S(t,x))=0\,.

However, this description usually fails after some finite time, for the following reason: the graph of the map
x\mapsto{\nabla}_{x}S(t,x)
is the image of the graph of the map

x\mapsto{\nabla}_{x}S(0,\cdot)
by the flow of the classical Hamiltonian
H(x,\xi)
. In general, the graph of
{\nabla}_{x}S(0,\cdot)
becomes folded after some finite time in such
a way that it is no longer the graph of map from
\mathbf{R}^{d}
to
\mathbf{R}^{d}
. The image of these folds by the projection
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\ni(x,\xi)\mapsto x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}
is referred
to as the “caustic”, by analogy with geometric optics. The appearance of caustics is the reason why the WKB ansatz is in general only local in time.

Quiz 15.
Write the WKB ansatz for the free Schrödinger equation

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}\psi(t,x)=-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}\partial_{x}^{2}\psi(t,x)\,,\quad\psi(0,x)=a^{in}(x)e^{iS^{in}(x)/{\hbar}}\,,\qquad x\in\mathbf{R}\,,

where

S^{in}(x)=\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&-x^{1/3}&&\text{ for }x\geq 0\,,\\
&+|x|^{1/3}&&\text{ for }x<0\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Study the dynamics for all
t\in\mathbf{R}
(in the past
t<0
as well as in the future
t>0
), and describe the caustic in this case.

Another approach to the classical limit of quantum mechanics involves the
Wigner Transform
already introduced in Quiz 11. We briefly recall the essentials: start from
some integral operator
R\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
of the form

R\phi(x)=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}r(x,y)\phi(y)dy\,.

For
R=\text{projection on }\mathbf{C}\psi
with
\|\psi\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
, written
R=|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
in Dirac’s notation:

R\phi(x)=\underbrace{\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\overline{\psi(y)}\phi(y)dy\right)}_{=:\langle\psi|\phi\rangle}\psi(x)\implies r(x,y)=\psi(x)\overline{\psi(y)}\,.

We consider its Wigner transform at scale
{\hbar}
, given by the formula

W_{\hbar}[R](q,p):=\tfrac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}r(q+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y,q-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y)e^{ip\cdot y}dy\,.

Some assumptions are needed on the integral kernel
r
for the Wigner transform to make sense. For instance, if
R\in\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})
is a Hilbert-Schmidt operator, its integral
kernel
r
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
, so that the map

(q,y)\mapsto r(q+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y,q-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y)

belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{q}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y})
, since the Jacobian of the transformation

(q,y)\mapsto(q+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y,q-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y)

is
(-{\hbar})^{d}
, which is in particular independent of
(q,y)
. In that case, the Wigner transform
W_{\hbar}[R]
belongs to
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{q}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{p})
by the Plancherel theorem,
as the partial Fourier transform of a square integrable measurable function.

Observe that

R=R^{*}\implies W_{\hbar}[R](q,p)\in\mathbf{R}\,,

but

R\geq 0\text{ does not imply that }W_{\hbar}[R]\geq 0\,.

(For instance, if
\psi
is an odd wave function, then
W_{\hbar}[|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|](0,0)<0
.)

We have seen in Quiz 11 that, if a continuous, time-dependent density operator
R(t)
is a weak solution of the von Neumann equation

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}R(t)=[-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V,R(t)]\,,

its Wigner transform
W_{\hbar}[R(t)]
is a weak solution of the Wigner equation

({\partial}_{t}+q\cdot{\nabla}_{q})W_{\hbar}[R(t)](q,p)+\Theta[V]W_{\hbar}[R(t)](q,p)=0\,,

where
\Theta(V)
is the nonlocal (linear) operator with distribution kernel

\tfrac{1}{(2\pi)^{d}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}\tfrac{1}{i{\hbar}}((V(q+\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y)-V(q-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}y))e^{iy\cdot(p^{\prime}-p)}dy\,.

If
V
is a polynomial of degree
\leq 2
, one easily checks that

\Theta[V]=-{\nabla}V(q)\cdot{\nabla}_{p}\,.

The classical limit of quantum mechanics can be formulated in terms of the Wigner function, in the following manner.

Theorem (Lions-Paul).
Assume that
V
is a real-valued function satisfying the assumptions

\inf_{q\in\mathbf{R}}V(q)>-\infty\,,\,\,V\in C^{1,1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\text{ and }V(q)=O(|q|^{n})\text{ for some }n\geq 0\text{ as }|q|\to\infty\,,

and let

\mathbf{H}=-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V

be the quantum Hamiltonian, with domain
H^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d};V(x)_{+}^{2}dx)
. Let
R^{in}_{\hbar}
be a family of
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
such that

W_{\hbar}[R_{\hbar}^{in}]\to f^{in}\text{ in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\text{ as }{\hbar}\to 0\,.

Then

f^{in}\text{ is a probability density on }\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\,,

and

W_{\hbar}[e^{-it\mathbf{H}/{\hbar}}R_{\hbar}^{in}e^{it\mathbf{H}/{\hbar}}]\to f(t,\cdot,\cdot)\text{ in }\mathcal{S}^{\prime}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\text{ as }{\hbar}\to 0

uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
for each
T>0
, where
f
is the probability density solution to the Liouville equation

{\partial}_{t}f(t,q,p)+\{\tfrac{1}{2m}|p|^{2}+V(q),f(t,q,p)\}=0\,,\qquad f{\big|}_{t=0}=f^{in}\,.

This is Theorem IV.1 in
[
50
]
, and the interested reader is referred to this article for its proof, together with several other interesting examples involving the Wigner
transform.

The connection between these two approaches to the classical limit of quantum mechanics is made clear by the following example, which is left to the reader as an exercise.

Quiz 16.
Consider a
WKB wave function

\psi_{\hbar}^{in}(x)=a^{in}(x)e^{iS^{in}(x)/{\hbar}}\,,

with
\|a^{in}\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
S\in\operatorname{Lip}(\mathbf{R}^{d};\mathbf{R})
. Prove that

W_{\hbar}[|\psi^{in}\rangle\langle\psi^{in}|](q,p)\to|a^{in}(q)|^{2}{\delta}(p-{\nabla}S^{in}(q))

in the sense of tempered distributions as
{\hbar}\to 0
. Explain how the eikonal and the transport equation predicted in statements (a)-(b) above in this section emerge from the
dynamics of
f^{in}
predicted by the Lions-Paul Theorem.

2.2.
Amplification of the Pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
by Hamiltonian Dynamics

This is the core of the present lecture. Our goal is to control amplifications of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
constructed in Lecture I by Hamiltonian dynamics (classical or quantum).

2.2.1.
Pair Dispersion in Classical Mechanics

In this brief section, we seek to compare the evolution of two different initial data, with two different potentials, in classical and in quantum mechanics. (The term “pair
dispersion” is used in Lagrangian fluid mechanics, and is perhaps not so common in the present setting.) The results obtained here should be thought of as a warm-up
— and a motivation — for the study of the dynamical amplification of
\mathfrak{d}
.

Classical dynamics.
We seek to compare two solutions of Newton’s equations,
t\mapsto(X,\Xi)(t)
and
t\mapsto(Y,H)(t)
with two different potentials
V
and
W

belonging to
C^{1,1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
:

\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&\dot{X}\!\!=\tfrac{1}{m}\Xi\,,\\
&\dot{\Xi}=-{\nabla}V(X)\,,\end{aligned}\right.\qquad\qquad\text{ and }\qquad\qquad\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&\dot{Y}=\tfrac{1}{m}H\,,\\
&\dot{H}=-{\nabla}W(Y)\,,\end{aligned}\right.

with initial data

(X,\Xi)(0)=(X^{in},\Xi^{in})\,,\qquad(Y,H)(0)=(Y^{in},H^{in})\,.

Setting
L:=\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)
, we compute

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dt}(|X-Y|^{2}+|\Xi-H|^{2})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{m}(\Xi-H)\cdot(X-Y)-2({\nabla}V(X)-{\nabla}W(Y))\cdot(\Xi-H)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{2}{m}(\Xi-H)\cdot(X-Y)-2({\nabla}V(X)-{\nabla}V(Y))\cdot(\Xi-H)

\displaystyle+2({\nabla}W(Y)-{\nabla}V(Y))\cdot(\Xi-H)

\displaystyle\leq(\tfrac{1}{m}+L)(|X-Y|^{2}+|\Xi-H|^{2})

\displaystyle+2\|{\nabla}(V-W)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}(|\Xi|+|H|)

\displaystyle\leq(\tfrac{1}{m}+L)(|X-Y|^{2}+|\Xi-H|^{2})

\displaystyle+2\|{\nabla}(V-W)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\sqrt{|\Xi|^{2}+2V(X)+2\|V\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}}

\displaystyle+2\|{\nabla}(V-W)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\sqrt{|H|^{2}+2W(Y)+2\|W\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}}

\displaystyle=(\tfrac{1}{m}+L)(|X-Y|^{2}+|\Xi-H|^{2})

\displaystyle+2\|{\nabla}(V-W)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\sqrt{|\Xi^{in}|^{2}+2V(X^{in})+2\|V\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}}

\displaystyle+2\|{\nabla}(V-W)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\sqrt{|H^{in}|^{2}+2W(Y^{in})+2\|W\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}}

\displaystyle,

where the last equality follows from the energy conservation. By Gronwall’s inequality

\displaystyle(|X(t)-Y(t)|^{2}+|\Xi(t)-H(t)|^{2})\leq(|X^{in}-Y^{in})|^{2}+|\Xi^{in}-H^{in}|^{2})e^{(\frac{1}{m}+L)t}

\displaystyle+M\frac{e^{(\frac{1}{m}+L)t}-1}{(\frac{1}{m}+L)}\|{\nabla}(V-W)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle,

where

M:=2\left(\sqrt{|\Xi^{in}|^{2}+2V(X^{in})+2\|V\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}}+\sqrt{|H^{in}|^{2}+2W(Y^{in})+2\|W\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}}\right)\,.

Quantum dynamics.
Consider the quantum dynamics of two different wave functions, driven by two potentials
V
and
W
such that both quantum Hamiltonians

-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V
and
-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+W
have self-adjoint extensions to
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
:

\displaystyle i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}\phi=(-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V)\phi\,,\qquad\phi{\big|}_{t=0}=\phi^{in}\,,

\displaystyle i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}\psi=(-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+W)\psi\,,\qquad\psi{\big|}_{t=0}=\psi^{in}\,.

Thus

{\partial}_{t}(\phi-\psi)=\tfrac{1}{i{\hbar}}(-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V)(\phi-\psi)+\tfrac{1}{i{\hbar}}(V-W)\psi\,,

so that

\displaystyle\|(\phi-\psi)(t,\cdot)\|_{\mathfrak{H}}\leq\|\phi^{in}-\psi^{in}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}+\frac{1}{{\hbar}}\int_{0}^{t}\|V-W\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\|\psi(s,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}ds

\displaystyle=\|\phi^{in}-\psi^{in}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}+\frac{t}{{\hbar}}\|V-W\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle.

If we compare these two estimates, we immediately see the following differences:

(a) the
L^{2}
bound on the difference of wave functions of the quantum particle involves the difference of potentials in sup norm, whereas the difference in the position
and momenta of the classical particle involves the difference of the force fields, i.e. of the gradients of the potentials, in sup norm;

(b) there is no exponential amplification of the
L^{2}
norm of the difference of wave functions in the quantum case, whereas the bound on the difference in positions
and momenta of the classical particle involves an amplification factor
e^{Lt}
, where
L
is the Lipschitz constant of one of the force fields; yet

(c) the
L^{2}
estimate on the difference of wave functions is not uniform in
{\hbar}
as
{\hbar}\to 0^{+}
.

Notice however that the
L^{2}
bound on this difference is uniform in
{\hbar}
in the very special case where both potentials are equal. This is perhaps of limited interest
in the context of numerical analysis, since numerical schemes typically replace the true potential
V
by an approximation thereof.

Also, the
L^{2}
bound on the difference of wave functions is an estimate of the same kind as a bound in trace, or Hilbert-Schmidt norm for the difference of the density
operators, since

\|\,|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|-|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|\,\|^{2}_{2}\leq 2\|\phi-\psi\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}}\|\phi\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}}+2\|\psi\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}}\|\phi-\psi\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}}=4\|\phi-\psi\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}}\,.

We have seen in Lecture I, in the case where
\phi=|q_{1},p_{1}\rangle
and
\psi=|q_{2},p_{2}\rangle
, that the Hilbert-Schmidt norm for the difference between such density
operators converges to
\|{\delta}_{(q_{1},p_{1})}-{\delta}_{(q_{2},p_{2})}\|_{TV}=2
unless
(q_{1},p_{1})=(q_{2},p_{2})
in the limit as
{\hbar}\to 0^{+}
. Therefore, in the case where
V=W
,
the fact that the quantum dynamics is unitary would typically result in the perfectly true, but uninteresting inequality
2\leq 4
in the small
{\hbar}
limit — assuming that

\phi(t,\cdot)=|q_{1},p_{1}\rangle
and
\psi(t,\cdot)=|q_{2},p_{2}\rangle
, corresponding to
\phi(0,\cdot)=U(t)^{*}|q_{1},p_{1}\rangle
and
\psi(0,\cdot)=U(t)^{*}|q_{2},p_{2}\rangle
, with

U(t):=\exp(-it(-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V){\hbar})
.

On the contrary, the pair dispersion estimate in the classical setting can be understood as

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}({\delta}_{(X(t),\Xi(t))},{\delta}_{(Y(t),H(t))})^{2}\leq

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}({\delta}_{(X^{in},\Xi^{in})},{\delta}_{(Y^{in},H^{in})})^{2}e^{(\frac{1}{m}+L)t}

\displaystyle+M\frac{e^{(\frac{1}{m}+L)t}-1}{(\frac{1}{m}+L)}\|{\nabla}(V-W)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}

\displaystyle\,.

It is an easy exercise (left to the reader) to extend this estimate to arbitrary phase space probability measures that are weak solutions of the Liouville equation — not necessary
of the form
{\delta}_{(z(t),\zeta(t))}
, where
t\mapsto(z(t),\zeta(t))
is a solution of Newton’s motion equations.

This suggests the following question: is there an estimate analogous to the classical pair dispersion estimate in the quantum setting for
\mathfrak{d}(R_{1}(t),R_{2}(t))
, where
R_{1}
and

R_{2}
are time-dependent density operators on
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
whose dynamics is governed by the von Neumann equation with two different potentials? Should such an
estimate exist, it would be

\bullet
uniform in
{\hbar}
as
{\hbar}\to 0^{+}
, and

\bullet
should involve the same amplification factor
e^{(\frac{1}{m}+L)t}
as in the classical case, and a similar error term of order

e^{(\frac{1}{m}+L)t}\|{\nabla}(V-W)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\quad\text{ instead of }\quad\frac{1}{{\hbar}}\|V-W\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\,.

Of course, such an estimate, should it exist, would require more regularity on the potentials (typically the same kind of regularity as in the classical setting) than what is needed
for the quantum Hamiltonians

-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V\quad\text{ and }\quad-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+W

to have (unbounded) self-adjoint extensions to
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

2.2.2.
Propagation Estimate for
\mathfrak{d}
.

It will be convenient to consider, instead of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
introduced in the previous lecture, a deformation thereof, henceforth designated by
\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
, where
{\lambda}>0

is the deformation parameter.

Pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
.
For all
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, and all probability density
f
on the phase space
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
with finite 2nd order moment

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f,R)^{2}:=\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{C}(f,R)}\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(x,\xi)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)Q(x,\xi)^{\frac{1}{2}})dxd\xi\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(R,S)^{2}:=\inf_{Q\in\mathcal{C}(R,S)}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(Q^{1/2}C_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}Q^{1/2})\,,

where
c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}
and
C_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}
are the differential operators defined on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
and
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
respectively by the formulas

\displaystyle c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)\phi(y):=

\displaystyle({\lambda}^{2}|x-y|^{2}+|\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}|^{2})\phi(y)\,,

\displaystyle C_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}\Phi(x,y):=

\displaystyle({\lambda}^{2}|x-y|^{2}-{\hbar}^{2}({\nabla}_{x}-{\nabla}_{y})\cdot({\nabla}_{x}-{\nabla}_{y}))\Phi(x,y)\,.

We leave it to the reader as an easy exercise to check that

c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)\geq{\lambda}d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}}\,,\qquad C_{\hbar}\geq 2{\lambda}d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}\,.

Theorem 6.

Assume that
V\in C^{1,1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
satisfies

V(y)\to+\infty\text{ as }|y|\to\infty\,,\quad\text{ and }\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)<\infty\,.

Hence
\mathbf{H}:=-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+
has a self-adjoint extension to
\mathfrak{H}
, and defines a quantum dynamics via the unitary group
U(t):=e^{-it\mathbf{H}/{\hbar}}
.
On the other hand, let
\Phi(t;\cdot,\cdot)
be the flow of the classical Hamiltonian
H(x,\xi):=\tfrac{1}{2m}|\xi|^{2}+V(x)
, which is defined for all
t\in\mathbf{R}
,
since
V
is confining (tends to
+\infty
at infinity). Then, for each
R_{1}^{in},R_{2}^{in}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
and each probability density
f
on the phase
space
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
with finite 2nd order moment, one has

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in}\circ\Phi(-t,\cdot,\cdot),\,U(t)R_{1}^{in}U(t)^{*})\leq

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in},R_{1}^{in})e^{L|t|}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(U(t)R_{1}^{in}U(t)^{*}\!,\!U(t)R_{2}^{in}U(t)^{*}\!)\leq

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(R_{1}^{in},R_{2}^{in})e^{L|t|}\,,

for all
t\in\mathbf{R}
, with

L:=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\frac{{\lambda}}{m}+\frac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}\right)\,.

Proof.

Let
Q^{in}\in\mathcal{C}(f^{in},R_{1}^{in})
; set

Q(t,x,\xi):=U(t)Q^{in}(\Phi(t,x,\xi))U(t)^{*}\,.

One easily checks that

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(t,x,\xi))=

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(U(t)Q^{in}(\Phi(-t,x,\xi))U(t)^{*})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q^{in}(\Phi(-t,x,\xi)))=f^{in}(\Phi(-t,x,\xi))\,,

while

\displaystyle\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}U(t)Q^{in}(\Phi(-t,x,\xi))U(t)^{*}dxd\xi=U(t)\left(\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}Q^{in}(\Phi(-t,x,\xi))dxd\xi\right)U(t)^{*}

\displaystyle=U(t)\left(\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}Q^{in}(X,\Xi)dXd\Xi\right)U(t)^{*}=U(t)R^{in}U(t)^{*}\,,

so that

Q(t,\cdot,\cdot)\in\mathcal{C}(f^{in}\circ\Phi(-t,\cdot,\cdot),U(t)R_{1}^{in}U(t)^{*})\,.

Thus

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in}\circ\Phi(-t,\cdot,\cdot),U(t)R_{1}^{in}U(t)^{*})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2}c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2})dxd\xi

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q^{in}(X,\Xi)^{1/2}U(t)^{*}c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(\Phi(t,X,\Xi)U(t)Q^{in}(X,\Xi)^{1/2})dXd\xi

\displaystyle,

and

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dt}\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2}c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2})dxd\xi

\displaystyle=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2}\{\tfrac{1}{2m}|\xi|^{2}+V(x),c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)\}Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2})dxd\xi

\displaystyle+\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2}\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}[-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V,c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)]Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2})dxd\xi

\displaystyle.

Now, we compute

\displaystyle\{\tfrac{1}{2m}|\xi|^{2}+V(x),c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)\}+\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}[-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V,c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{{\lambda}^{2}}{m}((\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})\cdot(x-y)+(x-y)\cdot(\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}))

\displaystyle-(\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})\cdot({\nabla}V(x)-{\nabla}V(y))-({\nabla}V(x)-{\nabla}V(y))\cdot(\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})

\displaystyle=\tfrac{{\lambda}}{m}((\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})\cdot{\lambda}(x-y)+{\lambda}(x-y)\cdot(\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}))

\displaystyle-\tfrac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}((\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})\cdot{\lambda}\tfrac{{\nabla}V(x)-{\nabla}V(y)}{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}+{\lambda}\tfrac{{\nabla}V(x)-{\nabla}V(y)}{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}\cdot(\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}))

\displaystyle\leq(\tfrac{{\lambda}}{m}+\tfrac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}})({\lambda}^{2}|x-y|+|\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}|^{2})

\displaystyle.

Therefore

\displaystyle\tfrac{d}{dt}\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2}c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2})dxd\xi

\displaystyle\leq(\tfrac{{\lambda}}{m}+\tfrac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}})\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2}c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2})dxd\xi

\displaystyle,

and hence

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in}\circ\Phi(-t,\cdot,\cdot),U(t)R_{1}^{in}U(t)^{*})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2}c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)Q(t,x,\xi)^{1/2})dxd\xi

\displaystyle\leq\exp(|t|(\tfrac{{\lambda}}{m}+\tfrac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}))\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q^{in}(x,\xi)^{1/2}c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)Q^{in}(x,\xi)^{1/2})dxd\xi

\displaystyle.

Choosing a minimizing sequence
Q^{in}_{n}
of elements of
\mathcal{C}(f^{in},R^{in}_{1})
so that

\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q_{n}^{in}(x,\xi)^{1/2}c_{{\lambda},{\hbar}}(x,\xi)Q_{n}^{in}(x,\xi)^{1/2})dxd\xi\to\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in},R^{in}_{1})^{2}

as
n\to\infty
leads to the first inequality.
∎

Quiz 17.
Prove the second inequality in Theorem 6 (the argument follows the one presented above for the first inequality).

Theorem 6 will often be used together with Theorem 1 from Lecture I, in the following manner. For instance, one could start from Toeplitz density operators, for which
the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
is very well-known, by using Theorem 1 (1). Usually, the quantum dynamics fails to preserve the Toeplitz structure of the density operator,
but at time
t
, one can use the lower bound for
\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
deduced from Theorem 1 (2) to compare the Husimi transforms of the (quantum) density operators by means
of the classical Wasserstein
\mathcal{W}_{2}
metric. The resulting statement is as follows.

Corollary 7.
Under the same assumptions as in Theorem 6, let
f^{in}
be a probability density with finite second order moments on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, and
let
g_{1}^{in},g_{2}^{in}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Set
R_{1}^{in}:=\mathcal{T}[g_{1}^{in}]
and
R_{2}^{in}:=\mathcal{T}[g_{2}^{in}]
. Then

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(f^{in}\circ\Phi(-t,\cdot,\cdot),\mathcal{H}[U(t)R^{in}_{1}U(t)^{*}])^{2}\leq

\displaystyle e^{L|t|}\frac{\max(1,{\lambda}^{2})}{\min(1,{\lambda}^{2})}\mathcal{W}_{2}(f^{in},g_{1}^{in})^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(1+{\lambda}^{2})d{\hbar}}{2\min(1,{\lambda}^{2})}(e^{L|t|}+1)\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[U(t)R^{in}_{1}U(t)^{*}],\mathcal{H}[U(t)R^{in}_{2}U(t)^{*}])^{2}\leq

\displaystyle e^{L|t|}\frac{\max(1,{\lambda}^{2})}{\min(1,{\lambda}^{2})}\mathcal{W}_{2}(g_{1}^{in},g_{2}^{in})^{2}

\displaystyle+\frac{(1+{\lambda}^{2})d{\hbar}}{\min(1,{\lambda}^{2})}(e^{L|t|}+1)\,,

where
L:=\tfrac{1}{2}(\tfrac{{\lambda}}{m}+\tfrac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}})
.

Quiz 18.
Prove Corollary 7. (Hint: compute the operators

\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}F(p)|q,p\rangle\langle q,p|dqdp\quad\text{ and }\quad\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}F(q)|q,p\rangle\langle q,p|dqdp\,,

where
F
is a polynomial of degree
2
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
. See Appendix B of
[
37
]
, or the basic properties of the Toeplitz map in Lecture I, and Quiz 8.)

In the remaining part of this lecture, we shall study three (more or less direct) applications of the propagation bound in Theorem 6:

Application 1:
mean-field and classical limits of quantum mechanics (section
2.3
);

Application 2:
time-splitting schemes for quantum mechanics (section
2.4
);

Application 3:
observation inequalities for quantum dynamics (section
2.5
).

2.3.
Mean-Field and Classical Limits of Quantum Mechanics

So far, we have considered the quantum dynamics of a single particle. In this section, we consider the quantum dynamics of
N
identical particles. We are concerned
with two different limiting regimes:

\bullet
the large
N
limit, and

\bullet
the semiclassical regime.

The large
N
limit is of special interest: in that case, the problem is set on a space of large dimension (typically
3N
, the number of position variables for
N
points
in space dimension
3
). Numerical simulations for problems of this type are very often untractable.

For this reason, one seeks to replace the original equation governing the dynamics of
N
-particle systems with “reduced models”, where the space dimension does
not increase with the number of particles considered. The situation studied in this section is summarized by the following diagram.

von Neumann

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}

Hartree

\downarrow

\downarrow

{\hbar\to 0}

\searrow

{\hbar\to 0}

\downarrow

\downarrow

Liouville

\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}

Vlasov

The upper horizontal arrow (quantum mean-field limit) has been proved by Spohn
[
60
]
— see also
[
8
]
— in the case of bounded potentials;
the case of a Coulomb potential was treated subsequently by
[
31
]
— see also
[
7
]
, by the method of the “BBGKY hierarchy” (see for instance

[
35
]
for an elementary introduction to the BBGKY hierarchy). Other approaches involve the method of second quantization
[
56
]
— see also
an original method due to Pickl
[
52
]
, which makes use of some notions originating from second quantization without the full machinery of Fock spaces. Both

[
56
]
and
[
52
]
include a treatment of potentials with a singularity at the origin of the same type as for the Coulomb potential.

The lower horizontal arrow is the mean-field limit in classical mechanics; it has been proved by Braun-Hepp
[
14
]
by using the notion of “Klimontovich”
solutions — i.e. phase-space empirical measures — of the Vlasov equation, with a convergence rate obtained by Dobrushin
[
30
]
, who used optimal transport
distances for the first time on this kind of problem. Dobrushin’s analysis is our first motivation for defining a quantum analogue of the Wasserstein distance, and for the
analysis in the present section.

The limit corresponding to the left vertical arrow follows from the Lions-Paul Theorem mentioned above (Theorem IV.1 in
[
50
]
). The limit corresponding to
the right vertical arrow follows from Theorem IV.2 in
[
50
]
. It should be mentioned that the case of the classical limit of the Hartree equation for singular
potentials including the Coulomb case is treated in Theorem IV.4 of
[
50
]
— one should however keep in mind that this proof assumes that the Wigner
function of the density operator solution of the Hartree equation is assumed to be bounded in
L^{2}
, which excludes the case of pure states, such as solutions of
the Schrödinger equation with WKB initial data, for instance.

Since we are interested in a situation involving two small parameters (specifically
\tfrac{1}{N}
and
\hbar
), it is natural to investigate the uniformity of the mean-field
(large
N
) limit in the semiclassical (small
{\hbar}
) regime. It seems that the first result in that direction is
[
43
]
— see also
[
51
]
.

To conclude this introduction, one should also mention that the mean-field limit in classical mechanics (the lower horizontal arrow) remains an open problem in the
case of the Coulomb potential — see however
[
45
,
46
]
in the case of interactions with a singularity at the origin weaker than that of the
Coulomb potential, and
[
59
]
in the case of the Coulomb potential itself, but for a restricted class of initial data (specifically for monokinetic data). The quantum
analogue of
[
59
]
, i.e. the joint mean-field and classical limit of the
N
-particle quantum dynamics in the case of monokinetic data, which is based on Serfaty’s
remarkable inequality on the Coulomb potential (Proposition 2.3 in
[
59
]
).

2.3.1.
Quantum
N
-Particle Dynamics

The state of a quantum
N
-particle system at time
t
is represented by a density operator
R(t)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H}_{N})
, where
\mathfrak{H}_{N}=\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes N}
is the
N
-particle
Hilbert space. If
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, then it is easily seen that
\mathfrak{H}_{N}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{dN})
.

Since the
N
particles are indistinguishable, their density operator should commute with permutations of the particle labels. More precisely, for each
{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}
,
we define the map

U_{\sigma}:\,\mathfrak{H}_{N}\ni\Psi\equiv\Psi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\mapsto(U_{\sigma}\Psi)\equiv\Psi(x_{{\sigma}^{-1}(1)},\ldots,x_{{\sigma}^{-1}(N)})\in\mathfrak{H}_{N}\,.

One easily checks that the map
{\sigma}\mapsto U_{\sigma}
is a unitary representation of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{N}
on the set of
N
elements in
\mathfrak{H}_{N}
. A density
operator for a system of
N
identical particles should satisfy the relation

U_{\sigma}R(t)U^{*}_{\sigma}=R(t)\,,\qquad{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}\,,\,\,t\in\mathbf{R}\,.

Henceforth, we denote by
\mathcal{D}^{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{N})
the set of density operators on
\mathfrak{H}_{N}
satisfying this symmetry property:

\mathcal{D}^{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{N}):=\{R\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H}_{N})\text{ s.t. }U_{\sigma}RU^{*}_{\sigma}=R\text{ for all }{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}\}\,.

Remark.
One should avoid confusing this symmetry, corresponding to indistinguishable particles, with the symmetries corresponding to the Bose-Einstein,
or the Fermi-Dirac statistics. The Bose-Einstein statistics applies to particles with integer spin, referred to as bosons, such as photons,
4
He nuclei (
{\alpha}
particles).
At low temperature, large numbers of bosons can condense in a a single energy state, thereby forming a Bose-Einstein condensate. The Fermi-Dirac statistics applies
to particles. with half-integer spin, referred to as fermions, such as electrons, protons, neutrons,
3
He atoms. Fermions satisfy the Pauli exclusion principle: two
(or more than two) fermions in a given quantum system cannot occupy simultaneously the same quantum state. The difference between bosons and fermions can be
read on their
N
-particle wave functions: for all
{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}
, one has

\displaystyle U_{\sigma}\Psi_{N}=

\displaystyle\Psi_{N}\,,

\displaystyle\text{ if the }N\text{ particles are bosons,}

\displaystyle U_{\sigma}\Psi_{N}=

\displaystyle(-1)^{\text{sign}({\sigma})}\Psi_{N}\,,

\displaystyle\text{ if the }N\text{ particles are fermions.}

Notice that, in both cases, one has

U_{\sigma}|\Psi_{N}\rangle\langle\Psi_{N}|U^{*}_{\sigma}=|U_{\sigma}\Psi_{N}\rangle\langle U_{\sigma}\Psi_{N}|=|\Psi_{N}\rangle\langle\Psi_{N}|\,,\qquad{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}\,,

so that the density operator
|\Psi_{N}\rangle\langle\Psi_{N}|\in\mathcal{D}^{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{N})
.

The quantum
N
-particle Hamiltonian
is the unbounded operator on the
N
-particle Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}_{N}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{dN})

\sum_{k=1}^{N}\!-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}_{x_{k}}+\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq N}V(x_{k}\!-\!x_{l})\,.

Of course, it is assumed that
N\geq 2
.

Since the
N
particles are identical, the total mass of the system is
M=Nm
, so that the “energy per particle” is

\tfrac{1}{N}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}\!-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}_{x_{k}}+\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq N}V(x_{k}\!-\!x_{l})\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\!-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2M}{\Delta}_{x_{k}}+\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq N}V(x_{k}\!-\!x_{l})\,.

Henceforth we set
M=1
without loss of generality, and consider as the quantum Hamiltonian the energy per particle, i.e.

\mathbf{H}_{N}:=\sum_{k=1}^{N}\!-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2}{\Delta}_{x_{k}}+\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq k<l\leq N}V(x_{k}\!-\!x_{l})\,.

We shall adopt the following
assumptions on the interaction potential:

V\in C^{1,1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\quad\text{ with }V^{-}\in L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\quad\text{ and }\quad V(z)=V(-z)\in\mathbf{R}\,.

Hence

\mathbf{H}_{N}\text{ is self-adjoint on }\mathfrak{H}_{N}\text{ with domain }\operatorname{Dom}(\mathbf{H}_{N})\supset H^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{dN})\,.

By Stone’s theorem, the quantum Hamiltonian
\mathbf{H}_{N}
generates a unitary group
\mathcal{U}_{N}(t):=e^{-it\mathbf{H}_{N}/{\hbar}}
on
\mathfrak{H}_{N}
.

The operator
R_{{\hbar},N}(t)=\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)R_{N}^{in}\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}
solves
the
N
-particle von Neumann equation

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}R_{{\hbar},N}(t)=[\mathbf{H}_{N},R_{{\hbar},N}(t)]\,,\qquad R_{{\hbar},N}(0)=R_{N}^{in}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H}_{N})\,.

If
R_{N}^{in}
is a pure state, meaning that
R_{N}^{in}=|\Psi_{N}^{in}\rangle\langle\Psi_{N}^{in}|
, then

R_{{\hbar},N}(t)=|\Psi_{{\hbar},N}(t)\rangle\langle\Psi_{{\hbar},N}(t)|

where
\Psi_{{\hbar},N}(t,\cdot):=\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\Psi_{N}^{in}
is the solution of the
N
-particle Schrödinger equation

i{\hbar}\Psi_{{\hbar},N}(t,\cdot)=\mathbf{H}_{N}\Psi_{{\hbar},N}(t,\cdot)\,,\qquad\Psi_{{\hbar},N}(0,\cdot)=\Psi_{N}^{in}\,.

We leave it to the reader, as an easy exercise, to check that

U_{\sigma}\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)=\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)U_{\sigma}\quad\text{ for all }{\sigma}\in\mathfrak{S}_{N}\text{ and all }t\in\mathbf{R}\,.

Therefore

R_{N}^{in}\in\mathcal{D}^{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{N})\implies R_{{\hbar},N}(t)=\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)R_{N}^{in}\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}\in\mathcal{D}^{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{N})\,\quad\text{ for all }t\in\mathbf{R}\,.

Henceforth, we are interested in situations where
N\gg 1
(mean-field regime) and
{\hbar}\ll 1
(semiclassical regime).

2.3.2.
Mean-Field Equations

The purpose of the mean-field limit is to replace the description of a
N
-particle system by the quantum Hamiltonian
\mathbf{H}_{N}
— involving functions of
N
variable in

L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{dN})
, therefore functions of
dN\gg 1
variables, by an equation, or a system of equations posed on the single-particle phase space
\mathfrak{H}
instead of
\mathfrak{H}_{N}
.
The physical idea leading to these mean-field equations can be expressed as follows: one seeks to write an equation governing the evolution of a single, “typical” particle.
This typical particle is driven by the interaction with the
N-1
other particles, approximated as follows: call
\rho(t,x)
the single-particle
density function
, i.e.

\rho(t,x)=r(t,x,x)

where
r(t,x,y)
is an integral kernel of
R(t)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
, the density operator of the typical, single particle, such that
z\mapsto r(t,x+z,x)
belongs to
C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{d};L^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d}))

for each
t\in\mathbf{R}
(see Lecture I, Quiz 4). Then, one expects that

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{1\leq j\leq N}V(x_{j}-y)\simeq\int_{\mathbf{R}^{d}}V(x-y)\rho(t,x)dx=

\displaystyle(V\star\rho(t,\cdot))(y)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}(V(\cdot-y)R(t))=:V_{R(t)}(y)

as
N\to\infty
. This time-dependent potential
V_{R(t)}
is usually referred to as the mean-field, self-consistent potential. Then one can write the equation governing
the (quantum) evolution of
R(t)
: this is a von Neumann equation where the potential is the mean-field potential, i.e.

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}R(t)=[-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}+V_{R(t)},R(t)]\,.

This is a quantum dynamical equation, referred to as the (time-dependent)
Hartree equation
, set on the single-particle Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, instead of the
N
-particle
Hilbert space
\mathfrak{H}_{N}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{dN})
. The advantage of this description is obvious: one has to manipulate wave functions depending on
d
space variables (
d\leq 3
in practice) instead
of
Nd
space variables, which is untractable. The drawback is that the mean-field equation is nonlinear (but the nonlinearity is relatively mild since it involves a convolution), and that it is
only an approximation of the true,
N
-particle dynamics.

In the case of pure states, the (time-dependent) Hartree equation takes the form

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}\psi(t,x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}_{x}\psi(t,x)+\psi(t,x)V\star_{x}|\psi|^{2}(t,x)\,,\quad x\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\,,

and
R(t)=|\psi(t,\cdot)\rangle\langle\psi(t,\cdot)|
.

In the classical setting, the idea is the same: the classical mean-field potential is defined in terms of the single-particle distribution function
f\equiv f(t,x,\xi)
by the formula

V_{f}(t,x):=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}V(x-y)f(t,y,\eta)dyd\eta=(V\star f(t,\cdot,\cdot))(x)\,.

Then, the mean-field equation, known as the
Vlasov equation
is written in terms of the mean-field Hamiltonian with the usual single particle Poisson bracket:

{\partial}_{t}f(t,x,\xi)+\underbrace{\xi\cdot{\nabla}_{x}f(t,x,\xi)-{\nabla}_{x}V_{f}(t,x)\cdot{\nabla}_{\xi}f(t,x,\xi)}_{=\{\frac{1}{2}|\xi|^{2}+V_{f}(t,x),f(t,x,\xi)\}}=0\,.

2.3.3.
Uniform in
{\hbar}
Error Bounds for the Quantum Mean-Field Limit

In this section, we present error bounds comparing the solution of the
N
-particle von Neumann equation and the solution of the quantum (Hartree), or the classical (Vlasov)
mean-field equations.

We begin with the error bound for the quantum mean-field limit (from the
N
-particle von Neumann equation to the Hartree equation). This is the upper horizontal arrow in the
diagram above.

Theorem 8.
Assume that
V\in C^{1,1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is a real-valued function such that

V(z)=V(-z)\geq-M\quad\text{ for some }M>0\,,

and set

L:=2(1+4\text{Lip}({\nabla}V)^{2})\,.

Choose an initial (
1
-particle) distribution function
f^{in}
such that
f^{in}dxd\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{2d})
. Set the Hartree initial data to be
R^{in}=\mathcal{T}[f^{in}]
, and the
N
-particle
initial data to be
R_{N}^{in}=(R^{in})^{\otimes N}=\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]
. Let
t\mapsto R(t)
be the solution of the time-dependent Hartree equation with initial data
R^{in}
,
while
t\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}
is the solution of the
N
-particle von Neumann equation with initial data
R_{N}^{in}
.

Then, for each
t>0
,

\frac{\mathfrak{d}\left(R(t)^{\otimes N},\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}\right)^{2}}{N}\leq 2d{\hbar}e^{Lt}+\frac{8\|{\nabla}V\|_{L^{\infty}}}{N\!-\!1}\frac{e^{Lt}\!-\!1}{L}\,.

Next we study the error bound for the joint mean-field and classical limits (from the
N
-particle von Neumann equation to the Vlasov equation). This is the diagonal arrow in the
diagram above.

Theorem 9.
Under the same assumptions as in Theorem 8, choose an initial (
1
-particle) distribution function
f^{in}
such that
f^{in}dxd\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{2d})
. Set the

N
-particle initial data to be
R_{N}^{in}=\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]
. Let
t\mapsto f(t,\cdot,\cdot)
be the solution of the Vlasov equation with initial data
f^{in}
, while the solution
of the
N
-particle von Neumann equation with initial data
R_{N}^{in}
is
t\mapsto\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}
.

Then, for each
t>0
,

\frac{\mathfrak{d}\left(f(t,\cdot,\cdot))^{\otimes N},\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}\right)^{2}}{N}\leq d{\hbar}e^{Lt}+\frac{8\|{\nabla}V\|_{L^{\infty}}}{N\!-\!1}\frac{e^{Lt}\!-\!1}{L}\,.

Several comments are in order before going further.

Remarks.

(1) In both Theorems 8 and 9, we have restricted our attention to Toeplitz initial density operators. The reason for using such well-prepared initial data comes from statement (1)
in Theorem 1: if
T

is a Toeplitz operator in
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, then

\mathfrak{d}(T,T)=\sqrt{2d{\hbar}}=\min_{R\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})}\mathfrak{d}(R,R)\,.

Similarly, if
f
is the symbol of
T
— i.e. if
T=\mathcal{T}[f]
— then

\mathfrak{d}(f,T)=\sqrt{d{\hbar}}=\min_{g\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{2d})\atop R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})}\mathfrak{d}(g,R)\,.

Therefore, for this choice of initial data, the first terms in the right-hand side of the upper bounds in both theorems are as small as possible.

(2) The reason for considering the expressions

\frac{\mathfrak{d}\left(R(t)^{\otimes N},\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}\right)^{2}}{N}

and

\frac{\mathfrak{d}\left(f(t,\cdot,\cdot))^{\otimes N},\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}\right)^{2}}{N}\,,

instead of

\mathfrak{d}\left(R(t)^{\otimes N},\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}\right)^{2}

and

\mathfrak{d}\left(f(t,\cdot,\cdot))^{\otimes N},\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}\right)^{2}

comes from the fact that the quantum-to-quantum transport cost and the classical-to-quantum transport cost in
\mathfrak{H}_{N}
are sums of
N
terms, respectively

\sum_{k=1}^{N}I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes C_{\hbar}\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}^{\otimes(N-k)}\,,

and

\sum_{k=1}^{N}I_{\mathfrak{H}}^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes c_{\hbar}(x_{k},\xi_{k})\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}}^{\otimes(N-k)}\,.

(3) For all
R_{N}\in\mathcal{D}^{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{N})
, we consider its
k
-th marginal

R_{N:k}\in\mathcal{D}^{s}(\mathfrak{H}_{k})
defined by the following prescription

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{k}}(R_{N:k}A)=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}(R_{N}(A\otimes I^{N-k}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N-k}}))\,,\quad\text{ for all }A\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H}_{k})\,.

Quiz 19.
Let
R_{N},S_{N}\in\mathcal{D}^{s}_{2}(\mathfrak{H}_{N})
and let
f_{N}
be a symmetric probability density on
(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})^{N}
with finite second order moments.
Prove that

\mathfrak{d}(R_{N:1},S_{N:1})^{2}\leq\ldots\leq\frac{\mathfrak{d}(R_{N:k},S_{N:k})^{2}}{k}\leq\ldots\leq\frac{\mathfrak{d}(R_{N},S_{N})^{2}}{N}\,,\quad 1\leq k\leq N\,,

and that

\mathfrak{d}(f_{N:1},S_{N:1})^{2}\leq\ldots\leq\frac{\mathfrak{d}(f_{N:k},S_{N:k})^{2}}{k}\leq\ldots\leq\frac{\mathfrak{d}(f_{N},S_{N})^{2}}{N}\,,\quad 1\leq k\leq N\,,

where

f_{N:k}(x_{1},\xi_{1},\ldots,x_{k},\xi_{k}):=\int_{(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})^{N-k}}f_{N}(x_{1},\xi_{1},\ldots,x_{N},\xi_{N})dx_{k+1}d\xi_{k+1}\ldots dx_{N}d\xi_{N}\,.

(Hint: use the formulas for the quantum-to-quantum and the classical-to-quantum costs in (2)).

In particular, Theorems 8 and 9 imply the following result on the first marginal of the
N
-particle density operator.

Corollary 10.
Under the same assumptions as in Theorems 8 and 9, one has

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\left(R(t),(\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*})_{:1}\right)^{2}\leq 2d{\hbar}e^{Lt}+\frac{8\|{\nabla}V\|_{L^{\infty}}}{N\!-\!1}\frac{e^{Lt}\!-\!1}{L}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}\left(f(t,\cdot,\cdot)),(\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*})_{:1}\right)^{2}\leq d{\hbar}e^{Lt}+\frac{8\|{\nabla}V\|_{L^{\infty}}}{N\!-\!1}\frac{e^{Lt}\!-\!1}{L}\,.

(4) One can obviously use Theorems 8 and 9, together with Corollary 10 and Theorem 1 (2) in order to compare the Vlasov solution and the Husimi transform of the Hartree
solution to the Husimi transform of the first marginal of the
N
-particle density operator.

Corollary 11.
Under the same assumptions as in Theorems 8 and 9, one has

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(\mathcal{H}[R(t)],\mathcal{H}[(\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*})_{:1}]\right)^{2}\leq 2d{\hbar}(e^{Lt}+1)+\frac{8\|{\nabla}V\|_{L^{\infty}}}{N\!-\!1}\frac{e^{Lt}\!-\!1}{L}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{2}\left(f(t,\cdot,\cdot)),\mathcal{H}[(\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)\mathcal{T}[(f^{in})^{\otimes N}]\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*})_{:1}]\right)^{2}\leq d{\hbar}(e^{Lt}+1)+\frac{8\|{\nabla}V\|_{L^{\infty}}}{N\!-\!1}\frac{e^{Lt}\!-\!1}{L}\,.

(5) Since density operators are characterized by their Husimi transforms (see Quiz 20 below), and since the Husimi transform of a density operator is a probability density,
a natural idea to define optimal transport distances between density operators is to use the formula

d_{ZS}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})=\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[\rho_{1}],\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}])\,,\qquad\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

This approach has been proposed by K. Życzkowski and W. Słomczyński
[
65
]
.

This definition has some advantages over the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
discussed in these lectures

(a) it is a bona fide metric (see quiz below), and

(b) one is always dealing with probability densities, i.e. functions on phase space, which are easier to manipulate than operators.

Similarly, one might prefer considering

\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,\mathcal{H}[R])\quad\text{ instead of }\quad\mathfrak{d}(f,R)

for
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
and
f
a probability density on
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
with finite second order moments. Indeed, with the first quantity, one transforms the density operator

R
into a probability density by means of the Husimi transform, and then compares this probability density with
f
by means of the Wasserstein distance. On the contrary, the
second quantity compares two very different objects (a probability density and a density operator), which is like comparing apples with pears.

Quiz 20: the Husimi transform is one-to-one.
Set
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

(1) Let
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
. Prove that
\mathcal{H}[R]
is a probability density, and compute

\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}(|q|^{2}+|p|^{2})\mathcal{H}[R](q,p)dqdp

in terms of
R
.

(2) Let
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, and assume that
\mathcal{H}[R]=\mathcal{H}[S]
. Prove that
R=S
. (Hint: let
r\equiv r(y,y^{\prime})
be an integral kernel of
R
. Set

J(x,\xi):=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}r(y,y^{\prime})e^{-(|y|^{2}+|y^{\prime}|^{2})/2{\hbar}}e^{x\cdot(y+y^{\prime})-i\xi\cdot(y-y^{\prime})/{\hbar}}dydy^{\prime}.

Prove that
J
extends as a holomorphic function on
\mathbf{C}^{d}\times\mathbf{C}^{d}
, and therefore is uniquely determined by its restriction to
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
. Conclude (a) by finding
the formula relating
\mathcal{H}[R]
to
J
, and (b) by computing the integral kernel
r
of
R
in terms of
J
.)

However, there is a rather heavy price to pay with this approach, which is that the Husimi transform, and therefore
d_{ZS}
is not easy to propagate by usual quantum dynamics.
This is easily explained if one returns to Quiz 11 (3) and (5): if
t\mapsto R(t)
is a time-dependent density operator, solution of the von Neumann equation

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}R(t)=[-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}+V,R(t)]\,,\qquad R(0)=R^{in}\,,

we have seen that its Wigner transform
W_{\hbar}[R(t)]
satisfies the Wigner equation

({\partial}_{t}+\xi\cdot{\nabla}_{x})W_{\hbar}[R(t)](x,\xi)+\Theta[V]W_{\hbar}[R(t)](x,\xi)=0\,,

and that

\mathcal{H}[R(t)]=\exp(\tfrac{{\hbar}}{4}{\Delta}_{x,\xi})W_{\hbar}[R(t)]\,.

Therefore, the evolution of
\mathcal{H}[R(t)]
can be described as follows:

\mathcal{H}[R^{in}]\mapsto W[R^{in}]\mapsto W_{\hbar}[R(t)]\mapsto\mathcal{H}[R(t)]\,.

The second arrow is the value at time
t
of the group generated by the Wigner equation, and the third arrow is
\exp(\tfrac{{\hbar}}{4}{\Delta}_{x,\xi})
. But the first arrow corresponds to
expressing
W_{\hbar}[R^{in}]
in terms of
\mathcal{H}[R^{in}]
by solving

\exp(\tfrac{{\hbar}}{4}{\Delta}_{x,\xi})W_{\hbar}[R^{in}]=\mathcal{H}[R^{in}]\,.

This first step corresponds to inverting the heat flow at time
{\hbar}/4
, hence the difficulty of this problem.

A more thorough discussion of the evolution of the Husimi transform of a solution of the von Neumann equation can be found in
[
5
]
.

This discussion explains one of the advantages of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
over
d_{KS}
, that is the remarkable simplicity of the propagation estimate stated in Theorem 6, and
the fact that the bounds reported in that theorem are exactly the same as the pair dispersion estimate in classical mechanics (with the same potential in both dynamics). In other
words, when compared by means of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
, quantum particles behave as if they were points moving on classical phase-space trajectories. This picture of quantum
dynamics is known to be wrong in general — see for instance Young’s double-slit experiment and its discussion in chapter I, section 2 of
[
23
]
— but turns out to be true as
far as the pseudodistance
\mathfrak{d}
, or its variants
\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
discussed in Theorem 6, are concerned. Accordingly, the assumptions on the potential used in Theorem 6 are those used
in the definition of a classical dynamics by means of the Cauchy-Lipschitz theorem, which require in particular that
V\in C^{1,1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, which are much more restrictive than
the conditions on
V
which imply that
-\frac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V
has a self-adjoint extension as an unbounded operator on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

After this long list of remarks, here is a quick sketch of the proof of Theorems 8. Theorem 9 is proved in essentially the same way, and the necessary modifications are left to the
reader as a (relatively) easy exercise.

Sketch of the proof of Theorem 8.

This proof is split in several steps.

Step 1.
Pick
\mathcal{Q}_{N}^{in}\in\mathcal{C}(R_{N}^{in},(R^{in})^{\otimes N})
and solve for
\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H}_{N}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{N})
the linear von Neumann equation with time-dependent
potential

\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)\!=\!\left[\mathbf{H}_{N}\!\otimes\!I_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}+I_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\otimes\sum_{k=1}^{N}J_{k,N}(-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2}{\Delta}\!+\!V_{R(t)}),\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)\right]\,,\\
&\mathcal{Q}_{N}(0)=\mathcal{Q}_{N}^{in}\,,\end{aligned}\right.

with the notation

J_{k,N}A:=I_{\mathfrak{H}}^{\otimes(k-1)}\otimes A\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}}^{\otimes(N-k)}\,.

One checks, by taking partial traces and using uniqueness for the solution of the von Neumann equation with time-dependent potential, that

\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)\in\mathcal{C}(R_{N}(t),R(t)^{\otimes N})\,,\qquad\text{ for all }t\geq 0\,,

where
R(t)
is the Hartree solution, while

R_{N}(t):=\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)R_{N}^{in}\mathcal{U}_{N}(t)^{*}\,.

Step 2.
Let

D_{N}(t):=\tfrac{1}{N}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{N}}(\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}})\,,

with
C_{\hbar}
defined for all
\Phi\equiv\Phi(X_{N},Y_{N})\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{2dN})
by

C_{\hbar}\Phi\!:\equiv\!\sum_{j=1}^{N}(|x_{j}-y_{j}|^{2}\Phi-{\hbar}^{2}(\operatorname{div}_{x_{j}}\!-\!\operatorname{div}_{y_{j}})(({\nabla}_{x_{j}}\!-\!{\nabla}_{y_{j}})\Phi))(X_{N},Y_{N})\,.

Step 3.
By definition of
D_{N}(t)
, Step 1 shows that

D_{N}(t)\geq\tfrac{1}{N}\mathfrak{d}(R_{N}(t),R(t)^{\otimes N})^{2}\,,\qquad\text{ for all }t\geq 0\,.

Step 4.
On the other hand

\displaystyle i{\hbar}\frac{dD_{N}}{dt}=\tfrac{1}{N}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{N}}(\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}[\mathbf{H}_{N}\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}},C_{\hbar}]\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=1}^{N}\tfrac{1}{N}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{N}}(\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}[I_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\otimes J_{k,N}(-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}+V_{R(t)}),C_{\hbar}]\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle,

and it remains to compute

Z_{N}=-\frac{i}{{\hbar}}\left[\mathbf{H}_{N}\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}+I_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\otimes\sum_{k=1}^{N}J_{k,N}(-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}+V_{R(t)}),C_{\hbar}\right]\,.

Step 5.
Using the notation
A\vee B:=AB+BA
, one finds that

\displaystyle Z_{N}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}(x_{j}-y_{j})\vee(-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x_{j}}+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y_{j}})

\displaystyle+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}(-{\nabla}V(x_{j}-x_{k})+{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{j}))\vee(-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x_{j}}+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y_{j}})

\displaystyle,

and one uses the elementary operator inequality (see Quiz 7 (2))

AB^{*}+BA^{*}\leq AA^{*}+BB^{*}

to prove that

Z_{N}\leq 2C_{\hbar}+\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left|{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{j})-\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\nabla}V(x_{j}-x_{k})\right|^{2}.

Split the summand so as to involve the difference between the
N
-body and the mean-field potentials on the
y_{j}
variables only

\displaystyle Z_{N}\leq

\displaystyle 2C_{\hbar}+2\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left|\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}({\nabla}V(y_{j}-y_{k})-{\nabla}V(x_{j}-x_{k}))\right|^{2}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left|{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{j})-\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\nabla}V(y_{j}-y_{k})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2C_{\hbar}+\tfrac{2}{N}\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)^{2}\underbrace{\sum_{j,k=1}^{N}|(y_{j}-y_{k})-(x_{j}-x_{k})|^{2}}_{\leq 4NC_{\hbar}}

\displaystyle+2\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left|{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{j})-\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\nabla}V(y_{j}-y_{k})\right|^{2}\,.

Step 6.
It remains to bound

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\left(\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left|{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{j})-\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\nabla}V(y_{j}-y_{k})\right|^{2}\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\left(\left|{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{j})-\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\nabla}V(y_{j}-y_{k})\right|^{2}R(t)^{\otimes N}\right)

\displaystyle.
