This is easy since all the potentials considered involve only the variables
y_{j}
for
j=1,\ldots,N
, so that
\mathcal{Q}_{N}(t)
can be replaced with the
factorized
density

R(t)^{\otimes N}
, where
R(t)
is the Hartree solution. Here is the argument.

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\left(\left|{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{1})-\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\nabla}V(y_{1}-y_{k})\right|^{2}R(t)^{\otimes N}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\left(\left|\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}({\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{1})-{\nabla}V(y_{1}-y_{k}))\right|^{2}R(t)^{\otimes N}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}|{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{1})-{\nabla}V(y_{1}-y_{k})|^{2}R(t)^{\otimes N}\right)

\displaystyle+2\sum_{1\leq k<m\leq N}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\left(\tfrac{{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{1})-{\nabla}V(y_{1}-y_{k})}{N}\cdot\tfrac{{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{1})-{\nabla}V(y_{1}-y_{m})}{N}R(t)^{\otimes N}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\left(\sum_{k=1}^{N}|{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{1})-{\nabla}V(y_{1}-y_{k})|^{2}R(t)^{\otimes N}\right)\leq\frac{4}{N}\|{\nabla}V\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}^{2}

\displaystyle,

since

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}}\left(({\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{1})-{\nabla}V(y_{1}-y_{k}))\cdot({\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{1})-{\nabla}V(y_{1}-y_{m}))R(t)^{\otimes N}\right)=0

for all
m>k\geq 1
.

Step 7.
Hence

Z_{N}\leq 2(1+4\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)^{2})C_{\hbar}+2\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left|{\nabla}V_{R(t)}(y_{j})-\tfrac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\nabla}V(x_{j}-x_{k})\right|^{2}\,,

and therefore

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}D_{N}(t)=

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}_{N}\otimes\mathfrak{H}_{N}}(\mathcal{Q}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}}Z_{N}\mathcal{Q}(t)^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2(1+4\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)^{2})D_{N}(t)+\frac{8}{N}\|{\nabla}V\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}^{2}\,.

By Step 3 and the Gronwall inequality, one has

\tfrac{1}{N}\mathfrak{d}(R_{N}(t),R(t)^{\otimes N})^{2}\leq D_{N}(t)\leq D_{N}(0)e^{Lt}+\frac{8}{N}\|{\nabla}V\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\frac{e^{Lt}-1}{L}\,.

This inequality holds for each initial coupling
\mathcal{Q}_{N}^{in}
of
(R^{in})^{\otimes N}
with
R_{N}^{in}
, so that

\tfrac{1}{N}\mathfrak{d}(R_{N}(t),R(t)^{\otimes N})^{2}\leq\tfrac{1}{N}\mathfrak{d}(R_{N}^{in},(R^{in})^{\otimes N})^{2}e^{Lt}+\frac{8}{N}\|{\nabla}V\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})}\frac{e^{Lt}-1}{L}\,.

Specializing this inequality to

R^{in}=\mathcal{T}[f^{in}]\quad\text{ and }\quad R_{N}^{in}=(R^{in})^{\otimes N}

and using Theorem 1 (1) leads to the announced result.
∎

2.4.
Time-Splitting Numerical Schemes for Quantum Mechanics

In this section, we study the numerical error relative to some time-discretizations of quantum dynamical equations.

Consider the
von Neumann equation
with unkown
R(t)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})

i\hbar{\partial}_{t}R=[-\tfrac{1}{2}\hbar^{2}{\Delta}+V,R]\,,\qquad R{\big|}_{t=0}=R^{in}\,,

where the potential
V
is chosen so that the operator

\mathbf{H}_{\hbar}:=-\tfrac{1}{2}\hbar^{2}{\Delta}+V

has a self-adjoint (unbounded) extension to
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

Our purpose is to obtain uniform in
{\hbar}
error bounds for various time-splitting schemes for the von Neumann equation. The simplest example of time-splitting scheme for the
von Neumann equation is as follows:

Time-split Heisenberg equation.
Starting from
R^{0}=R^{in}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
, we define a sequence
R^{m}(t)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
for
m\in\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{N}
by the following formulas:

\displaystyle R^{n+\frac{1}{2}}=

\displaystyle\exp(\tfrac{i\hbar{\Delta}t}{2}{\Delta})R^{n}\exp(-\tfrac{i\hbar{\Delta}t}{2}{\Delta})\,,

\displaystyle R^{n+1}=

\displaystyle\exp(\tfrac{{\Delta}t}{i\hbar}V)R^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\exp(-\tfrac{{\Delta}t}{i\hbar}V)\,.

The analysis of time-splitting schemes like this one has been studied in detail, for instance by
[
27
]
. This particular time-splitting scheme is known as
the
Lie-Trotter splitting method
.

Error bound.
[
27
]
Pick
R^{in}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
such that

\|\langle\hbar D_{x}\rangle R^{in}\langle\hbar D_{x}\rangle\|_{1}\!=:\!M<\infty\qquad\text{ with }\langle\hbar D_{x}\rangle\!:=\!(1\!-\!\hbar^{2}{\Delta}_{x})^{\frac{1}{2}}\,.

Then, for each integer
n\geq 0
, one has

\|R(n{\Delta}t)-R^{n}\|_{1}\leq C(M,\|V\|_{W^{2,\infty}})\tfrac{{\Delta}t}{\hbar}\,.

This error bound is obviously
not uniform
as
\hbar\to 0
. The convergence of this scheme requires that
{\Delta}t\ll\hbar
, which is obviously very costly in the semiclassical
regime, where
{\hbar}\ll 1
.

In the sequel, we seek to obtain an error bound for the Lie-Trotter time-splitting scheme that is uniform in
{\hbar}
by the methodology of
asymptotic preserving (AP)
schemes.
The idea is to use the Descombes-Thalhammer error bound for
{\hbar}\geq 1
, and, in the case where
{\hbar}\ll 1
, to use the same time-splitting scheme for the classical Liouville
equation, which is the classical dynamical equation obtained by passing to the
{\hbar}\to 0
limit in the von Neumann equation, as explained for instance by the Lions-Paul theorem
recalled above. This last error estimate involves comparing the numerical quantum and the classical solutions, and the exact classical and numerical solutions on the other hand.
This leads to two different error bounds depending on
{\hbar}
, and one hopes to get a uniform in
{\hbar}
error estimate by optimizing in
{\hbar}>0
. This is summarized by the diagram
in Figure 5.

The first result on the uniform in
{\hbar}
convergence of the Lie-Trotter method for the von Neumann equation was obtained in
[
6
]
, without any error bound. The goal
of the present section is to provide such uniform in
{\hbar}
error estimates by means of the
\mathfrak{d}
pseudometric.

Figure 5.
The horizontal arrows represent the semiclassical limit
\hbar\ll 1
and the vertical arrows the convergence of the numerical scheme
{\Delta}t\ll 1
.

2.4.1.
Lie-Trotter Time Splitting for the Liouville Equation

As recalled above, the classical dynamical equation corresponding to the von Neumann equation is the
Liouville equation
with unknown
f\equiv f(t,x,\xi)\geq 0
, written as

{\partial}_{t}f(t,x,\xi)+\{H(x,\xi),f(t,x,\xi)\}=0\,,\qquad f{\big|}_{t=0}=f^{in}\,,

where the classical Hamiltonian is

H(x,\xi):=\tfrac{1}{2}|\xi|^{2}+V(x)\,.

We shall assume here that
V\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
, and that
V\geq-M
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
for some constant
M>0
.

This is a first order PDE that is solved by the
method of characteristics
. If one denotes by
\Phi_{t}
the Hamiltonian flow generated by
H
, one finds that

f(t,x,\xi)=f^{in}(\Phi_{-t}(x,\xi))\,,\qquad x,\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\,,\,\,t\in\mathbf{R}\,.

The
Lie-Trotter time-splitting
method for the Liouville equation is

\displaystyle f^{n+\frac{1}{2}}(y,\eta)

\displaystyle=f^{n}\circ K_{-{\Delta}}(y,\eta)\,,

\displaystyle f^{n+1}(y,\eta)

\displaystyle=f^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\circ P_{-{\Delta}t}(y,\eta)\,,

starting from
f^{0}:=f^{in}
, where the maps
K_{-{\Delta}t}
and
P_{-{\Delta}t}
are defined by the following formulas:

K_{t}(y,\eta):=(y+t\eta,\eta)\,,\qquad P_{t}(y,\eta):=(y,\eta-t{\nabla}V(y))\,.

The
error bound for the Lie-Trotter splitting scheme for the Liouville equation
is formulated below in terms of the Wasserstein distance of exponent
2
.
Setting

(X_{t},\Xi_{t}):=\Phi_{t}(x,\xi)\quad\text{ and }\quad(Y_{t},H_{t}):=P_{t}\circ K_{t}(y,\eta)\,,

one finds that

\displaystyle|X_{t}-Y_{t}|^{2}+|\Xi_{t}-H_{t}|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle(|x-y|^{2}+|\xi-\eta|^{2})e^{(2+{\Lambda})|t|}

\displaystyle+\frac{e^{(2+{\Lambda})|t|}-1}{2+{\Lambda}}\tfrac{9}{4}{\Lambda}^{2}(\tfrac{1}{2}+{\Lambda})^{2}t^{2}(1+|y|^{2}+|\eta|^{2})\,,

with

{\Lambda}:=\max(1,E,\|{\nabla}^{2}V\|_{L^{\infty}})\,,\quad E:=|{\nabla}V(0)|\,.

Quiz 21.
Prove the inequality above. (Hint: write a differential system satisfied by
t\mapsto(Y_{t},H_{t})
, and compare the trajectories of that system with
those of the Hamiltonian system corresponding to
H(x,\xi)
by means of the Gronwall inequality.)

The inequality above implies the following error estimate for the Lie-Trotter scheme in the case of the Liouville equation.

Lemma 12.
Let
V\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
satisfy
V\geq-M
for some
M>0
. Assume that
f^{in}
is a probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{2d}
such that

\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})f^{in}(x,\xi)dxd\xi<\infty\,.

Then, for each
{\Delta}t\in(0,1)
and each
n=0,\ldots,[T/{\Delta}t]
, one has

\mathcal{W}_{2}(f^{n},f(n{\Delta}t))\leq C_{T}[{\Lambda},E,f^{in}]{\Delta}t\,,

where
C_{T}[{\Lambda},E,f^{in}]
is a positive constant depending only on the computing time
T
, on the constants
{\Lambda}
and
E
defined above, and on the initial data
f^{in}
.

2.4.2.
Lie-Trotter Time Splitting for the Liouville Equation

At this point, we use Theorem 6 first with
{\lambda}=m=1
and
V=0
, so that

\mathfrak{d}(f^{n}\circ K_{-{\Delta}t},R^{n+\frac{1}{2}})\leq\mathfrak{d}(f^{n},R^{n})e^{\frac{{\Delta}t}{2}}\,.

Next we use Theorem 6, this time with
{\lambda}=1
and
m\to+\infty
, so that

\mathfrak{d}(f^{n+\frac{1}{2}}\circ P_{-{\Delta}t},R^{n+1})\leq\mathfrak{d}(f^{n+\frac{1}{2}},R^{n+\frac{1}{2}})e^{\frac{{\Delta}t}{2}\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}\,.

Hence, for all integer
n\geq 0
, one has

\mathfrak{d}(f^{n},R^{n})\leq\mathfrak{d}(f^{in},R^{in})\exp\left(\tfrac{1}{2}n{\Delta}t(1+\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V))\right)\,.

On the other hand, applying Theorem 6 with
m=1
shows that, for all
t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(f(t,\cdot,\cdot),R(t))=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(f^{in}\circ\Phi_{-t},e^{-it\mathbf{H}_{\hbar}/{\hbar}}R^{in}e^{it\mathbf{H}_{\hbar}/{\hbar}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(f^{in},R^{in})\exp\left(\tfrac{1}{2}t(1+\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V))\right)\,.

Putting together these two bounds, and the error estimate in the previous section, we arrive at the following result.

Theorem 13.
Let
V\in W^{2,\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
satisfy
V\geq-M
for some
M>0
. Let
f^{in}
be a probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
such that

\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}}(|x|^{2}+|\xi|^{2})f^{in}(x,\xi)dxd\xi<\infty\,.

Set
R^{in}=\mathcal{T}[f^{in}]
. Then, the Lie-Trotter splitting scheme for the Heisenberg equation satisfies the uniform as
{\hbar}\to 0
error bound

\mathfrak{d}(R^{n},R(n{\Delta}t))\leq C_{T}[{\Lambda},\|V\|_{W^{2,\infty}},f^{in}]{\Delta}t+2\sqrt{d\hbar}e^{\frac{T}{2}(1+\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V))}\,.

This implies the uniform in
\hbar
convergence rate

\displaystyle\sup_{\max(2\|\phi\|_{L^{\infty}},\operatorname{Lip}(\phi))\leq 1}\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\phi(x,\xi)(\mathcal{H}[R^{n}]-\mathcal{H}[R(n{\Delta}t)])dxd\xi\right|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}_{T}[{\Lambda},\|V\|_{W^{2,\infty}},f^{in}]{\Delta}t^{1/3}

\displaystyle.

The uniform as
{\hbar}\to 0
error estimate follows from the two bounds obtained in this section before Theorem 13, and the error bound for the Lie-Trotter
method for the Liouville equation in the previous section (Lemma 12). One uses also the following triangle inequality, which will be discussed in Lecture III:

\mathfrak{d}(R,S)\leq\mathfrak{d}(R,f)+\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,g)+\mathfrak{d}(g,S)\,.

The uniform in
\hbar
convergence rate follows from optimizing between the uniform as
\hbar\to 0
bound and the Descombes-Thalhammer bound.

There is a similar result with higher order splitting formulas, such as Strang’s splitting scheme, leading to a uniform
O\left({\Delta}t^{2/3}\right)
estimate.
The interested reader is referred to
[
36
]
for these higher order estimates, and for the missing details on the case of the Lie-Trotter splitting
method.

2.5.
Observation Inequalities for Quantum Dynamics

The observation problem for a general PDE can be formulated as follows: let
P(x,{\partial}_{x})
be a (linear) partial differential operator on some open set
{\Omega}\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}
,
and let
{\omega}
be an open subset of
{\Omega}
. Is a solution
u
of the PDE
P(x,{\partial}_{x})u(x)=0
for all
x\in{\Omega}
completely determined by its restriction to
{\omega}
?

Example.
Here is an elementary example. Set
d=2
, and identify
\mathbf{R}^{2}
to
\mathbf{C}
(by sending
(x,y)\in\mathbf{R}^{2}
to
z=x+iy\in\mathbf{C}
). Set

P(x,y,D_{x},D_{y})=\bar{\partial}=\tfrac{1}{2}({\partial}_{x}+i{\partial}_{y})\,,

(the Cauchy-Riemann operator) and set
{\omega}\subset{\Omega}\subset\mathbf{C}
be (nonempty) open sets in
\mathbf{C}
. Any distribution
u
on
{\Omega}
such that
\bar{\partial}u=0
on
{\Omega}
is
a holomorphic function on
{\Omega}
. The restriction of
u
to the connected component of
{\omega}
in
{\Omega}
is uniquely determined by the restriction
u{\big|}_{{\omega}}
. In particular,
if
u{\big|}_{{\omega}}=0
, then
u=0
on
{\Omega}
.

In the context of quantum dynamics, the observation problem can be formulated as follows. Consider the
von Neumann equation
with unkown
t\mapsto R(t)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})

i\hbar{\partial}_{t}R=[-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V,R]\,,\qquad R{\big|}_{t=0}=R^{in}\,.

The real-valued potential
V
is chosen so that the quantum Hamiltonian

\mathbf{H}:=-\tfrac{\hbar^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V

has a self-adjoint (unbounded) extension to
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

Specialists of control usually consider
R(t)=|\psi(t,\cdot)\rangle\langle\psi(t,\cdot)|
with

i{\hbar}{\partial}_{t}\psi(t,x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}_{x}\psi(t,x)+V(x)\psi(t,x)\,.

A class
\mathcal{K}
of solutions
t\mapsto R(t)\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
of the von Neumann equation can be
observed
on a domain
{\Omega}\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}
during time
T
if there exists a
constant
C_{OBS}>0
, which may depend on
\mathcal{K}
, on
T
and on
{\Omega}
, but not on the specific solution
R\in\mathcal{K}
, such that

1(=\|R(t)\|_{1})\leq C_{OBS}\int_{0}^{T}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}({\bf 1}_{\Omega}R(t))dt\quad\text{ for all }R\in\mathcal{K}\,.

The reason for the interest in this notion is explained by the HUM method (Hilbert Uniqueness Method) introduced by J.-L. Lions: if
{\Omega}
and
T
are such that all solutions of the
Schrödinger equations — meaning that
\mathcal{K}=C_{b}(\mathbf{R};L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}))
— can be observed on
(0,T)\times{\Omega}
, one can control the Schrödinger equation by acting only on the
domain
{\Omega}
and on the time interval
(0,T)
via a source term in the Schrödinger equation. (As a matter of fact, Lions’ initial interest was with the wave equation, or with the
equations of linear elasticity
[
49
]
, and the problem was to eliminate vibrations on large solid structures by acting with motors on small regions of the structure; the question
was obviously to find the optimal way of placing the stabilizing motors.)

Quiz 22: The Lions HUM method.
Let
H
be an unbounded self-adjoint operator on
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
— for instance the Hamiltonian
H=-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V
under some
appropriate conditions on the real-valued potential
V
. Consider the control problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&i{\partial}_{t}\phi=H\phi+{\bf 1}_{(0,T)\times{\Omega}}f\,,\\
&\phi{\big|}_{t=T}=0\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Can one drive any solution
\phi\in C_{b}(\mathbf{R};L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}))
to
0
at time
T
by acting on the domain
{\Omega}
only via the control
f
? In other words, is the control operator

\mathcal{C}:\,L^{2}((0,T)\times{\Omega})\ni f\mapsto-i\phi{\big|}_{t=0}\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})

onto?

To answer this question, one considers instead the observation problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&i{\partial}_{t}\psi=H\psi\,,\\
&\psi{\big|}_{t=0}=\psi^{in}\,,\end{aligned}\right.

and the observation operator

\mathcal{O}:\,L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\ni\psi^{in}\mapsto\psi{\big|}_{(0,T)\times{\Omega}}\in L^{2}((0,T)\times{\Omega})\,.

If the full class
C_{b}(\mathbf{R};L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}))
of solutions of the Schrödinger equation is observable on
(0,T)\times{\Omega}
in the sense of the definition above, the operator
\mathcal{O}
is
one-to-one.

(1) Prove that
\mathcal{C}
and
\mathcal{O}
are bounded operators.

(2) Prove that
\mathcal{O}
is the adjoint of
\mathcal{C}
.

(3) Which statement about the control operator can be deduced from the fact that the class
C_{b}(\mathbf{R};L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}))
of solutions of the Schrödinger equation is observable on

(0,T)\times{\Omega}
?

The controllability problem for the wave (or elasticity) equation has been studied by several authors under various specific conditions, until Bardos, Lebeau and Rauch
[
9
]

came up with a very satisfying answer based on the propagation of high frequency waves. As is well known, high frequency waves propagate according to the laws of geometric
optics, and one approach to relating geometric optics to the wave equation is through the consideration of the wave front set in microlocal analysis. (There was a serious additional
difficulty in the Bardos-Lebeau-Rauch paper, namely the fact that they sought to control the solution only at the boundary of the domain, which involved using the Melrose-Sjöstrand
theory of propagation of the wave front set along “broken bicharacteristics”, in other words, optical rays from the theory of geometric optics interacting with the boundary.) However,
the key idea in the Bardos-Lebeau-Rauch approach was a “geometric condition” saying that all the rays of geometric optics should hit the region of control at the boundary at least
once in the time interval
(0,T)
. Under this condition, all solutions of the wave equation can be driven to
0
after time
T
by some appropriate action on the region of control at the
boundary of the domain.

In view of the analogy between geometric optics and the classical limit of quantum mechanics (see for instance §§6 and 46 in
[
48
]
), we shall consider the following geometric
condition, which can be formulated in terms of the trajectories of the classical mechanics for the same potential as in the quantum problem for which the observation problem is posed.

Thus, to the quantum Hamiltonian
\mathbf{H}
above, we associate the classical Hamiltonian

H(x,\xi):=\tfrac{1}{2m}|\xi|^{2}+V(x)

generating a flow
\Phi(t;\cdot,\cdot)=(X(t;\cdot,\cdot),\Xi(t;\cdot,\cdot))
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
defined by the following prescription:

\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&\dot{X}=\tfrac{1}{m}\Xi\,,\qquad\qquad&&X(0;x,\xi)\!=x\,,\\
&\dot{\Xi}=-{\nabla}V(X)\,,&&\,\Xi(0;x,\xi)=\xi\,.\end{aligned}\right.

Bardos-Lebeau-Rauch Geometric Condition.
Let
K\subset\mathbf{R}^{2d}
be compact, consider a domain
{\Omega}\subset\mathbf{R}^{d}
and let
T>0
; the triple
(K,{\Omega},T)
is said to satisfy
the Bardos-Lebeau-Rauch (BLR) geometric condition if

[formula]

\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&\text{for each }(x,\xi)\in K\,,\text{ there exists }\\
&t\!\in\!(0,T)\text{ such that }X(t;x,\xi)\!\in\!{\Omega}.\end{aligned}\right.

Lemma 14.
Assume that
V\in C^{1,1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
is real-valued and satisfies
V\geq-M
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
for some
M>0
. Let
K
be a compact subset of
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, let

{\Omega}
be an open set in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
and let
T>0
. Assume that the triple
(K,{\Omega},T)
satisfy (GC). Then

C[K,{\Omega},T]:=\inf_{(x,\xi)\in K}\int_{0}^{T}{\bf 1}_{\Omega}(X(t;x,\xi))dt>0\,.

Proof.

Since
{\Omega}
is open in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
, its indicator function
{\bf 1}_{\Omega}
is lower semicontinuous (l.s.c.) on
\mathbf{R}^{d}
. By Fatou’s lemma, the function

(x,\xi)\mapsto\int_{0}^{T}{\bf 1}_{\Omega}(X(t;x,\xi))dt\in(0,+\infty)\text{ is l.s.c. on }\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\,.

Since
K
is compact in
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, there exists
(x^{*},\xi^{*})\in K
such that

C[K,{\Omega},T]=\int_{0}^{T}{\bf 1}_{\Omega}(X(t;x^{*},\xi^{*}))dt\,.

By the geometric condition, there exists
t^{*}\in(0,T)
such that

{\bf 1}_{\Omega}(X(t^{*};x^{*},\xi^{*}))=1\,.

Since
t\mapsto{\bf 1}_{\Omega}(X(t;x^{*},\xi^{*}))
is l.s.c. on
(0,T)
, there exists
\eta\in(0,T)
such that

|t-t^{*}|<\eta\implies{\bf 1}_{\Omega}(X(t;x^{*},\xi^{*}))=1\,.

Hence

C[K,{\Omega},T]=\int_{0}^{T}{\bf 1}_{\Omega}(X(t;x^{*},\xi^{*}))dt\geq\eta>0\,.

∎

Figure 6.
The geometric condition in space dimension
d=1
, with
V\equiv 0
. The classical free flow is
\Phi(t;x,\xi)\!:=(X(t;x,\xi),\Xi(t;x,\xi))\!=\!(x+t\xi,\xi)
. The picture
represents the image of the closed phase-space rectangle
K
by the map
(x,\xi)\mapsto\Phi(t;x,\xi)
at time
t=\tfrac{1}{2}
and
t=1
. The interval
{\Omega}
satisfies the
geometric condition with
T=1
, at variance with
{\omega}
. Indeed, phase-space points on the bottom side of
K
stay out of the strip
{\omega}\times\mathbf{R}
for all
t\in[0,1]
.

With the propagation estimate for
\mathfrak{d}
presented in Theorem 6 above, one can formulate a quantitative observation inequality for the von Neumann equation, with
rather explicit observation constants defined in terms of the classical dynamics. The following result has been obtained in
[
41
]
.

Theorem 15.
Let
V\in C^{1,1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
be a real-valued potential satisfying
V\geq-M
for some
M>0
. Let
K
be a compact subset of
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
,
let
{\Omega}
be an open set in
\mathbf{R}^{d}
and let
T>0
. Assume that the triple
(K,{\Omega},T)
satisfy (GC). Then, for all initial density operator
R^{in}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
and all

{\delta}>0
, one has the “observation” inequality on the open set
{\Omega}_{\delta}={\Omega}+B(0,{\delta})

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}({\bf 1}_{\Omega_{\delta}}U(t)R^{in}U(t)^{*})dt\geq

\displaystyle C[K,\Omega,T]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{{\delta}}\inf_{{\lambda}>0}\frac{1}{\l}

\displaystyle\tfrac{\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}T\left(\frac{{\lambda}}{m}+\frac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}\right)\right)-1}{\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\frac{{\lambda}}{m}+\frac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}\right)}\inf_{\operatorname{supp}(f^{in})\subset K}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in},R^{in})\,.

We have put the word observation between quotes in Theorem 15, since the inequality in that theorem is a bona fide observation inequality only if there exists

{\lambda}>0
and a probability density
f^{in}
with support in
K
such that

\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in},R^{in})<C[K,{\Omega},T]\frac{\frac{{\delta}{\lambda}}{2}\left(\frac{{\lambda}}{m}+\frac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}\right)}{\exp\left(\frac{T}{2}\left(\frac{{\lambda}}{m}+\frac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}\right)\right)-1}\,.

Here are two examples where this inequality is known to be true.

Example 1: Toeplitz initial data.
Assume that the initial density operator
R^{in}
is of the form

R^{in}:=\mathcal{T}[\mu^{in}]\,,\qquad\text{ where }\mu^{in}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{2d})\text{ satisfies }\operatorname{supp}(\mu^{in})\subset K\,.

By Theorem 1 (1) (see Lecture I)

{\lambda}d{\hbar}\leq\mathfrak{d}_{\hbar}(f^{in},R^{in})^{2}\leq\max(1,{\lambda}^{2})W_{2}(f^{in},\mu^{in})^{2}+d{\hbar}\,,

so that

\operatorname{supp}(\mu^{in})\subset K\implies\inf_{\operatorname{supp}(f^{in})\subset K}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in},R^{in})=\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{2}({\lambda}^{2}+1)d{\hbar}}\,.

Indeed,

\operatorname{supp}(\mu^{in})\subset K\implies\inf_{\operatorname{supp}(f^{in})\subset K}\mathcal{W}_{2}(f^{in},\mu^{in})=0\,,

since any probability measure
\mu^{in}
supported in
K
is the weak limit of a sequence of absolutely continuous (with respect to the Lebesgue measure of
\mathbf{R}^{2d}
)
probability measures supported in
K
. (To see this, let
\zeta_{\epsilon}
be a regularizing sequence on
\mathbf{R}^{2d}
, and set

f_{\epsilon}(x,\xi):=Z_{\epsilon}^{-1}(\mu^{in}\star\zeta_{\epsilon})(x,\xi){\bf 1}_{K}(x,\xi)\,,\quad\text{ with }\quad Z_{\epsilon}:=\iint_{K}\mu^{in}\star\zeta_{\epsilon}(x,\xi)dxd\xi\,.

Then
f_{\epsilon}
is a probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{2d}
with support in
K
, and one easily check that

f_{\epsilon}\to\mu^{in}\text{ weakly in the sense of probability measures as }{\epsilon}\to 0\,,

and since
\operatorname{supp}(f_{\epsilon})\subset K
for each
{\epsilon}>0
, this implies that
\mathcal{W}_{2}(f_{\epsilon},\mu^{in})\to 0
as
{\epsilon}\to 0^{+}
.)

Example 2: Pure state.
Assume now that
R(t)=|U(t)\psi^{in}\rangle\langle U(t)\psi^{in}|
, where
U(t)=e^{-it\mathbf{H}/{\hbar}}
is the Schrödinger group. (We recall that

\mathbf{H}:=-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V\,,

and the assumptions on
V
imply that
\mathbf{H}
has a self-adjoint extension to
L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.)

Choosing

f^{in}(q,p):=\frac{|\langle q,p|\psi^{in}\rangle|^{2}}{(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}}=\mathcal{H}[|\psi^{in}\rangle\langle\psi^{in}|]

and setting
{\lambda}=1
leads to

\frac{1}{C_{OBS}}=C[K,\Omega,T]\iint_{K}|\langle q,p|\psi^{in}\rangle|^{2}\tfrac{dqdp}{(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}}-D[T,\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)]\frac{\Sigma[\psi^{in}]}{{\delta}}\,,

where

\displaystyle D[T,L]:=

\displaystyle 4\frac{e^{(\frac{1}{m}+L)T/2}-1}{\frac{1}{m}+L}\,,

\displaystyle\Sigma[\psi^{in}]^{2}:=

\displaystyle\langle\psi^{in}|\,|x|^{2}|\psi^{in}\rangle-|\langle\psi^{in}|x|\psi^{in}\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle+\langle\psi^{in}|-{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}_{x}|\psi^{in}\rangle-|\langle\psi^{in}|-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x}|\psi^{in}\rangle|^{2}\,.

Quiz 23.
The missing argument to arrive at the formula for
C_{OBS}
in the pure state case is a good opportunity to revise some elementary facts about
\mathfrak{d}
.

(1) Find
\mathcal{C}(\mathcal{H}[|\psi^{in}\rangle\langle\psi^{in}|],|\psi^{in}\rangle\langle\psi^{in}|)
.

(2) Compute
\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{H}[|\psi^{in}\rangle\langle\psi^{in}|],|\psi^{in}\rangle\langle\psi^{in}|)
. (Hint: observe that
x_{j}
,
-i{\hbar}{\partial}_{x_{j}}
,
x_{j}^{2}
and
-{\hbar}^{2}{\partial}_{x_{j}}^{2}
are Toeplitz operators,
and compute their symbols.)

Here is a sketch of the proof of Theorem 15.

Proof.

Call
f(t,\cdot,\cdot):=f^{in}\circ\Phi(-t;\cdot,\cdot)
and
R(t):=U(t)R^{in}U(t)^{*}
. For each coupling
Q(t)\in\mathcal{C}(f(t,\cdot,\cdot),R(t))
, one has

\displaystyle\left|\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\chi R(t))-\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\chi(x)f(t,x,\xi)dxd\xi\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}((\chi(x)-\chi(y))Q(t,x,\xi)dxd\xi\right|

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{\operatorname{Lip}(\chi)}{{\lambda}}\left(\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}}({\lambda}^{2}|x\!-\!y|^{2}\!+\!|\xi+i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y}|^{2})Q_{t}^{\frac{1}{2}})dxd\xi\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle,

so that, by the propagation estimate in Theorem 6,

\displaystyle\left|\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\chi R(t))-\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\chi(x)f(t,x,\xi)dxd\xi\right|\leq\tfrac{\operatorname{Lip}(\chi)}{{\lambda}}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f(t,\cdot,\cdot),R(t))

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{\operatorname{Lip}(\chi)}{{\lambda}}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in},R^{in})\exp\left(\tfrac{1}{2}t\left(\frac{{\lambda}}{m}+\frac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle.

Since

\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\chi(x)f(t,x,\xi)dxd\xi=\iint_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\chi(X(t;x,\xi))f^{in}(x,\xi)dxd\xi\,,

one has

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\chi R(t))dt\geq

\displaystyle\inf_{(x,\xi)\in K}\int_{0}^{T}\chi(X(t;x,\xi))dt\iint_{K}f^{in}(x,\xi)dxd\xi

\displaystyle-\frac{\operatorname{Lip}(\chi)}{{\lambda}}\frac{\exp\left(\tfrac{1}{2}T\left(\frac{{\lambda}}{m}+\frac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}\right)\right)-1}{\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\frac{{\lambda}}{m}+\frac{\operatorname{Lip}({\nabla}V)}{{\lambda}}\right)}\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}(f^{in},R^{in})\,.

Conclude by choosing
\chi(x):=\left(1-\frac{\text{dist}(x,\Omega)}{\delta}\right)_{+}
, so that
\operatorname{Lip}(\chi)=\frac{1}{\delta}
.
∎

The main result in this lecture is obviously Theorem 6, which explains how the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
, or its variant
\mathfrak{d}_{\lambda}
are propagated by self-adjoint quantum Hamiltonians
of the form

-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V\,.

However, there are other quantum dynamics for which it may be useful to know how
\mathfrak{d}
is propagated. The example in the following exercise was communicated to us by
E. Carlen.

Quiz 24: Quantum Heat Equation.

(1) Let
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
. Prove that

\mathcal{W}_{2}(e^{t{\Delta}}\mu,e^{t{\Delta}}\nu)\leq\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\,.

(Hint: represent
e^{t{\Delta}}\mu(x)
by means of the Brownian motion, and consider the process
(x+B_{t},y+B_{t})\in\mathbf{R}^{n}\times\mathbf{R}^{n}
, with the
same
Brownian motion
B_{t}
.)

(2) Find another proof of (1) without appealing to the representation of the solution by means of the Brownian motion. (Hint: pick
\rho_{in}\in\mathcal{C}(\mu,\nu)
and propagate
\rho_{in}

by a degenerate diffusion operator
A^{*}A
, i.e. set

{\partial}_{t}\rho_{t}+A^{*}A\rho_{t}=0\,,\qquad\rho_{0}=\rho_{in}\,,

where
A
is a 1st order differential operator such that
A|x-y|^{2}=0
.)

(3) Set
\mathfrak{H}:=L^{2}(\mathbf{R})
and
q\psi(y):=y\psi(y)
while
p\psi(y):=-i{\hbar}\tfrac{d\psi}{dy}(y)
. Consider the Quantum Heat Equation

{\partial}_{t}R=-\tfrac{1}{{\hbar}^{2}}[p,[p,R]]-\tfrac{1}{{\hbar}^{2}}[q,[q,R]]\,,\qquad R(0)=R^{in}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

Prove that the Cauchy problem above is solved by a contraction semigroup on
\mathcal{L}^{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, and that
R(t)\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
for all
t\geq 0
.

(4) Let
R_{1},R_{2}
be the solutions of

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}R_{1}=-\tfrac{1}{{\hbar}^{2}}[p,[p,R_{1}]]-\tfrac{1}{{\hbar}^{2}}[q,[q,R_{1}]]\,,\qquad R_{1}(0)=R_{1}^{in}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\,,

\displaystyle{\partial}_{t}R_{2}=-\tfrac{1}{{\hbar}^{2}}[p,[p,R_{2}]]-\tfrac{1}{{\hbar}^{2}}[q,[q,R_{2}]]\,,\qquad R_{2}(0)=R_{2}^{in}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

Prove that

\mathfrak{d}(R_{1}(t),R_{2}(t))\leq\mathfrak{d}(R_{1}^{in},R_{2}^{in})\,,\qquad t\geq 0\,.

(Hint: consider the operators
[p\otimes I+I\otimes p,q\otimes I-I\otimes q]
and
[q\otimes I+I\otimes q,p\otimes I-I\otimes p]
.)

3.
Lecture III: Triangle Inequalities and Optimal Transport

in the Quantum Setting

We have seen in Lecture II that the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
defined in Lecture I behaves very satisfyingly under propagation by the most fundamental quantum dynamics
(i.e. the unitary group generated by self-adjoint Hamiltonians of the form
-\frac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V
, and also by the quantum heat equation). We have not treated the
interesting case of the Schrödinger equation with a magnetic field, leading to Hamiltonians of the form

\tfrac{1}{m}|-i{\hbar}{\nabla}+A|^{2}+V

where
A
is the vector potential (so that the magnetic field is
B=\text{curl}A
): see
[
12
]
for a comprehensive treatment of this case.

Although Lecture II gives (hopefully) convincing arguments, mostly based on applications, in favor of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
, we have already seen in Lecture I, that

\mathfrak{d}
is not a bona fide metric, in particular because
13
13

13

One might think of changing the definition of
\mathfrak{d}
, for instance by replacing the quantum-to-quantum cost
operator
C_{\hbar}
in Lecture I by
C_{\hbar}-2d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}
. However, if you have understood the essence of the proof of Theorem 6, this is not going to help.
Indeed, this proof is based on the operator inequality

[H\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}}+I_{\mathfrak{H}}\otimes H,C_{\hbar}]\leq LC_{\hbar}

for some positive constant
L
, where
H
is a Hamiltonian of the form
-\tfrac{{\hbar}^{2}}{2m}{\Delta}+V
. Changing
C_{\hbar}
into
C_{\hbar}-{\alpha}I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}
will obviously
not change the commutator on the left-hand side, but changes the right-hand side.

\mathfrak{d}(T,T)>0
for all
T\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
.

In Lecture I, we have postponed the necessary task of exploring the properties of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
, except those reported in Theorem 1, which have proved
already very useful for applications of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
to quantum dynamical problems. For instance, we have not discussed the triangle inequality for
\mathfrak{d}
,
in spite of the fact that we used it in Lecture II in deriving a uniform in
{\hbar}
error estimate for the Lie-Trotter splitting scheme for the von Neumann equation.

In this last lecture we shall return in particular to this question. We shall also discuss further properties of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
by analogy with the Wasserstein
distance
\mathcal{W}_{2}
. Specifically, we shall study the following topics:

\bullet
a Kantorovich-type duality for quantum optimal transport (section
3.3
)

\bullet
two kinds of triangle inequalities for the quantum pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
on
\mathfrak{D}
(presented in section
3.1
), and

\bullet
the structure of optimal couplings for the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
(section
3.5
).

3.1.
Restricted Triangle Inequalities

In this section, we shall prove the triangle inequality for
\mathfrak{d}
in some special cases — hence the terminology of “restricted” triangle inequality used here. Our goal
is the following statement.

Theorem 16.
For all
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\in\mathfrak{D}=\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\cup\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, one has

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})\leq\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})\,,

provided that
\rho_{2}
is a probability density in
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
or one of the
\rho_{j}
s is a rank-
1
density operator on
\mathfrak{H}
.

See Theorems A and 3.1 in
[
40
]
; see also Theorem 3.5 in
[
39
]
.

3.1.1.
Operator Inequalities

As a preparation to the proof of Theorem 16, we prove several inequalities involving the quantum-to-quantum and the classical-to-quantum transport cost operators.
These inequalities are of the same type as triangle inequalities, with some arbitrary parameter
{\alpha}>0
.

First, we discuss inequalities where the intermediate “point” is an operator.

Lemma 17.
For all
{\alpha}>0
, one has

\displaystyle|x-z|^{2}+|\xi-\zeta|^{2}\leq

\displaystyle(1+{\alpha})c_{\hbar}(x,\xi;y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})

\displaystyle+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})c_{\hbar}(z,\zeta;y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})\,,

\displaystyle c_{\hbar}(x,\xi;z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})\leq

\displaystyle(1+{\alpha})c_{\hbar}(x,\xi;y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})

\displaystyle+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})C_{\hbar}(y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})\,,

\displaystyle C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})\leq

\displaystyle(1+{\alpha})C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})C_{\hbar}(y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})\,.

These operator inequalities mean that, for all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{2d}_{x,\xi}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y}\times\mathbf{R}^{2d}_{z,\zeta})
, or all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{2d}_{x,\xi}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{z})
,
or all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{z})
,

\langle\phi|r.h.s.-l.h.s.|\phi\rangle\geq 0\,.

Proof.

Write

\displaystyle C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})=

\displaystyle|x-y+y-z|^{2}-{\hbar}^{2}|{\nabla}_{x}-{\nabla}_{y}+{\nabla}_{y}-{\nabla}_{z}|^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})+C_{\hbar}(y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})

\displaystyle+2(x-y)\cdot(y-z)-2{\hbar}^{2}({\nabla}_{x}-{\nabla}_{y})\cdot({\nabla}_{y}-{\nabla}_{z})\,.

Use the Peter-Paul elementary inequality

2(x-y)\cdot(y-z)\leq{\alpha}|x-y|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}}|y-z|^{2}\,,

and, for operators
A,B
, the analogous inequality

A^{*}B+B^{*}A\leq{\alpha}|A|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}}|B|^{2}

with
A=A^{*}=-i{\hbar}({\partial}_{x_{j}}-{\partial}_{y_{j}})
and
B=B^{*}=-i{\hbar}({\partial}_{y_{j}}-{\partial}_{z_{j}})
for all indices
j=1,\ldots,d
. (Observe that these operators commute, which is inessential here).
The operator inequality comes from expanding

0\leq\left|{\alpha}^{\frac{1}{2}}A-{\alpha}^{-\frac{1}{2}}B\right|^{2}={\alpha}|A|^{2}+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}}|B|^{2}-A^{*}B-B^{*}A\,.

See also Quiz 7 in Lecture I.

Hence

\displaystyle 2(x-y)\cdot(y-z)-2{\hbar}^{2}({\nabla}_{x}-{\nabla}_{y})\cdot({\nabla}_{y}-{\nabla}_{z})

\displaystyle\leq{\alpha}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}}C_{\hbar}(y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})

With the previous equality involving
C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})
, we arrive at the 3rd inequality of Lemma 17.
∎

Quiz 25.
Prove the first two inequalities in Lemma 17.

Next, we discuss inequalities similar to those of Lemma 17, but with classical phase-space point as intermediate term.

Lemma 18.
For all
{\alpha}>0
, one has

\displaystyle c_{\hbar}(x,\xi;z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})\leq

\displaystyle(1+{\alpha})(|x-y|^{2}+|\xi-\eta|^{2})

\displaystyle+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})c_{\hbar}(y,\eta;z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})

\displaystyle C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})\leq

\displaystyle(1+{\alpha})c_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,\eta)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})c_{\hbar}(y,\eta,z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})\,.

These operator inequalities mean that

\langle\phi|r.h.s.-l.h.s.|\phi\rangle\geq 0

for all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{2d}_{x,\xi}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y,\eta}\times\mathbf{R}^{2d}_{z,\zeta})
, or all
\phi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbf{R}^{d}_{x}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{y,\eta}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}_{z})
.

Quiz 26.
Prove Lemma 18 (by the same method as in the proof of Lemma 17).

3.1.2.
The Rank-
1
Case

At this point, we can prove the part of Theorem 16 involving a rank-
1
density operator.

Proof of Theorem 16: sketch for the rank-
1
case.

Assume for example that
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
while
\rho_{3}
is a rank-
1
density operator, and let
Q\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
. Set

T:=Q\otimes\rho_{3}\,,\quad T_{13}=\operatorname{trace}_{2}T\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})\,.

Hence, by the 3rd inequality in Lemma 17,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})^{2}\leq\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}(T_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})T_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 3}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})T^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle\leq(1+{\alpha})\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 3}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})T^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 3}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})T^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=(1+{\alpha})\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}(Q^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})Q^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})\underbrace{\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}((\rho_{2}\otimes\rho_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(y,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})(\rho_{2}\otimes\rho_{3})^{\frac{1}{2}})}_{=\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})^{2}}

\displaystyle.

That the last term on the r.h.s. is equal to
\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})^{2}
follows from the fact that
\mathcal{C}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})=\{\rho_{2}\otimes\rho_{3}\}
since
\rho_{3}
has rank
1

(see Lemma 2 in Lecture I). Minimizing the last r.h.s. in
Q\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
shows that

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})^{2}\leq(1+{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})^{2}+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})^{2}\,.

Minimizing the r.h.s. in
{\alpha}>0
, i.e. setting

{\alpha}:=\frac{\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})}{\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})}\quad\text{ assuming }\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})>0\,,

leads to

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})^{2}\leq\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})^{2}+\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})^{2}+2\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})\,.

Conclude by taking the square root of both sides of this inequality.
∎

Quiz 27.
Complete the missing argument in the proof by justifying the equality

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}(T_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})T_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 3}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})T^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle.

(1) Prove this identity when
C_{\hbar}
is replaced with
(I_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}+\tfrac{1}{n}C_{\hbar})^{-1}C_{\hbar}
.

(2) Using the Fatou lemma for trace-class operators, prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}\left(T_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}}\tfrac{C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})}{I_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}+\frac{1}{n}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})}T_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}\left(T_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})T_{13}^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle,

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 3}}\left(T^{\frac{1}{2}}\tfrac{C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})}{I_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 3}}+\frac{1}{n}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})}T^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 3}}\left(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})T^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)

\displaystyle.

Quiz 28.
Complete the proof of Theorem 16 by treating the missing cases where one of the
\rho_{j}
’s is a rank-
1
density operator.

3.1.3.
The Case of a Classical Intermediate Density

All the cases of the triangle inequality involving a rank-
1
operator are easy, because of Lemma 2. Indeed, the set of couplings of any density (quantum or classical) with
a rank-
1
density operator is a singleton, and therefore the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
is easily computed explicitly in such a case.

In the present section, we shall discuss all the cases of the triangle inequality where the intermediate point is a classical density on phase-space. The triangle inequality in
such cases is much more involved, but fortunately, the proof can be modelled on one of the proofs of the triangle inequality for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
(See the proof of Theorem 7.3 in

[
63
]
.)

The key step is the following lemma, which explains how to
disintegrate
a coupling between a classical probability density on phase-space and a density operator.

Lemma 19.
Let
f
be a probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, let
R\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
and let
Q\in\mathcal{C}(f,R)
. There exists a weakly measurable map

\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\ni(x,\xi)\mapsto Q_{f}(x,\xi)\in\mathcal{L}^{1}(\mathfrak{H})

defined a.e. on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, which satisfies

Q_{f}(x,\xi)=Q_{f}(x,\xi)^{*}\geq 0\,,\quad\operatorname{trace}(Q(x,\xi))=1\,,

and

Q(x,\xi)=f(x,\xi)Q_{f}(x,\xi)\quad\text{ a.e. in }(x,\xi)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\,.

Proof.

First replace
f
with a Borel representative, and consider the set

\mathcal{N}:=f^{-1}(\{0\})\,,

which is Borel measurable. Pick
u\in\mathfrak{H}
such that
\|u\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
, and set

Q_{f}(x,\xi):=\frac{Q(x,\xi)+{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{N}}(x,\xi)|u\rangle\langle u|}{f(x,\xi)+{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{N}}(x,\xi)}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

Obviously

Q(x,\xi)=Q(x,\xi)^{*}\geq 0\,\,\text{ and }\,\,f(x,\xi)\geq 0

and hence

Q_{f}(x,\xi)=Q_{f}(x,\xi)^{*}\geq 0\,,\quad\text{ for a.e. }(x,\xi)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\,.

Moreover

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(x,\xi)+{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{N}}(x,\xi)|u\rangle\langle u|)=f(x,\xi)+{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{N}}(x,\xi)\,,

so that

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q_{f}(x,\xi))=1\,,\quad\text{ for a.e. }(x,\xi)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\,.

Finally

f(x,\xi)Q_{f}(x,\xi)=\frac{f(x,\xi)Q(x,\xi)}{f(x,\xi)+{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{N}}(x,\xi)}=Q(x,\xi)

for a.e.
(x,\xi)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
. Indeed, whenever
f(x,\xi)>0
, one has
{\bf 1}_{\mathcal{N}}(x,\xi)=0
and the claimed equality is obvious. On the other hand, since

Q(x,\xi)=Q(x,\xi)^{*}\geq 0\quad\text{ and }\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q(x,\xi))=f(x,\xi)

for a.e.
(x,\xi)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, it follows that

f(x,\xi)=0\implies Q(x,\xi)=0=f(x,\xi)Q_{f}(x,\xi)\,.

∎

With Lemma 19 at our disposal, the proof of Theorem 16 in the case where the intermediate point is a classical density follows the proof of Theorem 7.3 in
[
63
]
.

Proof of Theorem 16: the case of a classical intermediate density.

Consider for example the case where both
\rho_{1}
and
\rho_{3}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, and assume that
\rho_{2}=f(y,\eta)dyd\eta\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Choose couplings

Q^{1}\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{1},f)
while
Q^{3}\in\mathcal{C}(f,\rho_{3})
. Call
Q^{3}_{f}
the disintegration of
Q^{3}
with respect to
f
as in Lemma 19. Set

T(y,\eta):=Q^{1}(y,\eta)\otimes Q^{3}_{f}(y,\eta)\,.

By construction

T(y,\eta)=T(y,\eta)^{*}\geq 0\,,

and

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{1}(T(y,\eta))=

\displaystyle f(y,\eta)Q^{3}_{f}(y,\eta)=Q^{3}(y,\eta)\,,

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{3}(T(y,\eta))=

\displaystyle Q^{1}(y,\eta)\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q^{3}_{f}(y,\eta))=Q^{1}(y,\eta)\,.

In particular

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}T(y,\eta)dyd\eta=:\mathcal{Q}\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})\,.

By the second inequality in Lemma 17

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})^{2}\leq\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}(\mathcal{Q}^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})\mathcal{Q}^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}(T(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})T(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}})dyd\eta

\displaystyle\leq(1+{\alpha})\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{1}\left(\operatorname{trace}_{3}(T(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,\eta)T(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}})\right)dyd\eta

\displaystyle+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{3}\left(\operatorname{trace}_{1}(T(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,\eta)T(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}})\right)dyd\eta

\displaystyle\leq(1+{\alpha})\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(Q^{1}(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,\eta)Q^{1}(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}})dyd\eta

\displaystyle+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}\left(Q^{3}_{f}(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},y,\eta)Q^{3}_{f}(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)f(y,\eta)dyd\eta

\displaystyle.

Minimizing the last right-hand side in
Q^{1}\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})
and in
Q^{3}\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})
leads to the inequality

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})^{2}\leq(1+{\alpha})\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})^{2}+(1+\tfrac{1}{{\alpha}})\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})^{2}\,,

and we conclude as in the rank-1 case.
∎

Quiz 28.

(1) Complete the missing details in the proof of Theorem 16 in the case where
\rho_{1},\rho_{3}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
and
\rho_{2}=f(y,\eta)dyd\eta
. In particular, prove the identity

\displaystyle\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}(\mathcal{Q}^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})\mathcal{Q}^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}^{\otimes 2}}(T(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}(x,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{x},z,{\hbar}{\nabla}_{z})T(y,\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}})dyd\eta

\displaystyle.

(2) Write the proof of Theorem 16 in the missing cases.

3.2.
Applications of the Restricted Triangle Inequalities

Before going further in our discussion of the triangle inequality for the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
, we shall present two easy applications of the (restricted) triangle inequalities already
established in the previous section.

3.2.1.
Definition of
\mathfrak{d}
on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\times\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})

Our first application is of a quite fundamental nature, since it completes our definition of
\mathfrak{d}
in Lecture I. So far we have defined
\mathfrak{d}(\mu,R)=\mathfrak{d}(R,\mu)
for

\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
only when
\mu=f(x,\xi)dxd\xi
, with
f
a probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
— i.e. only when

\mu
is absolutely continuous with respect to the phase-space Lebesgue measure, by the Radon-Nikodym theorem.

Theorem 20.

For each
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, the map
f\mapsto\mathfrak{d}(f,R)
, defined for all probability density
f
with finite second order moments on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
has a
unique extension to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
satisfying

|\mathfrak{d}(\mu,R)-\mathfrak{d}(\nu,R)|\leq\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,g)\,,\qquad\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\,.

See section 3 in
[
40
]
.

Remark.
This extension of
\mathfrak{d}
obviously satisfies the restricted triangle inequality

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})\leq\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})

for all
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\in\mathfrak{D}
provided that
\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
or if one of the
\rho_{j}
s is a rank-
1
density operator on
\mathfrak{H}=L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

Proof.

For all
f,g
probability densities with finite 2nd order moments on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, one has the triangle inequality

\mathfrak{d}(f,R)\leq\mathfrak{d}(f,g)+\mathfrak{d}(g,R)

by Theorem 16, so that

\mathfrak{d}(f,R)-\mathfrak{d}(g,R)\leq\mathfrak{d}(f,g)=\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,g)\,.

Exchanging
f
and
g
in the inequality above implies that

|\mathfrak{d}(f,R)-\mathfrak{d}(g,R)|\leq\mathcal{W}_{2}(f,g)\,.

Thus the function
f\mapsto\mathfrak{d}(f,R)
is Lipschitz-continuous for the metric
\mathcal{W}_{2}
. It has therefore a unique Lipschitz-continuous extension to
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
by the density
argument recalled in Lemma 21 below.
∎

Lemma 21.

Let
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
and let
\chi_{\epsilon}(x)=\chi(x/{\epsilon})/{\epsilon}^{n}
be an even
C^{\infty}
mollifier with support in
B_{\epsilon}(0)
. Then
f_{\epsilon}:=\chi_{\epsilon}\star\mu
is a
C^{\infty}

probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
and

\mathcal{W}_{2}(f_{\epsilon},\mu)\to 0\quad\text{ as }{\epsilon}\to 0\,.

This is Lemma 3.2 in
[
40
]
.

Proof.

For all
\phi\in C_{0}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
, one has

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\phi(x)\mu(dx)-\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}f_{\epsilon}(x)\phi(x)dx=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}(\phi(x)-\chi_{\epsilon}\star\phi(x))\mu(dx)

since
\chi_{\epsilon}
is even, so that

\left|\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\phi(x)\mu(dx)-\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}f_{\epsilon}(x)\phi(x)dx\right|\leq\|\phi-\phi\star\chi_{\epsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbf{R}^{n})}\to 0\,.

Hence
f_{\epsilon}\to\mu
weakly in
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{R}^{n})
as
{\epsilon}\to 0
.

It remains to establish the tightness property (see the properties of
\mathcal{W}_{2}
recalled in Lecture I). Since
\chi_{\epsilon}
is even,

\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}{\bf 1}_{|x|>R}|x|^{2}\chi_{\epsilon}\star\mu(x)dx=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}\chi_{\epsilon}\star({\bf 1}_{|x|>R}|x|^{2})\mu(dx)\,.

On the other hand, for all
{\epsilon}\in(0,1)

\displaystyle\chi_{\epsilon}\star({\bf 1}_{|x|>R}|x|^{2})\leq

\displaystyle\bf{\displaystyle 1}_{|x|+1\geq R}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|x-{\epsilon}y|^{2}\chi(y)dy

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2{\bf 1}_{|x|+1\geq R}\left(|x|^{2}+{\epsilon}^{2}\underbrace{\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}|y|^{2}\chi(y)dy}_{\leq 1}\right)

Hence

\sup_{0<{\epsilon}<1}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}{\bf 1}_{|x|>R}|x|^{2}\chi_{\epsilon}\star\mu(x)dx\leq 2\int_{\mathbf{R}^{n}}{\bf 1}_{|x|+1>R}(|x|^{2}+1)\mu(dx)\to 0

as
R\to\infty
, by dominated convergence.

Therefore, by Theorem 7.12 of
[
63
]

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\chi_{\epsilon}\star\mu,\mu)\to 0\qquad\text{ as }{\epsilon}\to 0\,.

∎

3.2.2.
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is the Classical Limit of
\mathfrak{d}

Our next application of the restricted triangle inequalities presented in Theorem 16 can be thought of as a confirmation of the geometric picture proposed in Lecture I.

We recall the idea of considering
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
as a limit set, or boundary set, of
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
in
\mathfrak{D}
. The next result completes this picture by showing
that
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is the limiting metric deduced from the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
on
\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H})
.

Theorem 22.

Let
R_{\hbar},S_{\hbar}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
and
\mu,\nu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
. Assume that
\mu,\nu
are the classical limits of
R_{\hbar},S_{\hbar}
respectively, i.e.

\mathfrak{d}(\mu,R_{\hbar})+\mathfrak{d}(\nu,S_{\hbar})\to 0\quad\text{ as }{\hbar}\to 0\,.

Then

\lim_{{\hbar}\to 0}\mathfrak{d}(R_{\hbar},S_{\hbar})=\mathfrak{d}(\mu,\nu)\,.

This is Theorem C (see also Theorem 5.5) in
[
40
]
.

Figure 7.
The Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is the classical limit of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
as
{\hbar}\to 0
.

Proof.

By the restricted triangle inequality

\mathfrak{d}(R_{\hbar},S_{\hbar})\leq\mathfrak{d}(R_{\hbar},\mu)+\mathfrak{d}(\mu,\nu)+\mathfrak{d}(\nu,S_{\hbar})\,,

so that

\varlimsup_{{\hbar}\to 0}\mathfrak{d}(R_{\hbar},S_{\hbar})\leq\mathfrak{d}(\mu,\nu)=\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\,.

On the other hand, by Theorem 1 (2) of Lecture I,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(R_{\hbar},\mu)^{2}\geq\!\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[R_{\hbar}],\mu)^{2}-d{\hbar}\implies\lim_{{\hbar}\to 0}\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[R_{\hbar}],\mu)=0\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(S_{\hbar},\nu)^{2}\geq\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[S_{\hbar}],\nu)^{2}-d{\hbar}\implies\lim_{{\hbar}\to 0}\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[S_{\hbar}],\nu)=0\,.

Hence

\mathfrak{d}(R_{\hbar},S_{\hbar})^{2}\geq\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[R_{\hbar}],\mathcal{H}[S_{\hbar}])^{2}-2d{\hbar}\,,

implies that

\varliminf_{{\hbar}\to 0}\mathfrak{d}(R_{\hbar},S_{\hbar})\geq\lim_{{\hbar}\to 0}\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mathcal{H}[R_{\hbar}],\mathcal{H}[S_{\hbar}])=\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\,.

(This part of the argument does not appeal to the restricted triangle inequality, and had been already established in Theorem 2.3 (2) of
[
37
]
, albeit in a slightly
different form). Summarizing

\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\leq\varliminf_{{\hbar}\to 0}\mathfrak{d}(R_{\hbar},S_{\hbar})\leq\varlimsup_{{\hbar}\to 0}\mathfrak{d}(R_{\hbar},S_{\hbar})\leq\mathcal{W}_{2}(\mu,\nu)\,.

∎

3.3.
Quantum Kantorovich Duality

In the classical setting, Kantorovich duality has several important consequences. The Knott-Smith and Brenier theorems, giving invaluable information on the structure
of optimal couplings between two (Borel) probability measures on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
are among the most important applications of duality — at least in the case of the Wasserstein
distance
\mathcal{W}_{2}
.

It is therefore natural to seek extensions of Kantorovich duality to the quantum setting. However, the examples studied at the end of Lecture I (in the section showing that
“quantum optimal transport is cheaper”) suggest that the structure of quantum optimal couplings might differ significantly from that of classical optimal couplings.

First we consider the case of
\mathfrak{d}(f,R)
, where
f
is a probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
with finite 2nd order moments and
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
.

Define the set
\mathfrak{k}
of test Kantorovich potentials, as follows:

\mathfrak{k}:=\{(a,B)\,:\,a\in C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\text{ and }B=B^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ s.t. }a(x,\xi)I_{\mathfrak{H}}+B\leq c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)\}\,.

The operator inequality in the definition of
\mathfrak{k}
means that

a(x,\xi)\|\phi\|^{2}_{\mathfrak{H}}+\langle\phi|B|\phi\rangle\leq\langle\phi|c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)|\phi\rangle\,,\qquad x,\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\,,

for all
\phi\in H^{1}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d};|y|^{2}dy)
.

Theorem 23.
Let
f
be a probability density on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
with finite second order moments and let
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
. Then

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(f,R)^{2}=

\displaystyle\sup_{(a,B)\in\mathfrak{k}}\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}a(x,\xi)f(x,\xi)dxd\xi+\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(BR)\right)\,.

The proof of Theorem 23 is somewhat involved technically, and we shall not repeat it in these notes. We refer instead the interested reader to
Theorem 4.1 and section 4 of
[
40
]
.

However, it is a good idea to keep in mind the core argument in that proof, which is based on convex duality, exactly as in the classical setting.

Set
\mathcal{E}:=C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{2d};\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H}))
with

\|T\|_{\mathcal{E}}:=\sup_{x,\xi\in\mathbf{R}^{d}}\|T(x,\xi)\|_{\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})}\,.

Define

G(T):=\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&0&&\text{ if }T(x,\xi)=T(x,\xi)^{*}\geq-c_{\hbar}(x,\xi),\\
&+\infty&&\text{ otherwise, }\end{aligned}\right.

and

H(T):=\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\tilde{a}f(x,\xi)dxd\xi+\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(\tilde{B}R)&&\text{ if }\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&T(x,\xi)\!=\!T(x,\xi)^{*}\\
&=\tilde{a}(x,\xi)I_{\mathfrak{H}}+\tilde{B},\end{aligned}\right.\\
\\
&+\infty&&\text{ otherwise. }\end{aligned}\right.

Theorem 23 follows from the Fenchel-Rockafellar duality formula

\inf_{T\in\mathcal{E}}\left(G(T)+H(T)\right)=\max_{\Lambda\in\mathcal{E}^{\prime}}\left(-G^{*}(-\Lambda)-H^{*}(\Lambda)\right)\,.

The role of the functional
G
in this formulation is obviously to penalize the inequality constraint in the definition of
\mathfrak{k}
. (Sometimes, in convex
analysis, the functional equal to
0
if a constraint is satisfied, and to
+\infty
otherwise is referred to as the “indicator function” of that constraint.
This is not to be confused with the classical notion of indicator function of a set, equal to one on the set, and to zero outside.)

Next we consider the case of
\mathfrak{d}(R,S)
where
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
. Here again, there is an analogue of the classical Kantorovich duality theorem.

Define the set
\mathfrak{K}
of test Kantorovich potentials as follows

\mathfrak{K}:=\{(A,B)\,:\,A=A^{*}\text{ and }B=B^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\text{ s.t. }A\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}}+I_{\mathfrak{H}}\otimes B\leq C_{\hbar}\}\,.

The operator inequality means that for all
\Phi\equiv\Phi(x,y)\in\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}
such that

({\nabla}_{x}-{\nabla}_{y})\Phi\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})\quad\text{ and }\quad\Phi\in L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d};|x-y|^{2}dxdy)\,,

it holds

\langle\Phi|A\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}}+I_{\mathfrak{H}}\otimes B|\Phi\rangle\leq\langle\Phi|C_{\hbar}|\Phi\rangle\,.

Theorem 24.
For all
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, one has

\mathfrak{d}(R,S)^{2}=\sup_{(A,B)\in\mathfrak{K}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(AR+BS)\,.

Here again, the proof of Theorem 24 is too technically involved to be of immediate interest for these lecture notes. We refer the interested reader to

[
20
]
for a detailed exposition of duality in the quantum-to-quantum setting, together with various applications thereof.

Although we have chosen to avoid reproducing the proofs of Theorems 23 and 24, we believe that both results are of key importance in the study
of the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
, and in our approach of quantum optimal transport. As a matter of fact, the end of Lecture III will be focussed on two
applications of Theorem 23, which we believe are of some importance.

3.4.
Generalized Triangle Inequalities

In this section, we return to the question of the triangle inequality for
\mathfrak{d}
for
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\in\mathfrak{D}
, and consider the case where the intermediate
point
\rho_{2}
is a density operator and none of the points
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}
are rank-
1
density operators.

In that case, we do not know of any analogue of Lemma 19, and at the time of this writing we do not know how to “glue”
Q_{12}\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})

and
Q_{23}\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})
along
\rho_{2}
as in Lemma 7.6 in
[
63
]
, to mimic the proof of Theorem 7.3 in
[
63
]
in the
classical setting.

At the beginning of this lecture, we have proved “restricted” triangle inequalities for
\mathfrak{d}
, i.e. the triangle inequality under addditional assumptions on

\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}
— specifically if one of the points
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}
is a rank-
1
density operator, or if the intermediate point
\rho_{2}
is
an element of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
.

In the sequel, we shall prove a “generalized triangle inequality”, which holds for all
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\in\mathfrak{D}
, but includes a correction term of order

\sqrt{{\hbar}}
in the right-hand side.

Theorem 25.

For all
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\in\mathfrak{D}
, one has

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})<\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})+\sqrt{d{\hbar}}\,.

In particular

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})<\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})+\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{2})\,.

Remark.
One should compare this result with the De Palma-Trevisan generalized triangle inequality for their distance — which is reminiscent
of our generalization of the Wasserstein distance
\mathcal{W}_{2}
to
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
in
[
37
]
(the restriction of
\mathfrak{d}
to
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\times\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
).
They arrive at the inequality

d_{dPT}(R,T)\leq d_{dPT}(R,S)+d_{dPT}(R,T)+d_{dPT}(S,S)

for all
R,S,T\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
for
d_{dPT}
defined in formula (38) of Definition 8, on p. 3208 in
[
26
]
: see formula (51) in Theorem 2 of

[
26
]
on p. 3210. Notice however that
d_{dPT}
differs from the restriction of
\mathfrak{d}
to
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\times\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
— in particular
the definition of couplings in
[
26
]
uses the notion of quantum channel and is very different from the definition used in these lectures,
which comes from
[
37
]
.

Quiz 29.
Can one slightly modify the definition of
\mathfrak{d}
so as to obtain a functional
\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}
defined on
\mathfrak{D}\times\mathfrak{D}
that is symmetric and
satisfies the (genuine) triangle inequality? Same question for the DePalma-Trevisan functional
d_{dPT}
. (Of course, the functional
\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}
so
obtained satisfies
\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}>\mathfrak{d}>0
and therefore is not a bona fide metric either, since
\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}(\rho,\rho)>0
for each
\rho\in\mathfrak{D}
. The functional

\tilde{\mathfrak{d}}
obtained in this exercise is therefore of limited interest.)

The proof of Theorem 25 is very different from the proof of the triangle inequality for the original Wasserstein metric
\mathcal{W}_{2}
— although it uses at
some point the restricted triangle inequality in Theorem 16, whose proof is modelled on the proof of Theorem 7.3 in
[
63
]
.

A key step in the proof of Theorem 25 is the following lemma, which can be seen as a consequence of duality for the classical-to-quantum pseudometric
presented in Theorem 23.

Lemma 26.
For each
R,S\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, one has

\mathfrak{d}(R,S)^{2}\geq\mathfrak{d}(R,\mathcal{H}[S])^{2}-d{\hbar}\,.

Taking this lemma for granted, we give a quick proof of Theorem 25.

Proof of Theorem 25.
Using
\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}]
as intermediate point, the restricted triangle inequality implies that

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})\leq\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}])+\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}],\rho_{3})\,.

Then, Lemma 26 implies that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}])\leq\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})^{2}+d{\hbar}}<\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{d{\hbar}}\,,

\displaystyle\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}],\rho_{3})\leq\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})^{2}+d{\hbar}}<\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})+\tfrac{1}{2}\sqrt{d{\hbar}}\,.

The second inequalities above result from the following elementary observation

X>Y>0\implies\sqrt{X^{2}+Y^{2}}\leq X+\tfrac{1}{2}Y\,,

whose proof is left to the reader as an (easy) exercise.

With the restricted triangle inequality above (Theorem 16), this implies the first generalized triangle inequality in Theorem 25.

To get the second inequality, observe that

\rho_{2}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\implies\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{2})\geq\sqrt{2d{\hbar}}\,.

\Box

Remark.
In fact, we have proved the slightly more precise inequality

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})\leq\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})^{2}+d{\hbar}}+\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})^{2}+d{\hbar}}\,.

Proof of Lemma 26.

For all
a\in C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
and all
B=B^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
satisfying

a(x,\xi)I_{\mathfrak{H}}+B\leq c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)\,,

one applies the Toeplitz map to both sides of the inequality above in the variables
x,\xi
, to find

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}[a]\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}}+(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}I_{\mathfrak{H}}\otimes B\leq

\displaystyle(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\int|q,p\rangle\langle q,p|c_{\hbar}(q,p)dqdp

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\left(C_{\hbar}+d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}\right)

(see the basic properties of the Toeplitz map and Quiz 8 in Lecture I). Thus, for all
T\in\mathcal{C}(R,S)
, one has

\displaystyle(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\left(\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}T^{\frac{1}{2}})+d{\hbar}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}\left(T^{\frac{1}{2}}(\mathcal{T}[a]\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}}+(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}I_{\mathfrak{H}}\otimes B\right)T^{\frac{1}{2}})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}\left(T(\mathcal{T}[a]\otimes I_{\mathfrak{H}}+(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}I_{\mathfrak{H}}\otimes B\right))

\displaystyle=\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(R\mathcal{T}[a])+(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(SB)

\displaystyle.

Transforming
\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(R\mathcal{T}[a])
into an integral involving the functions
a
and
\mathcal{H}[R]
, i.e. (see Lecture I, formula (4) on Husimi transforms)

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(R\mathcal{T}[a])=(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\mathcal{H}[R](q,p)a(q,p)dqdp\,,

we arrive at the formula

\displaystyle(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\left(\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}T^{\frac{1}{2}})+d{\hbar}\right)

\displaystyle\geq(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\left(\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\mathcal{H}[R](q,p)a(q,p)dqdp+\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}(SB)\right)\,.

Maximizing the right-hand side above in
a\in C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
B=B^{*}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
s.t.

a(x,\xi)I_{\mathfrak{H}}+B\leq c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)\,,

and applying the duality formula in Theorem 16 shows that

(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\left(\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}T^{\frac{1}{2}})+d{\hbar}\right)\geq(2\pi{\hbar})^{d}\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{H}[R],S)^{2}

i.e.

\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}}(T^{\frac{1}{2}}C_{\hbar}T^{\frac{1}{2}})\geq\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{H}[R],S)^{2}-d\,.

Minimizing the left-hand side of the inequality above in
T\in\mathcal{C}(R,S)
leads to the desired inequality.
∎

Quiz 30.
Use Lemma 26 to recover the following result (already known as statement (2) in Theorem 1 of Lecture I)

\mathfrak{d}(R,S)^{2}\geq\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{H}[R],\mathcal{H}[S])^{2}-2d{\hbar}\,.

Remarks.

(1) If you include the proof of the duality formula in Theorem 16, this is the longest and most difficult proof of the inequality above… On the other hand,
Lemma 26 is a (much) stronger statement — it is the key to the generalized triangle inequality. That its proof is more involved than the proof of the
inequality (2) in Theorem 1 is only natural.

(2) Summarizing, in order to prove the triangle inequality for
\mathfrak{d}
when the intermediate point is not a classical density and none of the density operators
involved are rank-
1
projections, you

(i) first use the exact (restricted) triangle inequality from Theorem 16

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})\leq\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}])+\mathfrak{d}(\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}],\rho_{3})\,,

(ii) and then pay the price for replacing
\rho_{2}
with its Husimi function

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})\leq\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})^{2}+d{\hbar}}+\sqrt{\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})^{2}+d{\hbar}}

which is the result in Lemma 26, based on the Kantorovich duality for the the classical-to-quantum distance (Theorem 23). The end of the proof is
Kindergarten analysis.

The reason for the detour through
\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}]
instead of
\rho_{2}
is due to the fact that question (5) in the following quiz is answered in the
negative
14
14

14

While preparing the final version of these lecture notes, I showed the problem to Prof. Denis Serre, who found a counterexample.
At first sight, Serre’s counterexample does not seem to suggest that the triangle inequality itself (without the extra
\sqrt{d\hbar}
term on the
right-hand side) should be wrong. However, it shows that the procedure of “glueing” classical couplings described in Lemma 7.6 of
[
63
]
,
which is key to proving the triangle inequality for
\mathcal{W}_{p}
with
1<p<\infty
, does not have an analogue for general quantum couplings. This
is a rather fundamental difference between classical and quantum optimal transport.
.

Before working on this exercise, it is a good idea to review the proofs of Theorem 7.3 (the triangle inequality for
\mathcal{W}_{p}
) and of Lemma 7.6 (i.e. the
disintegration and the glueing of couplings) in
[
63
]
.

Quiz 31.

Pick
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
, all of them or rank
\geq 2
— otherwise, there is nothing to prove. Pick
R_{12}
and
R_{23}
to be optimal
couplings of
\rho_{1},\rho_{2}
and
\rho_{2},\rho_{3}
(recall briefly why such couplings exist).

(1) Assume there exists
T\in\mathcal{D}(\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H}\otimes\mathfrak{H})
such that

\operatorname{trace}_{1}(T)=R_{23}\quad\text{ and }\quad\operatorname{trace}_{3}(T)=R_{12}\,.

Prove that

\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})\leq\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{1},\rho_{2})+\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{3})\,.

(Hint: observe that
\operatorname{trace}_{2}(T)\in\mathcal{C}(\rho_{1},\rho_{3})
.)

Therefore, proving the triangle inequality boils down to proving the existence of such a
T
. The classical analogue of this is precisely the content of
Lemma 7.6 in Villani’s book
[
63
]
.

Let us consider this problem in finite dimension:
\mathfrak{H}=\mathbf{C}^{2}
— notice that
2
is the first interesting dimension, because if one of the densities
\rho_{j}

for
j=1,2,3
has rank
1
, the triangle inequality is already known (see Theorem 16 on the restricted triangle inequality).

(2) Let
R,R^{\prime}\in M_{2}(\mathbf{C})
. Find a necessary and sufficient condition on
R,R^{\prime}
such that there exists
A,B,C\in M_{2}(\mathbf{C})
for which the block-wise matrix

T:=\left(\begin{matrix}A&B\\
B^{*}&C\end{matrix}\right)\,,\qquad A=A^{*}\,,\,\,C=C^{*}\,,

satisfies

\tau^{\prime}(T):=A+C=R\quad\text{ and }\quad\tau(T):=\left(\begin{matrix}\operatorname{trace}(A)&\operatorname{trace}(B)\\
\operatorname{trace}(B^{*})&\operatorname{trace}(C)\end{matrix}\right)=R^{\prime}\,.

(3) Assume now that
R,R^{\prime}\in M_{2}(M_{2}(\mathbf{C}))
. Find a necessary and sufficient condition on
R,R^{\prime}
such that there exists
A,B,C\in M_{2}(M_{2}(\mathbf{C}))
for
which the block-wise matrix

T:=\left(\begin{matrix}A&B\\
B^{*}&C\end{matrix}\right)\,,\qquad A=A^{*}\,,\,\,C=C^{*}

satisfies

\tau^{\prime}(T)=A+C=R\quad\text{ and }\quad\left(\begin{matrix}\operatorname{trace}_{M_{2}(\mathbf{C})}(A)&\operatorname{trace}_{M_{2}(\mathbf{C})}(B)\\
\operatorname{trace}_{M_{2}(\mathbf{C})}(B^{*})&\operatorname{trace}_{M_{2}(\mathbf{C})}(C)\end{matrix}\right)=R^{\prime}.

The notation needs being explained. An element of
B\in M_{2}(M_{2}(\mathbf{C}))
is of the form

B=\left(\begin{matrix}B_{11}&B_{12}\\
B_{21}&B_{22}\end{matrix}\right)\quad\text{ with }B_{kl}\in M_{2}(\mathbf{C})\,.

Then

B^{*}:=\left(\begin{matrix}\overline{B^{T}_{11}}&\overline{B^{T}_{21}}\\
\overline{B_{12}^{T}}&\overline{B_{22}^{T}}\end{matrix}\right)

while

\operatorname{trace}_{M_{2}(\mathbf{C})}(B):=B_{11}+B_{22}\,.

(4) Explain how (3) is related to the problem of finding
T
as in (1), in the case where
\rho_{1},\rho_{2},\rho_{3}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{C}^{2})
.

Now, there’s the rub…

(5) Assuming that
R,R^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbf{C}^{2})
, does (the) block-wise matrix (matrices)
T
obtained in (3) satisfy
T=T^{*}\geq 0
?

Remark.
A final observation on the generalized triangle inequality is in order. The presence of the additional term
\sqrt{d{\hbar}}

in the right-hand side of the generalized triangle inequality may be related to the fact that some points in
\mathfrak{D}
have positive thickness,
in the sense that
\mathfrak{d}(R,R)\geq\sqrt{2d{\hbar}}
for all
R\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
. However, the idea of relating this additional term to the “thickness”
of the intermediate point
\rho_{2}
in the triangle inequality could be misleading. In the first place, the genuine triangle inequality holds
if
\rho_{2}
is a rank-
1
density operator, although
\mathfrak{d}(\rho_{2},\rho_{2})\geq\sqrt{2d{\hbar}}
in that case.

Besides, the extra term
\sqrt{d{\hbar}}
on the right-hand side of the generalized triangle inequality seems more related to the method of
proof of Theorem 25 (viz. the idea of replacing the intermediate point
\rho_{2}
with its Husimi transform
\mathcal{H}[\rho_{2}]
) than to some
intrinsic feature of
\mathfrak{d}
.

Ultimately, it could be that question (5) in Quiz 31 is answered in the negative, and yet the genuine triangle inequality holds on
\mathfrak{D}

because there always exists an optimal coupling with a special structure for each pair of finite energy density operators on
\mathfrak{H}
, and
this special structure acts in favor of the genuine triangle inequality.

This suggests investigating the structure of optimal couplings for the “pseudometric”
\mathfrak{d}
— however, the example discussed in
Proposition 5 suggests that this is not an easy task.

3.5.
Towards Quantum Optimal Transport

Until now, we have not said much about quantum optimal transport
per se
, although it is the topic of this school.

We shall conclude these lectures with some (partial) remarks in that direction. The material in this section mostly comes from
[
40
]
.

We recall that the proofs of the Brenier, or of the Knott-Smith theorems use some form of Kantorovich duality to obtain some additional information
on the structure of optimal couplings. That an optimal coupling for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
is supported in the graph of the subdifferential of some appropriate
function comes from the specific structure of the inequality constraint in the Kantorovich dual formulation of the
\mathcal{W}_{2}
metric, and from the
specific structure of the quadratic transport cost.

It is therefore natural to study the quantum analogue of Kantorovich duality in order to obtain some information on the structure of optimal couplings
for
\mathfrak{d}
. In the sequel, we shall follow this approach with the classical-to-quantum duality formula in Theorem 23.

Our first task is to give a systematic procedure for constructing elements of the class
\mathfrak{k}
of test Kantorovich potentials defined before the statement
of Theorem 23.

Set
z:=(x,\xi)\in\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
and
Z:=(y,-i{\hbar}{\nabla}_{y})
, with the notation

z\cdot Z:=x\cdot y-i{\hbar}\xi\cdot{\nabla}_{y}\,.

Thus
c_{\hbar}(x,\xi)=|Z|^{2}+|z|^{2}I_{\mathfrak{H}}-2z\cdot Z\geq d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}}
and by Weyl’s theorem (see Corollary 2 of Theorem XIII.14 in
[
55
]
)

\tilde{B}\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})\implies c_{\hbar}(z)^{-1}\tilde{B}\in\mathcal{K}(\mathfrak{H})\implies\text{ess-spec}(c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B})=\varnothing\,.

Assume that
\tilde{B}=\tilde{B}^{*}
is such that
c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B}
has nondegenerate ground state (i.e. with geometric multiplicity
1
) for each
z\in\mathbf{R}^{2d}
.
(For instance, choose for
\tilde{B}
to be a bounded multiplication operator, and apply Theorem XIII.47 in
[
55
]
).

Define next

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(z):=\min\text{spec}(c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B})=

\displaystyle\inf_{\|\phi\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1}\langle\phi|c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B}|\phi\rangle

\displaystyle\implies c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B}\geq\tilde{a}(z)I_{\mathfrak{H}}\,.

Besides,
z\mapsto\tilde{a}(z)
is continuous (even real-analytic) by the Kato-Rellich theorem (Theorem XII.8 of
[
55
]
), and

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(z)

\displaystyle\leq\langle z|c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B}|z\rangle=d{\hbar}-\langle z|\tilde{B}|z\rangle\leq d{\hbar}+\|\tilde{B}\|\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{a}(z)

\displaystyle\geq d{\hbar}+\inf_{\|\phi\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1}\langle\phi|-\tilde{B}|\phi\rangle\geq d{\hbar}-\|\tilde{B}\|\,.

Hence
\tilde{a}\in C_{b}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
, and we have obtained in this way

(\tilde{a},\tilde{B})\in\mathfrak{k}\,.

The Kato-Rellich theorem recalled above also implies the existence of a continuous (even real-analytic) map

\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}\ni z\mapsto\psi_{z}\in\mathfrak{H}\quad\text{ s.t. }\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&(c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B})\psi_{z}=\tilde{a}(z)\psi_{z}\,,\\
&\text{and }\|\psi_{z}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1,\,\,\,z\in\mathbf{R}^{2d}\,.\end{aligned}\right.

With this, we can define a notion of quantum optimal transport from
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
to
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
.

Theorem 27.

Let
\tilde{B}=\tilde{B}^{*}
be such that
c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B}
has nondegenerate ground state, set
\tilde{a}(z):=\min\text{spec}(c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B})
, and let
z\mapsto\psi_{z}

be a continuous map from
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
to
\mathfrak{H}
such that
\|\psi_{z}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
and
\psi_{z}\in\operatorname{Ker}(c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B}-\tilde{a}(z)I_{\mathfrak{H}})
.

Then, for each probability density
f
with finite 2nd order moments, the map
z\mapsto f(z)|\psi_{z}\rangle\langle\psi_{z}|
is an optimal coupling for
the pseudometric
\mathfrak{d}
between
f
and the operator

\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{B}}[f]:=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}f(z)|\psi_{z}\rangle\langle\psi_{z}|dz\in\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})\,.

Example.
Take for example
\tilde{B}=0
; then, one easily checks that

\tilde{a}(z)=d{\hbar}\,,\quad\operatorname{Ker}(c_{\hbar}(z)-d{\hbar}I_{\mathfrak{H}})=\mathbf{C}|z\rangle\,,

where
|z\rangle
is the Schrödinger coherent state centered at
z:=q+ip
, so that

\mathcal{T}^{0}[f]=\mathcal{T}[f]

is the Toeplitz operator of symbol
f
.

We already knew from Theorem 1 (1) in Lecture I that

\mathfrak{d}(f,\mathcal{T}[f])=\sqrt{d{\hbar}}=\inf_{\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{2d})\times\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})}\mathfrak{d}\,.

Proof of Theorem 27.

Set
Q(z):=f(z)|\psi_{z}\rangle\langle\psi_{z}|
, so that
Q(z)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\sqrt{f(z)}|\psi_{z}\rangle\langle\psi_{z}|
, and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}\left(Q(z)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(z)Q(z)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)dz=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\langle\psi_{z}|c_{\hbar}(z)|\psi_{z}\rangle f(z)dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}(\tilde{a}(z)\langle\psi_{z}|\psi_{z}\rangle+\langle\psi_{z}|B|\psi_{z}\rangle)f(z)dz

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\tilde{a}(z)f(z)dz+\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}\left(\tilde{B}\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{B}}[f]\right)

\displaystyle.

Since
(\tilde{a},\tilde{B})\in\mathfrak{k}
and
Q\in\mathcal{C}(f,\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{B}}[f])
, this implies that
