\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}\left(Q(z)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(z)Q(z)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)dz

\displaystyle=\min_{T\in\mathcal{C}(f,\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{B}}[f])}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}\left(T(z)^{\frac{1}{2}}c_{\hbar}(z)T(z)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)dz

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{d}\left(f,\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{B}}[f]\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\,.

This also implies that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}\tilde{a}(z)f(z)dz+\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}\left(\tilde{B}\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{B}}[f]\right)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(a,B)\in\mathfrak{k}}\int_{\mathbf{R}^{2d}}a(z)f(z)dz+\operatorname{trace}_{\mathfrak{H}}\left(B\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{B}}[f]\right)

\displaystyle.

Therefore, in this case, the sup is attained in
\mathfrak{k}
(this is not true in general).

\Box

Remarks.

(1) Thus the optimal transport map for
\mathfrak{d}
between
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d})
and
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
can be thought of as a
deformation

of the Toeplitz quantization, at least when
\tilde{B}
is such that
c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B}
has a ground state of geometric multiplicity
1
.

(2) Notice that the starting point in Theorem 27 is the pair
(\tilde{a},\tilde{B})\in\mathfrak{k}
, and not the pair consisting of
f
(the probability density) and
the density operator which are the arguments of
\mathfrak{d}
. This approach is vaguely reminiscent of the notion of geodesic in Riemannian geometry:
the original definition of a geodesic curve on a Riemannian manifold is that of the shortest path between two points on the manifold. The calculus
of variations shows that geodesics define local solutions of an ODE system set on the tangent, or cotangent bundle of the manifold. Conversely,
solutions of this ODE system define
local
geodesic curves, which may differ from prescribing arbitrary end points and finding a shortest
path between these points. There is a very loose analogy between the local theory of geodesics through the ODE system, and the definition of
optimal couplings starting from elements of
\mathfrak{k}
as explained in Theorem 27. In other words, the transport map
\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{B}}
is independent
of the choice of the endpoints — or more precisely the endpoints
f
and
\mathcal{T}^{\tilde{B}}[f]
follow from the optimal transport map, instead of
the other way around.

This raises the following question: in Brenier’s theorem, the (classical) optimal transport map is the gradient of a convex function. Is there some analogous
property in the quantum setting?

Operator Legendre Duality and Quantum Optimal Transport

If
(\tilde{a},\tilde{B})\in\mathfrak{k}
, one has

\underbrace{|Z|^{2}+|z|^{2}I_{\mathfrak{H}}-2z\cdot Z}_{=c_{\hbar}(z)}\geq\tilde{a}(z)I_{\mathfrak{H}}+\tilde{B}\iff a(z)+B\geq z\cdot Z\,,

with

a(z):=\tfrac{1}{2}(|z|^{2}-\tilde{a}(z))\,,\qquad B=\tfrac{1}{2}(|Z|^{2}-\tilde{B})\,.

Besides, one has

\operatorname{Dom}(c_{\hbar}(z))\!=\!\operatorname{Dom}(|Z|^{2})\!=H^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d})\cap L^{2}(\mathbf{R}^{d},|y|^{4}dy)\!=:\!D\,.

After these preliminaries, we define a notion of Legendre transform of an (unbounded) operator on
\mathfrak{H}
.

Definition.
Let
B
satisfy
|Z|^{2}-2B\in\mathcal{L}(\mathfrak{H})
. The Legendre dual of
B
is the convex function (upper envelope of affine functions)

B^{L}(z):=\sup_{\phi\in D,\,\|\phi\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1}\left(z\cdot\langle\phi|Z|\phi\rangle-\langle\phi|B|\phi\rangle\right)\,.

Indeed, we recall that, if
T
is an operator on
\mathfrak{H}
, one can think of
\psi\mapsto\langle\psi|T|\psi\rangle
as the noncommutative analogue of
the evaluation at
x\in\mathbf{R}^{n}
of a real-valued function
f
defined on
\mathbf{R}^{n}
. Therefore, the definition above is analogous to the usual
definition of the Legendre(-Fenchel) transform

\phi^{*}(\xi)=\sup_{x\in E}(\langle\xi,x\rangle_{E^{\prime},E}-\phi(x))\,,\qquad\xi\in E^{\prime}\,,

for all
\phi:\,E\to(-\infty,+\infty]
where
E
is a normed linear space on
\mathbf{R}
, and
\phi
is not identically equal to
+\infty
, and
E^{\prime}
is
the topological dual of
E
, i.e. the space of linear functionals on
E
that are continuous for the norm topology.

Theorem 28.

Let
\tilde{B}=\tilde{B}^{*}
be such that
c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B}
has nondegenerate ground state, set
\tilde{a}(z):=\min\text{spec}(c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B})
, and let
z\mapsto\psi_{z}

be a continuous map from
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
to
\mathfrak{H}
such that
\|\psi_{z}\|_{\mathfrak{H}}=1
and
\psi_{z}\in\operatorname{Ker}(c_{\hbar}(z)-\tilde{B}-\tilde{a}(z)I_{\mathfrak{H}})
.

(1) Setting

a(z):=\tfrac{1}{2}(|z|^{2}-\tilde{a}(z))\,,\qquad B:=\tfrac{1}{2}(|Z|^{2}-\tilde{B})\,,

one has

a=B^{L}\,.

(2) Besides

{\nabla}a(z)=z-{\nabla}\tilde{a}(z)=\langle\psi_{z}|Z|\psi_{z}\rangle\,.

Proof.
Statement (1) follows from the definition and the variational formula for the ground state. As for (2), differentiate in
z
the identity

B\psi_{z}-z\cdot Z\psi_{z}+a(z)\psi_{z}=0\,,

and take the inner product with
\psi_{z}
to get

\underbrace{\langle\psi_{z}|B-z\cdot Z+a(z)|\dot{\psi}_{z}\rangle}_{=0\text{ since }B=B^{*},\,Z=Z^{*},\,a(z)\in\mathbf{R}}+\langle\psi_{z}|-Z+{\nabla}a(z)|\psi_{z}\rangle=0\,.

\Box

Remarks.

(1) In the Knott-Smith theorem recalled in Lecture I, optimal couplings for
\mathcal{W}_{2}
are supported in the graph of the subdifferential of a l.s.c. convex function,
while, in the Brenier theorem, the optimal transport map is the gradient of a convex function. In both results, the function is obtained from an optimal Kantorovich
potential by the same transformation as
\tilde{a}\mapsto a
. Theorem 28 (2) is a partial analogue of this crucial piece of information, except that, in the quantum
setting, density operators are not “functions of
Z
”.

(2) In classical optimal transport, there exist an optimal pair
(a,b)
of Kantorovich potentials; they are l.s.c. proper convex functions and are Legendre duals of
each other, so that
\nabla a\circ\nabla b=\text{Id}
; besides
a\in L^{1}_{\mu}
and
b\in L^{1}_{\nu}
. If one tries to proceed by analogy, in the present case, one
should define some notion of “quantum gradient” of the operator
B
.

One idea to do so is to use the phase space symplectic structure. For a smooth function
{\alpha}\equiv{\alpha}(x,\xi)
on
\mathbf{R}^{d}\times\mathbf{R}^{d}
, one has

{\partial}_{x_{j}}\alpha=\{\xi_{j},\alpha\}\,,\quad{\partial}_{\xi_{j}}\alpha=-\{x_{j},\alpha\}\,,\qquad j=1,\ldots,d\,.

This suggests to define “quantum derivatives” as follows,

{\partial}^{Q}_{y_{j}}B:=\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}[-i{\hbar}{\partial}_{y_{j}},B]\,,\quad{\partial}^{Q}_{\eta_{j}}B:=-\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}[y_{j},B],,\qquad j=1,\ldots,d\,,

by using the correspondence principle and the analogy between commutator and Poisson bracket

\tfrac{i}{{\hbar}}[\cdot,\cdot]\to\{\cdot,\cdot\}

recalled in Lecture II. Since

B\psi_{z}=z\cdot Z\psi_{z}+a(z)\psi_{z}\,,\quad B=B^{*}\,,\quad Z=Z^{*}\text{ and }a(z)\in\mathbf{R}\,,

one easily checks that

\left\{\begin{aligned} {}&x_{j}=\langle\psi_{z}|{\partial}^{Q}_{y_{j}}B|\psi_{z}\rangle\\
&\xi_{j}=\langle\psi_{z}|{\partial}^{Q}_{\eta_{j}}B|\psi_{z}\rangle\end{aligned}\right.

This formula can be viewed as the inverse transform of Theorem 28 (2).

(3) Analogous ideas on a definition of an optimal transport “map” between elements of
\mathcal{D}_{2}(\mathfrak{H})
can be found in
[
20
]
.
Partial results analogous to Theorem 28 have been obtained there, but much remains to be done.

Following the proof of Theorems 27–28 suggests viewing the operator

-\tfrac{1}{2}(|x|^{2}-{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}_{x}-A)

as the “smallest eigenvalue” of the operator

\tfrac{1}{2}(|y|^{2}-{\hbar}^{2}{\Delta}_{y}-B)-x\cdot y+{\hbar}^{2}{\nabla}_{x}\cdot{\nabla}_{y}\,,

viewed as a “matrix” whose entries are operators in the
x
-variables. However, inequalities between operators do not define a total order relation,
so that even the notion of ground state in this setting does not seem to make much sense.

New ideas on this problem are obviously needed.
